# TARROCK: THE FOOL'S READING

*Come in, then, and mind the step. There's a chair. There's always a chair — I keep one by
for company, though company is a thing I've had rather less of than you'd expect.*

*You'll want to know what this is. It's a reading. Not the sort where a woman in a striped
tent promises you a tall stranger and relieves you of your coin; the other sort. The sort
where somebody sits down badly frightened and asks the one question they've been carrying
about for years without once saying it aloud, and the cards come out honest, and what's in
them is in them.*

*The question was mine. I'll own that at the start, so nobody can accuse me later of
dealing from the bottom. It was a short question. I was three hundred years working up to
the asking of it.*

*And here's the other thing I'll say plainly, while there's still time to put your coat
back on: it ends. The story ends. I'll not walk you the whole long way and then produce
some clever business at the finish to pretend otherwise — that isn't a reading, that's a
swindle, and I was never a swindler, only a bit of a coward. It ends. And the ending is
not a punishment for having stayed to hear it. The ending is the point of the whole thing.*

*You'll not believe me for a good long while. I don't hold it against you; I didn't
believe it either, and I had considerably longer to get used to the idea.*

*So: twenty-one cards to turn, a small white dog, and a young woman who does not yet know
a single solitary thing — a condition I have come to regard as a kind of wealth. She has
no number. She belongs to no position in the spread and may be played at any point in the
game, which is the whole of her power and the whole of her trouble.*

*Let's begin where she did. In the dark, before dawn, with the sound of a card being
dealt.*

---

# Chapter One — The Leap

Before there was anything to see, there was a sound.

Cards, squared against a table. Once, twice — that small satisfied knock a deck makes
when a pair of hands tidies it, the sound of somebody deciding to be serious about
something. Then a pause long enough to be a held breath. Then the snap-slide of a single
card coming off the top.

**THE QUERENT.** *One more card. There's always one more card.*

Wind, faint, somewhere a long way off, as if heard through a wall.

Another card, closer. Almost gentle.

*There you are. Good. Wake up, little Excuse — you're wanted.*

*  *  *

She woke with her hand already curled around something, which is a strange way to arrive
anywhere.

The first thing was the sky: the colour of a robin's egg, going gold along one edge like a
page held too near a candle. The second was the smell of a fire that had stopped being a
fire — embers gone grey and shy, a last thread of smoke standing straight up and thinning
into nothing. The third was the grass against the side of her face, cold and damp and
extremely interested in getting into her ear.

The fourth thing was that she had no fourth thing. No yesterday to fetch up out of the
dark and set beside all this; no name on the tip of her tongue, and no ache of one
missing. Some people would have found that a horror. She lay still and found it only
enormous, the way a room is enormous before the furniture arrives.

Then she sat up, and the world had to make space for her, and it did.

She was a young woman, brown-skinned, built wiry the way a whip is wiry, all forearm and
no spare. Her hair had been cropped rough and short by somebody in a hurry with their own
knife, and it stood up on one side where she'd slept on it. She wore a traveller's motley
gone soft with weather — patched at the elbows in a colour that had never matched
anything, boots creased into the exact shape of feet that had walked. You would have liked
her, I think. Nearly everybody did, in the end, and the ones who didn't had reasons of
their own that were mostly about themselves.

In her right hand, loose, lay a white rose. Three petals, closed and modest, and the stem
without a single thorn on it, which she would not appreciate properly for some weeks.

A few feet off, in the wet grass, a small white dog was sitting and watching her.

He had the specific patience of a dog who has been awake some considerable time and is
not in any hurry about it — scrappy, short-legged, one ear up and one ear making its own
arrangements, his coat the grubby white of a thing that has been white and is doing its
best. He did not bark. He did not perform any of the pleasure he was plainly feeling.
When her eyes found him, his tail rose and came down once against the turf. *Thump.* A
formality, observed.

"Hello," she said.

Her own voice startled her more than the dog had.

*Thump*, said the tail again, and that was that; the terms were agreed, and neither of
them ever revisited them.

The bundle leaned against a stone: a quarterstaff of pale close-grained wood, worn dark at
the grip, with everything she owned knotted into a bundle at one end. She got her hand
round the shaft and lifted, and it came up at exactly the weight her arm had already
guessed. That was the first thing that ever frightened her, and it lasted about a
heartbeat — the discovery that her body knew several things her head had not been told.

She pinned the rose at her belt. She stood up. Somewhere behind her ribs a voice that was
not quite a voice, that arrived the way a good idea arrives, said:

*Go on, then. Have a look at where you've landed. I'll wait — I've had three hundred
years of practice at that particular skill.*

She turned in a slow circle, looking for whoever had said it, and found instead the
meadow.

It went up in a long green shoulder to the north and fell away everywhere else, and the
grass stood as high as her knees, and the wind — there *was* wind here; remember that,
because it matters more than anything else I could tell you this morning — came across it
in broad slow strokes, laying the grass down silver and letting it up green, over and
over, like a hand smoothing a bedsheet that will not lie flat.

Past the grass, in every direction, was an edge. Past the edge was cloud: a bright white
floor of it, level as a table, out to a horizon with nothing standing on it at all.

The cloud did not move. Not the way the grass moved. Not a wisp, not a curl. It lay there
beneath the world's lip like something painted, and the wind that was busy in her hair had
no opinion about it whatsoever.

She did not know enough yet to find that odd.

*  *  *

The meadow was not empty. It only looked empty until you crossed it.

There were fire-rings in the grass — a great many of them, once she knew to look,
scattered about in the untidy way of things that were never planned together. Some were
only a darker circle in the turf, the stones long since gone to moss. One still had half
a lean-to over it, the canvas rotted to a lace. One had a bent tin cup sitting upright, as
though set down between mouthfuls. And one, up near the shoulder of the hill, had a
walking-stick driven into the turf with its handle polished pale — exactly the way a
person leaves a stick when they mean to come straight back for it.

None of them were the same age. She knelt at one and found the ash gone to soil with grass
through it. She knelt at another, tucked under an overhang out of the weather, and found
the ash still black and faintly gritty, close enough to warm that she put her palm flat in
it to check. It was cold. But it had not been cold long.

*Every one of those belonged to somebody who woke up exactly where you did*, said the
voice in her ear. *Some of them stayed a night. Some of them stayed rather longer than
that, by the look of it. All of them left the same way, in the end.*

She counted, for a while, and then stopped counting, because the number stopped meaning
anything and started meaning something else.

Pip — she did not know his name yet, but he had one, and she would arrive at it the way
you arrive at a word you have always known — Pip had gone ahead to a patch of bare earth
by the largest ring and begun to dig. Not the aimless scuffing of a dog with time on his
hands. Proper work: front paws going like a mill-race, hind legs braced, soil arriving
between them in a steady brown fountain, tail a blur of professional joy.

She crouched and made a noise at him — nothing you could spell, a sort of encouraging
click — and he redoubled, then reversed out of the hole with something in his teeth and
brought it to her at a brisk trot, head high, the way you carry a thing to a person who is
going to be sensible about it.

A dog. A wooden one, whittled, no longer than her palm. Crude work — the legs were four
different lengths and the head was mostly optimism — one ear worn to a stub by handling,
the whole of it gone smooth and dark the way wood does when it's been turned over in
somebody's fingers on a great many evenings for a great many years.

It was not fine. It was somebody's own knife and somebody's own poor skill, and somebody
carving the one thing sitting beside them because they wanted a second copy to keep.

*Someone made that*, said the Querent. *For company, I'd guess. Or luck. Or because the
nights up here are long and a knife needs something to do. Keep it. It's not much. It's
yours now, all the same.*

She turned it over. "Who carved this?"

*Somebody with a knife, a dog, and too much night to fill. That's all I know, and it's
more than most keepsakes get.*

She looked at the real dog, who had already lost interest and was investigating the hole
for a possible second dog. "Can dogs really walk with every Fool?"

*So they say. So Pip says, in his way — which is to say, not a word, and every answer you
need.*

"Pip," she said, trying it. The ear that was up stayed up. The other one swivelled about
a quarter of the way and then thought better of it.

She wrapped the wooden dog in a corner of the bundle and knotted it in. "I'll keep it
safe."

*Good*, said the Querent, and there was something under the word, in the way there is
sometimes something under a floorboard. *That's rarer than you'd think, keeping a small
thing safe. Most people save that for the large ones.*

It stayed in the Bindle. I'll say no more about it than that; I have a long way to go
this evening and no wish whatever to arrive early.

*  *  *

The ground rose to a modest knoll, and on the knoll stood the only tree on the whole
plateau.

It was dead. Bare, black-branched, its bark peeling away in long dry curls like paper
left in the sun. Everything else up there was green and windblown and aggressively,
insistently alive — the grass, the gorse, the small yellow flowers she had no name for.
This one tree was not. Nothing grew in its shade. Nothing had for a long time.

Pip trotted up to it, gave it a long professional consideration, and elected — with
visible reluctance, in the manner of a dog making a difficult moral choice — not to.

She put her hand flat on the trunk. The bark came away in a curl under her thumb and she
caught it and set it back, guiltily, where it would not stay.

*That's the only thing up here that dies, you know.* The voice had come down a register;
it was not doing its dry little performance any more. *Everything else on this plateau
just... carries on. Very restful, if you don't think about it too hard.*

She waited. The wind moved through the dead branches with a sound like a page turning.

*This meadow sits outside the proper Reading, little Excuse — off the edge of the Spread,
where the cards don't quite reach. Down there, further on than you can see yet, nothing
has been allowed to end in three hundred years. Not one thing.* A pause. *That tree's the
odd one out. Or perhaps it's the only honest one. I go back and forth.*

She thought about it for a while, in the way of somebody with no other thoughts to get in
the way — which is, I have found, the most dangerous sort of thinking there is.

"It's the only one that got to finish," she said.

I did not have anything to say to that. Not then.

*Anyway*, said the Querent, after a moment. *Nothing to be done about a tree. Onward.*

*  *  *

The path — or at least the place where the grass had been walked thin often enough to
remember it — narrowed between two standing stones, and past them stood the first thing on
the whole plateau that had been *built*: a little wayside shrine, half swallowed in ivy.

She did not reach it, because three soldiers stood up out of the grass.

They came up on either side of the path, unhurried, the way men rise when they have been
waiting rather than hiding — person-shaped, person-sized, in faded tabards, and where
each face should have been there was a smooth blank oval, like an egg, like the back of a
spoon. On the chest of one, a large printed **2** and a cup. On the second, a **2** and a
sword. On the third, a **2** and a wand.

They came at her without a word, because they had no mouths to make one with.

I would like to tell you she was magnificent. She was not, particularly. She got the staff
up crossways because there was nothing else to do with her arms, and the first blow — the
Cups, coming in flat and slow — rang off the wood and jarred her back onto her heels, and
she made a noise she was embarrassed about for two full days afterwards.

But her hands had known the weight of the staff, and now her feet learned what her hands
already knew. She came out from under the second one sideways, in a roll that was mostly
falling over with ambition, and got up inside its reach where the swing was useless, and
put the butt of the Bindle into the middle of that blank white face.

*Blanks*, said the Querent, comfortably, from what sounded like a chair. *Twos — the
lowest rung there is, so don't take it personally if this feels easy. Hit them, don't get
hit back. It's not a more complicated plan than that for quite some time yet.*

"Not," she said, ducking, "helping."

It was on the third one that it happened. The Wands came in overhead, both hands, a big
committed swing you could see arriving from a fair way off — and something in her chest
took a breath and held it, and the whole morning slowed down like syrup off a spoon. She
had time. Absurd, unreasonable time: time to see the stitching along the hem of the
tabard, the grass bending under the soldier's forward boot, a bead of dew coming off a
stalk and hanging in the air with nothing to do. She stepped inside the swing the way you
step through a doorway.

Then the world let its breath out, and she was behind him, and he was not fast enough to
be anywhere.

Three strikes, clean, and it was done.

They did not die dramatically. They did not die at all, and it took her a while to work
out that this was the thing bothering her. Each one, when it had had enough, simply
slumped — folding at the knees and then the waist, settling into the grass like a puppet
set down kindly by somebody who meant to come back for it later.

And then the card came off. The stiff painted rectangle peeled away from each tabard,
unhurried, and went up. Not blown — *up*, tumbling end over end on a wind that was not
there, while the actual wind went on combing the grass the other way entirely, as
unbothered as a river by a fish.

She watched all three slide away over the ridge line. Beyond it the grass stirred and
three figures stood up out of it — blank-faced, empty-tabarded — and each card came down
and settled onto a chest and printed itself there, and the three of them walked off
briskly in three entirely different directions, on business of their own.

She stood in the flattened grass with her heart going and the staff hanging off one hand.

"Was that a person?"

*There they go*, said the Querent, which was not an answer, and I would like the record to
show that it was not a lie either. *Don't fret over where — they'll turn up again, same as
they always do. Nothing much ends around here just yet.* A beat. *You'll get used to the
shape of that, one way or another.*

*  *  *

The shrine, up close, was smaller than it had looked and older than it had any business
being: a stone basin, a low arch worn round at the edges by three centuries of hands she
would never meet, and a scatter of offerings gone so far to moss they were mostly moss
with an opinion. Somebody had left a thimble. Somebody, at some point, had left a boiled
sweet, and everybody since had sensibly left it alone.

She sat down on the step with her back against the arch, because her legs had begun
sending complaints, and found that the rose at her belt had gone strange.

It was moving. Not in the wind — against it. As she watched, one of the three closed
petals unfurled and opened out, taking its time, with a small private sound like a person
letting go of a breath they had been holding for politeness' sake.

*That's a Waystation*, said the Querent. *Rest here and the Rose comes back to you —
regrows entirely, no waiting about. There'll be one of these in every region worth the
name.* A pause, and then, more carefully than the rest of it: *Consider it the one promise
I intend to keep without complication.*

She rested. Pip lay down against her thigh with a grunt like a much larger dog and went to
sleep instantly, in the way of an animal for whom the world contains no outstanding items.

While he slept she went through the Bindle: a tin, a length of cord, a comb she had no use
for — and, sewn into the lining of her coat with a care the rest of the coat had never
received, three shallow pockets, one behind the other, each about the size of a playing
card.

All three were empty.

*Still nothing to slot in that little spread of yours*, said the Querent. *That changes
soon enough.*

"What goes in them?"

*Ah*, said the Querent, and let the silence get comfortable, and then said nothing else
at all.

*  *  *

Past the shrine, the meadow simply stopped.

Not a slope, not a scree, not a decent gradual argument between land and air — a long,
clean edge of turf, as though the plateau had been dealt from a deck and this was where
the card ended. She sat down with her boots over the drop, and Pip came and sat beside her
with his chest out, surveying the nothing like a landlord.

There was nothing to do out there but ask questions of the only voice available. So she
did, and I answered, and I would like it noted that I told her the truth every single
time. Not all of it. But every word of it true.

"Who are you?"

*Someone who's waited a long while to have someone to talk to. That'll have to do for
now.*

"Will you tell me eventually?"

*Perhaps. Ask me again somewhere further along, and further along after that. I'm not
being coy —* a considering pause *— well. I am. But there's a reason.*

She pulled up a stalk of grass and split it with her thumbnail. "What am I, exactly?"

*The Excuse, if you want the formal title. The one card that belongs to no position and
may go anywhere at all. Handy, that.* And then, warmer: *You're new, is what you are. No
past to speak of, none needed — every wonder you're about to have is going to be a
genuinely first one. I'd almost envy you, if envy were a thing I had time for.*

*And — between us — you're also the one holding all of this together right now, aren't
you. Every step, every choice.* A small dry laugh from nowhere in particular. *Funny how
that works. Don't let it go to your head.*

"I'll try not to."

She threw the split grass out over the edge and watched the wind take it sideways and up
until it was gone.

"What's wrong with the world?"

*The cards are stuck, little Excuse. Every last one of them, in place, since before your
grandmother's grandmother. Go and turn them loose.*

"Stuck how?"

*Stuck like a wheel that won't turn. A sun that won't set. A bell that won't stop ringing
the same hour. You'll see it plainly enough once you're down there.*

She thought about the dead tree with nothing growing under it. "Can I fix it?"

*"Fix" is a strong word for what's coming.* The voice did something then that she was not
experienced enough to catch, though she would remember it later, and so would I. *"Finish"
might serve better. Ask me again once you've actually met one of the stuck ones.*

The light had gone long and yellow while they talked. She sat with her arms round her
knees.

"Why me?"

*Because you're the one who got dealt.* Quite gently. *I don't make the rules of the
Reading. I only get to ask the questions.*

She nodded slowly, as if that were an answer, which it very nearly was.

*That's rather a lot of honesty for one afternoon*, said the Querent, brightening with
audible effort. *I'll dole the rest out as we go — in fairness, so will the world. Go on.
Have your look at the edge properly, this time.*

*  *  *

The sun came down the sky and turned the cloud-floor to a lake of gold, and the meadow
behind her went the colour of a struck match, and the wind dropped the way it does at the
end of a day, until the grass stood still and listening.

Pip got up. He trotted past her legs and out to the very lip — right out, where the turf
gave way to a hairline of soil and then to nothing at all — and stood with his ears up and
his tail going, looking out over the drop with the frank enthusiasm of a dog who has done
this exact thing more times than anybody has bothered to count.

"Pip," she said. "Come back from there."

He turned his head and gave her a look. It was not a challenging look. It was the look of
a colleague waiting on a slower colleague.

Then he jumped. Off, out, into open air, front paws first, tail still going, without one
flicker of hesitation — and gone.

She was on her feet without deciding to be, at the edge, staring down into gold cloud with
her heart slamming and absolutely no plan.

*That's rather the whole method, you know*, said the Querent, and it was the kindest the
voice had been all day. *He never looks down first. Neither should you.* A beat. *It's not
bravery, particularly. It's just what a Fool does when a Fool is done standing still.*

She stood there a long moment on the edge of the only world she had, with the last of the
light on her face.

Then she stepped off.

*  *  *

Falling has a beginning, which is terrible, and a middle, which — and I say this as
something that has watched a great many people find it out — is not.

The scream came out of her and blew away behind. The cloud took her, cold and wet and
blind, and then it was under her — and the whole world lay open below in the last of the
light like a hand of cards dealt face-up on a table too big to see the far side of.

A wheel of country, laid in a ring: fields; a black wood; a canyon splitting the green
like a dropped plate; grey stone stacked into a city; water. And running round the whole
of it a great pale road, with something moving along it very slowly.

At the centre, where the hub would be, was a point of still silver — an open eye of white
stone with no cloud over it and no shadow in it. She squinted, falling, her eyes
streaming. There was a figure at the middle of it. One figure, very small, turning.
Turning, and turning, and turning.

She could not see it clearly. She could not look away from it either. That was the first
time, and there would be a great many others, and I dreaded every single one.

*There it is*, said the Querent, over the roar. *The Spread, entire.*

Away north, a tower stood snapped clean through like a stick of chalk, the rubble of its
own falling hanging in the air around it, stopped, every stone waiting its turn.

*The Axis at its heart. The Spire still coming down after three hundred years of trying.*

Away east, gold fields blazed under a hard white sun that sat high and did not appear to
be going anywhere at all, though the sun over her own shoulder was three parts set.

*The Noonlands sitting under a sun that never once thought to set. It's beautiful and it's
wrong, and it has been both those things for longer than anyone still living can properly
remember.*

The ring of the world turned beneath her, growing, filling everything.

*Go and put that right, little Excuse. One card at a time.*

Then the details arrived all at once, the way they do — a crossroads, a hedge, a
chalk-white lane, a great heaped wagon of hay standing at the meeting of four roads,
improbable, enormous, and directly, generously beneath her.

She hit it with a *whump* that went off across the fields like a dropped mattress. Straw
went up in a wave and came down for a long time afterwards. She lay in the crater she had
made, entirely unbroken, wheezing, with a stalk of hay up her nose and one boot missing.

Pip was already sitting on the far side of the wain. He had not a single piece of straw
on him. He looked at her with the mild interest of a dog whose friend has taken an
unnecessarily complicated route.

Somewhere close, birds were singing. That is worth marking: in that field, at that
particular moment, there were birds and they were singing, and she would not hear anything
of the kind again for a good while — nor know, for a good while, that she was missing it.

She got her elbows under her. Beyond the hedge the sky was going the colour of a bruise,
and against it stood a great many poles with a great deal of striped canvas slung between
them, lit from within, gold and warm and enormous. Out of it came the sound of a crowd —
the settled shuffling murmur of people who have been sitting in the same seats for a very,
very long time.

And then, from somewhere close and delighted and entirely too loud:

"STEP RIGHT UP, step right up, don't mind the wagon, don't mind the straw, ladies and
gentlemen and whatever *that* was that just fell out of the sky —"


---

# Chapter Two — The Greatest Trick

She got out of the haywain the undignified way, which is the only way there is, and stood
in the lane picking straw out of her collar, and that was when she noticed the smoke.

A fry-stall stood at the top of the lane with a griddle going under it, and the smoke went
straight up out of the pipe for about the height of a man and then stopped going anywhere
at all. It did not thin. It did not lean. It hung there in a crooked grey column — the
exact shape of a breeze that had happened once, long ago, and had never been followed up.

Behind her, over the hedge, the birds were still at it in the last of the light. Ahead of
her they were not. The line between ran along the hedge as neat as a seam, and when she
walked through it the sound stopped, the way a door shuts.

Then the banners: strung up the poles in their dozens, striped red and cream, every one
standing out stiff and full as though a good wind were in it — held mid-snap, corner
turned over and never turning back. Nothing anywhere moved but a great quantity of dust
hanging in the gaslight in slow golden constellations, which parted politely round her as
she walked and swirled shut again behind.

*There*, said the Querent in her ear, comfortably. *You've felt it. I'll not spoil it by
naming it.*

"There's no wind," she said.

*Isn't there. Well spotted. Keep hold of that.*

Then the smell: popcorn, hot and buttered and overwhelming, and old the way a cellar is
old. It did not drift to her on any current. It was simply there, and had been for three
hundred years.

Over an arch of painted board, in letters gone soft at the edges from being retouched by
loving hands too many times, it said:

**WELCOME, WELCOME, ALWAYS WELCOME.**

And a young man in a striped waistcoat two sizes too enthusiastic for him abandoned his
post at the rope line and came across the green at a dead sprint.

"Fresh face!" he shouted, from about forty feet out. "Oh, we do *love* a fresh face round
here — sit anywhere, the good seats are all the seats!" He arrived, out of breath, radiant.
"Also you fell out of the *sky*, friend, and I still haven't decided if that's the best
ticket I've ever sold or the worst."

He put out his hand. She looked at it, worked out what it was for, and shook it.

"Flick," he said. "Page of Wands, and personally — *personally* — the first friendly face
you'll meet on the whole Spread. I checked. Welcome to the Prestige! Show's been running
three hundred years and it has never, not once, had a night this good."

"Every night's the best night?"

"Every single one! That's showbusiness for you."

Pip sat down on Flick's foot. Flick did not appear to notice.

"What is this place?"

"The Prestige! Finest show on the whole Spread — and before you ask, yes, the *only* show
on the whole Spread. We don't get much competition out here."

She looked past him at the tents: striped canvas rising in naves and aisles like something
built to be prayed in, gaslit gold from within, the shadows of people printed on the walls
of them and not moving.

"Why hasn't the show ended?"

"*Ended*?" Flick laughed. It was a real laugh; that was the awful part. "Why would it end?
It's *good*. Best crowd, best acts, best —" and there it was, a half-second where his face
went somewhere else and came back, "— best everything. Every night."

"You sound unsure."

"Do I? Must be the popcorn talking." He said it fast, and he said it kindly, and he did
not look at her while he said it. "Go on, go and enjoy yourself. That's an order from
Management, and I *am* Management, technically, on Tuesdays."

"Who's the Magician?"

"Ah!" He came back to himself all at once, the way a lamp comes back when the wick's
trimmed. "The Bataleur. Star of the whole thing. And — get this — first volunteer in three
hundred years is going up there tonight, matter of fact. Lucky soul, whoever it turns out
to be."

"Can I see the show?"

"Course you can!" He was already digging in his waistcoat. "Three little acts first, mind.
House of Mirrors, the Menagerie, the High Wire — earn your stub at each and the big tent
opens right up." He pressed a folded paper programme into her hand, printed in three
colours, each act with a small empty box beside it waiting for a stamp. "Three acts, three
stubs, one ticket to the big show. Go on — Pip looks like he knows his way around a
carnival already."

She did not ask how he knew the dog's name. It did not occur to her until much later, by
which time there was nobody sensible left to ask.

*  *  *

The main tent lay across her path to everything else, so she went in at the back of the
audience, and that is where the Prestige stopped being charming.

There were nine hundred people in there, give or take, shoulder to shoulder on board
benches in three-hundred-year-old Sunday best — bonnets, watch chains, a boy in a collar
that had plainly been the worst hour of his life — and every one of them facing front, at
a stage with nothing on it.

Nobody turned to look at her. Nobody had turned to look at anything in a very long time.

Halfway down the nearest row a woman was holding out a toffee apple to the child beside
her, arm extended, wrist gone soft under the weight of the gesture. The child's hand was
six inches from the apple and had been for three centuries. The toffee shone like new. Of
course it did. Nothing here was allowed to go off.

She walked down the aisle very quietly, which was ridiculous, and she knew it was
ridiculous, and went on doing it.

"We're in the *good* seats," a man remarked, to nobody, as she passed. "Three hundred
years, the good seats. Worth the wait, I expect."

"My father queued all night for these tickets," said the woman beside him, comfortably.
"Wonderful man. Haven't the faintest idea what became of him."

"Ferridge there's been in the front row longer than any of us. Says he doesn't remember
what he did before the show." A pause. "Neither do the rest of us, come to that."

In the third row, feet not quite reaching the ground, sat a girl of about eight with a
ribbon in her hair, swinging her legs to a rhythm only she could hear — the one thing in
the tent moving under its own steam. She turned right round on the bench to look.

"Are you new?" she said. "We don't get new very often. Actually we don't get new *ever*.
You're the newest thing in three hundred years, I should think, other than the popcorn,
and even that's mostly the same popcorn."

"How long have you been sitting here?"

"Since before my mum was born, I think." The girl beamed. "Isn't that funny."

She said it exactly the way you say a funny thing. It was not, quite, and the tent seemed
to know it: nine hundred people held a beat of quiet nobody had asked them for. Then Wren
went back to swinging her feet and watching the empty stage, patient as stone.

I intend to be honest with you throughout, which is not the same as being forthcoming, so:
standing in that aisle, the Fool did a sum. She had no numbers for it — she was one day
old — but she had the shape. *These people are waiting. The waiting is what's wrong. To
mend it, somebody must take the waiting away.* She could not have told you what that would
cost. Neither could I, at the time, though I had a much better idea.

She went out the side of the tent, and Pip went with her, and the toffee apple stayed where it
was.

*  *  *

The House of Mirrors was a long canvas gullet of angled looking-glass, lit from below, and
the moment she stepped into it there were forty of her.

They did not behave. She raised a hand and thirty-nine hands came up, but the fortieth
came up a heartbeat late, and by the time she'd found it in the glass it was doing
something else entirely. She turned a corner and met herself coming back, wearing an
expression she was fairly sure she had never made in her life.

Pip's opinion of the House of Mirrors was that it contained a great many other white dogs
and he did not care for a single one of them. He inspected the nearest, found it insolent,
and thereafter walked pressed to her boot with his nose forward, ready.

At the centre of the first chamber stood a woman in a coat sewn all over with mirror
shards, so that she came apart into fragments of herself whenever she moved.

"Everybody thinks the trick's in the glass," she said. "It's never in the glass. It's in
whichever one of us is fool enough to look real while we're doing it."

"How do I tell which one's you?"

"Watch for the one who's tired." Tansy Quill smiled — a lovely smile, worn down to the
canvas at the corners. "Glass doesn't get tired. People do. Even people who've been doing
this same trick for three hundred years." A beat. "*Especially* those people."

Then there were nine of her, and eight of them were perfect.

It took a long while. She was wrong twice, and being wrong meant walking into a mirror
hard enough to make Pip sneeze with disapproval. But by the third chamber she had stopped
looking at the coats and the faces and started looking for the one small thing that did
not match — and there it was. One Tansy Quill, of nine, held her shoulders a fraction
lower than the rest. Just tired. Nothing magical about it at all.

She walked up and stood in front of her, and the other eight went out like blown candles.

"There," said Tansy, pleased, and stamped the first stub with a little brass press she
kept in a pocket. She held on to the programme half a second longer than she needed to.
"If the show ever stops, I go where nobody's looked at the same trick twice. Fresh eyes
are the only currency that matters to me." She let go. "Not that it stops. Only if."

*  *  *

The Menagerie was a wagon-yard behind the tents, and everything in it was made of paper.

Folded, hinged, painted, jointed with wire and pinned with brass — a lion the size of a
pony, three ostriches, an elephant, and a peacock of such structural ambition that she
stopped walking. They moved. Not much, not smoothly, and with a sound like a book being
riffled, but they moved: the lion's ribs went in and out, the peacock's tail opened a
hand's-breadth and shut again.

A very large man sat on an upturned barrel with the elephant's back leg across his knees,
doing something delicate to a hinge with fingers like sausages.

"Wind gets in the paper," he said, without looking up. "They go a bit wild. Nothing worse."

There has not been a wind in the Prestige for three hundred years. He said it anyway,
because it was what you said, and because you do not stop saying a thing merely on account
of its no longer being true. I have watched an entire world do that. He was only a man.

Three of the beasts had wandered out of the pens — an ostrich picking about in the guy
ropes, a paper hare, and the lion, which had wedged itself between two wagons and was
riffling gently to itself.

"Send the dog at the stray ones," said Perrin Loom. "He'll herd better than I ever could,
and I lift *bridges*."

She looked down at Pip. Pip looked up at her. She had never sent him anywhere in her life,
and not knowing how lasted about as long as not knowing how to hold the staff had: she
made a sound in her throat and flicked two fingers, and he was gone like a thrown stone.

What followed was, by some distance, the best thing that had happened to her since she
woke up. Pip did not bark at the paper beasts. He *worked* them — head low, shoulders
down, cutting the angles, turning the ostrich with a look and steering it into the pen at
a brisk trot. The lion he simply leaned on. The hare put up a fight and lost.

"There," said Perrin, folding the elephant's leg shut. "Good dog. Good — whatever you are,
too."

He stamped the second stub with a hand the size of a dinner plate. The stamp disappeared
entirely inside it and he had to fish about for it afterwards.

"They'll ask you, before it's over. Whether we ought to go on the road or stop somewhere.
Everyone's got a view." He shrugged, which was an event, geologically. "I go where they
go. Not much point being strong on your own."

*  *  *

The High Wire was strung between two poles at an altitude that could only be called a
joke, lit the whole way with little lanterns, and there was no net.

There was, in fact, a net. It lay folded on the grass with a sign on it reading *NET
(AVAILABLE)*, and the sign had been there so long the paint had lifted, and nobody had
ever once asked, which was the joke.

Beneath the near pole a woman sat at a card table with a lamp and a worn deck laid out in
a cross.

"Shall I read your cards before you risk your neck?" she said. Then, before the Fool could
open her mouth: "...No. Don't answer, I already know. I always already know. It's the least
funny thing about this job."

"Will you read mine?"

"I already have, dear." Marigold Fen turned a card over, looked at it, and put it back
without comment. "You're the sort of card that doesn't sit still for a reading. Comes with
the title, I expect."

"What do you see for the show?"

"An ending, eventually." She said it lightly, the way you'd mention rain. "A good one, if
I'm reading it right. And I am always reading it right."

The Fool thought about the toffee apple, and the woman's arm held out for three hundred
years.

"I'd rather not know."

Marigold Fen laughed out loud — one short surprised bark of it — and put the back of her
hand to her mouth as though the laugh had escaped.

"Wisest answer anyone's given me all century. Off you go." And, as the Fool started up the
ladder: "I read cards for a living in a world made of them. No, I've never once found that
funny. Ask me again in three hundred years."

The wire was a finger's width and the whole world was underneath it.

She went out in three stretches, and here is what nearly killed her: on the second, the
wind came up. A steady warm press of it from below and behind, smelling of oiled canvas
and lamp black, lifting her coat and putting a long shiver down the wire. She leaned into
it, because you lean into a wind. Then it dropped, exactly, all at once, the way weather
never does — and she went over sideways with one arm windmilling and only came back
because her feet had already learned more than her head had.

She stood shaking, halfway across the Prestige, in air that smelled of machinery, and
understood that she had just been lied to by something. She did not yet know by what.

*Keep hold of that as well*, said the Querent, very quietly.

At the far pole a wiry old man was juggling five clubs. He was not watching them. He was
watching her come, and while he watched, the clubs went round and round in a lazy figure
like something a river does.

"Three hundred years," he said, "five clubs, not one dropped." He caught all five in one
hand without appearing to notice he had finished. "Some nights I think about letting just
the one go. Just to see." He looked at the clubs in his fist. "Never quite manage it.
Suppose that's its own kind of trick."

He stamped the third stub, and pressed the programme back into her hands, and held on to
it for a moment the way Tansy had.

"If the tent ever folds for good," said Old Tomkin, "I'd not mind new roads under it. Same
act, new faces watching." The corner of his mouth went. "Might even drop a club for them."

*  *  *

Three stubs. The main tent's flap glowed gold ahead of her, and from inside came a noise
she had not heard from that tent before: a crowd, awake, leaning forward. It swelled as
she came, and it was — unmistakably, for the first time in three hundred years — *for her*.

She put the programme into the gatekeeper's hand and went in.

The ring was enormous: gilt and red velvet worn soft to the weave, sawdust raked in
perfect circles, and above it, tier on tier, every seat full and every face turned. Nine
hundred people looked at her at once. It went through her like cold water.

The spotlights swung down, and out of the dark came a man in a top hat and tails, arms
spreading, smile arriving before he did.

"Ladies! Gentlemen! And whatever climbed out of that haywain earlier tonight —" the crowd
found this hilarious, in unison, on cue "— a *volunteer!* Our first new volunteer in three
hundred years!"

The gasp came up off the benches in a single perfect wave, the way the tide comes in, and
you could hear three centuries of rehearsal in it. The Bataleur's smile was a wonderful
thing, and worn absolutely through: the smile of a man who has made this exact gesture ten
thousand times because he has made it ten thousand times.

"Now then. Watch closely." He turned once on the spot, and the sawdust did not stir behind
him. "This is the greatest trick this ring has ever held, and it is about to be performed
on *you*."

Three spotlights snapped on. There were three of him.

They came round her at a walk, evenly spaced, identical down to the shine on their shoes,
tipping their hats in perfect time — and then all three came in at once, and the trick
turned out to be that only one of them could hurt her and all three could try.

She was badly outmatched; she had owned a quarterstaff for one day. But she had spent the
evening being taught, by a tired woman in a coat of broken glass, to stop looking at the
big obvious thing and start looking for the small thing that did not match — and it was in
the buttonhole. Each of them wore a red rose in the lapel. Two were flawless, waxwork,
eternal. The third was wilting: only a little, and a little more with every exchange, a
petal browning at the edge and curling. By the time she understood what she was seeing she
understood something with it — that the wilting was the only honest thing in the tent, and
that it was happening to the one doing the work.

She hit the man with the dying rose.

He staggered. The crowd screamed with delight. She went for a decoy by mistake and it
burst into confetti — and out of the confetti a stagehand straightened up, brushed itself
off, and came at her with a mallet: blank-faced, tabarded, a Page of Wands on its chest.

"Which one, which one!" the Bataleur cried, from somewhere, from everywhere. "A showman
never tells his own secrets — good policy, that! Keeps a man employed for three centuries
running!"

"What's your name?" she shouted.

There was the smallest hitch in the patter — a quarter of a beat; you would have missed it
if you were enjoying yourself.

"The Bataleur!" he sang out. "The Magician! Master of the Manifest, the Marvellous, the—"

Which is a list of things, she thought, ducking a mallet, and not one of them is a name.

Then the world went slow and syrupy in her chest — she had begun to be able to *ask* for
it — and she stepped inside a swing that had already committed and put the Bindle into the
ribs of the Bataleur with the dying rose, and the whole ring flipped over.

Not a figure of speech. The floor rotated on its axis and dumped the lot of them into the
dark beneath.

She landed rolling on packed earth among the machinery of every trick she had seen that
night: ropes and pulleys and sandbag counterweights, trapdoor springs, a rack of identical
top hats, a rail of identical tail-coats. And at the end of it, big as a barn door, two
enormous canvas lungs on a crank, wheezing air into a pipe as thick as a man — the wind:
the great unstopping wind of the Prestige, the one that had nearly had her off the wire,
going up through the boards to fill the banners overhead. Not weather. Never weather. A
machine, breathing a dead thing's chest in and out for three hundred years.

"*As above, so below!*" The Bataleur was up already, hat gone, hair on end, and he sounded
almost glad. "Every good trick's got machinery holding it up! Mine's just been holding the
*same* trick up a touch longer than most!"

The doubles came on again, and down here they were shabby — strings visible at the
shoulders, paint thin, mouths not quite finished. It was easier, and much, much worse.

She fought him the length of the under-stage, between the counterweights, in the smell of
oil and old rope, and at the end of it he was leaning on a prop crate with his chest going
and his rose down to two brown petals. She brought the staff round one last time and
stopped it a hand's width from his head, because he had put his palm up.

He sat down heavily on the crate.

And the office cracked.

I have watched this happen twenty-one times and it has never once stopped frightening me.
It is not a wound. It is the sound a plaster mask makes coming apart at the seam — the top
hat and the patter and three hundred years of the same smile all going at once, in long
bright fissures, off the face of a man who has worn them so long that what is underneath
has gone soft for lack of weather.

"That's — that's rather the finish, isn't it." He blinked at his own hands. "Funny. I don't
believe I ever actually reached the finish before—"

He stopped.

Something came up under the crack. Not blood. A name, surfacing the way a held breath
finally lets go.

"...*Wicke*."

He said it slowly, tasting the shape of it, the way a man off a long desert road tastes
water and has to stop a moment to believe in it.

"Wicke. That's — yes. That's me." He laughed once, and it was nothing like the laugh he'd
been using all night. "I'd almost forgotten the *sound* of it."

He got up off that crate a different man from the one who had sat down on it: looser,
older about the eyes, entirely unperformed. He looked at her properly for the first time
all evening — one performer clocking another.

"First new volunteer in three centuries," he said, "and you turn out to be the one act I
never saw coming. That's — that's rather good, actually." He shook his head, with enormous
professional respect. "That's a *good* trick."

Then he reached into the air beside his ear and took a card out of it. No flourish, no
patter: just a man handing over a thing that was, at long last, simply his to give.

"Trump the First. Manifest, they'll call it, if anyone ever writes it down properly." He
put it into her hand and folded her fingers over it. "Take it. Hands were always the
trick, you know. Mine. Yours." He shrugged. "Everybody's, really."

And then the world ended.

Not the world — I am getting ahead of myself, and I have promised not to do that. The
*show* ended. But it was the first thing in three hundred years that had been permitted to
end, and it went off under that carnival like a rope parting.

The bellows stopped. Both lungs sighed out and did not fill again, and every banner
overhead went slack at once with a sound like a great flock settling.

And then, into the silence where the machine had been, came a draught.

It came down the under-stage stairs from the open air: cold, damp, smelling of hay and cut
grass and rain three fields off. It moved her hair. It went along the racks of hanging
coats and stirred every one of them, and up in the tent above their heads three hundred
years of dust came down out of the lamplight all at once, softly, in a slow bright fall
like the smallest possible snow.

Somewhere above, a rope began to swing. Nothing had swung here since before anyone was
born.

Wicke stood in the middle of it with his face turned up and said nothing at all, which for
him was a very great deal.

She looked at the card in her hand. Then she opened her coat and put it into the first of
the three sewn pockets — the near one, over the heart, the *now* one — and it went in as
though the pocket had been cut for it, which it had been.

Her hands came alive. That is the only way I can put it and be truthful. She had two
hands, and then, without gaining anything, she had the *idea* of a third: a reach that
went out fifteen feet past her fingers, closed on a top hat across the under-stage, and
brought it to her through the air. She dropped it at once and made a small undignified
noise.

*Three little pockets*, said the Querent, *and twenty cards still out there to fill them.
You'll have opinions about that arrangement before we're through.* A dry pause. *Everybody
does.*

*  *  *

Above them, the audience got up.

Not all at once, and not neatly. It came in a long ragged wave from the front rows
backward — nine hundred people standing after three hundred years of sitting — and the
sound of it was knees. Knees all up and down the tent, cracking like a fire taking hold,
and then a rolling groan of pure sensation, the noise a person makes when a limb they had
forgotten about comes back.

The woman in the fourth row lowered her arm. The child took the toffee apple. Nobody could
persuade her to eat it, but she carried it about for a week, and I count that a success.

And somewhere at the back, an old man finished a laugh he had plainly begun a very long
time ago. It came out of him in two halves, arriving in two different centuries, and the
second half turned into a coughing fit and then into something else his wife had to hold
his arm through.

"I don't recall my legs doing that before," somebody said, wonderingly. "Cracking like
that. I don't recall my *legs* at all, come to that."

"Good seats, they were," said another. "Still are, I suppose. Just — not the only seats
any more."

Wren stood up on her bench to see over the heads, and tugged her mother's sleeve, and
shouted across the tent to the man in the ring with the cracked-open face:

"What happens now? What does everybody *do* now?"

And Wicke — who for three hundred years had not had a single honest answer for any of the
nine hundred people in front of him — spread his hands.

"Anything," he said. "Anything at all, poppet. That's rather the whole idea."

*  *  *

The office door backstage had been jammed shut so long that it came off its hinge when
they opened it, and the room behind was small and cold and knee-deep in mail.

Three hundred years of it: letters, bills, playbills, invitations, a bird's nest in the
window frame that had never had a bird in it. A desk. A single chair nobody had sat in
since before the Stall, and Wicke sat down in it as if it still fitted, which it did not,
quite.

The Troupe crowded the doorway — Old Tomkin with his clubs, Marigold Fen with her deck
under one arm, Tansy Quill throwing little wandering fragments of lamplight round the
walls, and Perrin Loom, who did not so much stand in the doorway as wear it.

Wicke picked an envelope off the top of the drift and opened it with his thumb.

"Lamp oil," he said. "Forty gallons. Delivered, unpaid." He turned it over. "Three hundred
and six years overdue, at four per centum. Compounding." He looked up. "I want you all to
know that I would rather have fought her again."

Flick, from somewhere out in the passage: "I *told* you there was post!"

And at the edge of the group, not quite able to leave and not quite able to come in, stood
an old man in a good coat with a programme rolled in one fist. He had been in the front
row since before any of them. His face was wet and he had not noticed.

"I don't rightly know," said Ferridge, "what I'm meant to do with an evening that hasn't
got a show in it." He looked at the programme in his hand as if it had been handed to him
by a stranger. "Silly thing to grieve, I know."

Nobody said anything.

"Grieving it all the same," he said.

They all turned, then, and looked at her — which she had not expected and did not want,
and which would go on happening to her for the rest of the journey.

"Well," said Tomkin, into the silence. "There's a question in it, isn't there. Whether we
go, or whether we stop."

"Fresh eyes," said Tansy, to the ceiling.

"Same table," said Marigold Fen, to nobody. "Twice in a row. Just the once. A woman likes
to lay her cards on the *same* table twice in a while." She was shuffling as she said it,
fast and beautifully, the way other people breathe.

Perrin said nothing, having already said it in the yard over a paper elephant's leg.

So the Fool stood in a dead man's office with a card in her coat and straw still in her
collar, one day old, being asked what a hundred-odd people ought to do with the rest of
their lives. She thought about a tired woman who wanted fresh eyes, and an old man who
wanted, more than he could say, to be somewhere he was allowed to drop a club.

"Go," she said. "Be seen by people who've never seen you."

Tomkin's whole face came up like a lamp. Tansy Quill shut her eyes. Perrin Loom nodded
once, slowly, and that was that, because he went where they went.

Marigold Fen kept shuffling. She did not stop and she did not look up, and after a moment
she said, quite pleasantly, "There. I did say I was reading it right."

"Marigold—"

"No, no." She cut the deck one-handed. "It's a good ending, dear. I told you it was a good
one. I only ever said it was good." She put the cards away, and stood, and patted the
Fool's arm on the way past with real warmth, and did not stay to be thanked for it.

And Ferridge, who got no choice at all because nobody had thought to offer him one, stood
in the doorway and watched them decide, and rolled his programme tighter, and said nothing
whatsoever.

*  *  *

It took four days to bring the Prestige down, and she stayed for all of it — the only time
in the whole long journey she watched a region change at walking pace.

The canvas came down in enormous slow breaths, panel by panel, folded six at a time into
bales like loaves. The gaslamps were crated in straw. The benches went onto wagons. The
WELCOME arch was taken apart and its soft-worn letters stacked in a barrow, face to face,
like plates. And the ring was raked flat, and stopped being a ring, and became a circle of
paler grass, and then a field.

The weather came in, which nobody there had a word for. It rained on the second day —
properly, sideways, out of a sky that had not changed its colour in three centuries and
did not change it now, because the sun of the Spread was nailed where it was and would be
for a long while yet; the rain simply arrived anyway, like a guest who has stopped
waiting to be announced — and Flick stood out in it with his arms spread and his mouth
open until somebody made him come inside. The paper lion went soft in the wet and folded up on
itself, and Perrin Loom carried it to the fire himself and looked at the fire a long while
afterwards.

On the fourth day the wagons stood at the crossroads with their wheels greased, and
Old Tomkin juggled to pass the time — five clubs, round and round, because a habit is not
a chain and there is no hurry.

"Best show we ever ran," Flick told her, hauling on a rope, "and the very last one! Bit of
a mixed bag, feelings-wise, if I'm honest — which I try never to be, in this line of work."
He tied off badly and looked down the eastern road with an expression she would come to
know very well. "Road's calling, apparently. Never been much further than the crossroads
myself." He grinned, and it wobbled, and he let it. "Exciting, that."

Ferridge stood on the empty green with his programme.

She went over. She had nothing to give him, and there was nothing to be given: every one
of these was going to cost somebody, and this was the cheapest bill the journey ever sent,
and it was already too much.

"You'll be off, then," he said. "You've the look."

"I'm sorry about the evenings."

"Ah." He waved the programme at her, gently, and put it away in his inside pocket, over
the heart, where a man keeps a thing he means to keep. "It was a *good* show, mind.
Whatever else. It was a good show."

*  *  *

The eastern road out of the Prestige had been shut behind the tents for three hundred
years. Now it lay open: a chalk-white lane running out between hedges that had begun,
tentatively, to move.

She went down it while the lamps were still lit, with straw in her collar, a card in her
coat, a rose at her belt with three petals on it, and a small white dog trotting well out
ahead in the manner of a professional who has been to all these places before and does not
wish to make a fuss about it.

Behind her, wagon wheels. Ahead, hedgerows learning to move again, and weather loose in a
sky whose sun still sat exactly where it had sat for three hundred years — the days here
would be kept by candles and appetites for a while yet, and nobody minded, having known no
other way of keeping them. And far off across the whole width of the world, small and silver and perfectly
still, the Axis sat at the centre of everything and glittered like a coin at the bottom of
a well — and in the middle of it, so far away it was barely a flaw in the light, something
turned, and turned, and went on turning.

She looked at it a while. Then she whistled up the dog and walked on east, and did not
look at it again for eleven miles, which for her was a very long time indeed.


---

# Interlude — The Programme

**THE PRESTIGE**

*proudly, for once and only once, presents —*

**THE VERY LAST NIGHT**

*Being the Genuine, Actual, No-Fooling Final Performance of the whole Company, prior to
Departure upon the Open Road. Management begs your indulgence: for three hundred years
every night was billed as unforgettable and meant it as a figure of speech. This one we
mean literally, and we are ourselves a little unsteady about it.*

*  *  *

Ladies! Gentlemen! Small enthusiastic dogs, if any should present themselves at the gate
with the correct number of legs! You are cordially invited to the one show this
establishment has ever run that is actually, finally, allowed to end.

You have seen us before. Of course you have. You have seen us nine hundred times, in the
same seats, and we are ashamed to say we never once noticed how tired you must have got
of noticing. That debt is paid the only way it can be — not by refunding you the years
(we asked; it cannot be done; see NOTICE, below) but by giving you, at long last, a last
act. A door that shuts. A curtain that comes down and *stays* down, so that when you go
home tonight it will be because the show is over, and not because you simply ran out of
evening.

The bill in full: Tansy Quill's glass, one final time, and this once she promises to let
somebody catch her tired on purpose. Perrin Loom's Menagerie, paper beasts and all,
donated afterward to any child who can carry a paper elephant without crying (results
expected to be mixed). Old Tomkin's five clubs, on the wire, and — he has given
Management leave to print this — he means to drop one. On purpose. Once. For the joy of
it. Marigold Fen will not be reading fortunes tonight. She says she already knows how
the evening goes, and for the first time in three hundred years she is glad she does.

And in the ring: Wicke. Just Wicke. No Bataleur, no top hat held at the angle of a man
who has never once been surprised. He asked that this be printed plainly, and Management,
this once, obliged him.

*  *  *

**NOTICE TO PATRONS, RE: RAIN-CHECK TOKENS**

The management wishes to apologise, sincerely and at some length, for the matter of the
rain-check tokens. It has come to our attention that a number of patrons are in
possession of tokens issued against performances cancelled on account of weather,
weather here being a thing that, until quite recently, this establishment did not
technically have. We are aware that some of these tokens predate the founding of several
neighbouring towns. We are aware that one was reportedly found sewn into the lining of a
coat belonging to a gentleman's great-great-grandmother. We will honour every one of
them, at the door, tonight only, though we confess we no longer recall precisely what a
rain-check token entitles the bearer *to*, and would be obliged if the bearer would tell
us. A seat, presumably. We have those. We have rather a lot of them, going spare, as of
tomorrow.

**NOTICE, RE: THE MATTER OF MR CUTTER VOSS**

Patrons of the rival sideshow situated regrettably close to our own gate are advised
that Mr Voss's strongman act is, as ever, entirely genuine, and that the management
extends him every professional respect, and that none of the foregoing prevents Flick
from being asked, this very night, whether Mr Voss might care to come inside for once
and watch a proper closing curtain instead of shouting over it from his own tent. Mr
Voss's answer, verbatim, was unprintable. We are printing it anyway, in spirit: he will
be along presently, and he will sit in the back, and he will absolutely deny enjoying
himself.

**NOTICE — LOST PROPERTY, RECOVERED THIS EVENING FROM UNDER THE BENCHES**

One (1) wedding ring, gentleman's, engraving illegible with age and, we suspect,
sentiment. One (1) false tooth, provenance unknown, dental history not our department.
Forty-one single gloves, no pairs among them, which the management finds statistically
alarming. One child's shoe, small, well-loved, matched to no child presently on the
premises or, so far as anyone can determine, born in the last three centuries. One (1)
tortoise, deceased, believed to have arrived under its own power and to have been, in
its day, a serious professional rival of the Troupe's dog. All items may be claimed at
the ticket booth. None will be, and Management has made its peace with that.

*  *  *

**A WORD FROM MANAGEMENT**

*Best show we ever ran. Go carefully.*
— W.

*  *  *

**THE ITINERARY**

Where do we go from here? East, first — through the Bower, where they tell us the
orchards want looking at by somebody who isn't crying over them. Then wherever the road
after that turns out to run: the Divide, if the bridge will have us; the Longroad, if the
Longroad still wants marching music by the time we arrive. We have made no further plans
than that, on principle. A show that knows its whole route in advance has stopped being
a road show and started being a schedule, and we have had quite enough of schedules for
one three hundred years.

Wherever the road goes, and then the next place. That is the whole of the itinerary, and
we are printing it exactly that long on purpose.

*[in a smaller, rounder hand, wedged into the margin, plainly added after the type was
set:]*

*Flick — do the new towns have good seats? Wren wants to know before we go. I told her
you'd say every seat's a good seat but she wants the* real *answer. — W.T.*

*[and beneath it, in Flick's own hand, hurried:]*

*Best seats in the house, every one, everywhere we ever go. That's not the patter, poppet.
That's a promise.*

*  *  *

One further line, set by the printer in the smallest type on the bill, easy to miss and
not meant to be missed twice:

*Front row, centre. Retired from tonight, and every night after. Held open, in honour of
a patron who was here for every single one of the others.*

No name is given. None was thought necessary. The green outside stands empty already,
in the shape of a tent that isn't there any more, and there will be a man on it tomorrow
with a programme rolled up and put away in an inside pocket, watching the last wagon go,
who will not need to be told which seat they meant.


---

# Chapter Three — The Endless Harvest

The eastern road out of the Prestige ran between hedges for the better part of a day, and
somewhere along it — she never afterward could say exactly where — the hedges stopped
moving.

She noticed it the way you notice a held note ending: not the silence, but the absence of
the thing that had been filling it. One stretch of hedgerow rustled in a wind she could
feel on her own face. The next — she could not have told you the join — stood exactly as it
had grown and not one leaf further. The wind was still on her skin; it had simply stopped
being in the hedge. She put her hand flat against a hawthorn spray, and it gave, softly, and
settled back the moment she let go, the air around it gone still in a way not merely quiet.
It was *held*. Like a breath a body forgets to let go of.

She knew what that was now. Two days ago she'd have called it only strange. Now she had a
word for it, and the word was worse, because a word means you recognise a thing the second
time.

"Bound," she said, to the hedge, and mostly to Pip, who was nosing a rut ahead.

*There it is again*, said the Querent, comfortable in her ear. *You'll get so you can feel
the seam before you see it. Most Fools do, by about the second region. You're ahead of
schedule.*

"It doesn't feel like weather stopping. It feels like somebody's listening."

*That's rather a good way of putting it.* A pause with teeth in it, if she'd known to look.
*Hold on to that thought.*

Behind her, faint and fading, came the creak of the Troupe's wagons rolling out at their own
pace — Old Tomkin's voice raised in something between a work-song and an argument with a
wheel. She did not look back. A goodbye said twice loses something of its honesty.

Ahead, the hedges stood in postures the last breeze had left them in, patient as furniture.
Pip put his nose to a bank and began, with sudden conviction, to dig, and came out backwards
a moment later with something in his jaws, trotting it to her with the air of a professional
already three days into his trade.

It was a card. Stiff, small, painted — one of the common Minors, the pigment gone soft with
three centuries in the earth but the lines beneath it still sure.

"Where did this come from?" she asked the hedge, the dog, the world in general.

"Everywhere," said a voice that was not the Querent's — and had no business being anywhere
at all, because a moment before there had been nobody on that stretch of road but a Fool, a
dog, and a hedge.

She turned. A woman stood at the lane's edge where the woman had, with total certainty, not
been standing — dry as a stick of cinnamon, ageless, in a coat sewn all over with little
pockets, each the size and shape of a card. She held out one hand, palm up.

"That'll be mine, I should think," she said, and nodded at the card. "Or it will be, once
it's found its way home. Most of them do, given enough dogs."

The Fool held it out. The woman took it with two fingers, said "Thank you" as plainly as
you'd thank a stranger for holding a door, and it vanished into her coat.

"Why do you want it?" the Fool asked.

"Somebody has to pick the deck up," said the woman, in the tone of a person explaining
something so plain it barely required saying. "It got dealt carelessly, three hundred years
back, and nobody's swept the floor since. I'm looking for all of them" — the Fool's next
question answered before she'd asked it, and firmly declined — and the Fool felt a door
being gently shut in a room she hadn't found the handle of, and understood not to push.

Pip had already gone to sit at the woman's feet, looking up with something nearer
recognition than patience.

"You're new," the woman said, not a question.

"Three days old."

"That'll change fast. And no — you needn't ask my name. I've had a few, none matter to this
conversation. Ask me again somewhere further along the road. I go where the deck goes, same
as you." She was already turning, the coat of pockets settling around her like a held breath
let out at last. "We'll meet again. We generally do."

And then — the Fool would swear to this, and be no more believed than about the hedge — the
woman was simply not on the lane any more. Gone, the way a card slips back into a deck you
weren't watching closely enough.

*Well*, said the Querent, something in the voice she hadn't heard before. *There's a face I
hadn't expected to come up so early in the deal.*

"Who was that?"

*Someone who's been at this rather longer than you have. You'll see her again. I wouldn't
spend too much thought on her meanwhile — there's a garden up ahead that wants all the
thinking you've got.*

*  *  *

The wheat began without warning and did not stop — gold, unbroken, running to a horizon that
had given up pretending to be anywhere in particular. The cart road narrowed the way a
throat narrows, two ruts becoming a seam of shadow between walls of gold taller than her
shoulder. The light held the way everything here was held: thick, honeyed, a note struck
and never allowed to fade, and the air smelled of fruit gone a day past its best.

She found a hay-wain further on, wheels gone green to the hub, buried to the axles in wheat
grown through its slats, nobody having unloaded it in longer than she had words for. Pip
sniffed a wheel and sneezed once, enormously, at the pollen — unbothered.

*Here's where "later" set in, little Excuse*, said the Querent, an old tiredness in it that
had nothing to do with dryness for once. *Somebody parked that cart three hundred years
back, meaning to bring it round after the harvest — and the harvest was never called.
Everything ripe. Nothing gathered. Mind the smell. You get used to it. That's rather the
trouble.*

Where the road met a lane stood a lean-to stall, in the shadow of a granary so full its
doors bulged, grain leaking from a split board in a thin gold thread, unswept, into the
dust. At the stall sat a family: a spare, sun-browned man with a ledger on his belt; a woman
shelling windfall crab-apples, the one thing theirs to touch; two children, thin in the
wrists, watching Pip with the attention of people for whom a dog might, conceivably, turn
out to be food. Behind them: everything. In front of them: sour apples, and the discipline
not to reach past their own knees.

"Custom," said the man, consulting his ledger with a gravity that did not match his coat.
"First we've had in — some while. We've nothing to sell you and no leave to give it away."

"Why not just eat the grain behind you?" she asked, and had not yet learned that a
reasonable question does not make its answer any less terrible.

"Ah. Now." No bitterness in it — the flat arithmetic of a man long past being angry about a
sum he'd checked and found, every time, correctly cruel. "It's hers, until the harvest's
*called* — and a body that helps itself before the word is a body that's stolen from the
Lady who loves us. Bursting granaries and a lawful little hunger. I'd charge admission if I
thought anyone'd pay to see it."

She looked at the children's wrists, and thought that stupid arrangements could sometimes
simply be fixed. She opened the Bindle and laid out on a crate what she carried: bread gone
a little stale, a wedge of cheese, a twist of dried fruit. Nearly everything she had.

"Take some of my food," she said.

A pause — pride and hunger doing brief battle, hunger losing by the smallest margin, which
is to say pride let it win.

"...The little ones first, then. That's a kind thing. We'll not forget the face that did
it."

The children fell on the bread like a festival, which to two children raised on sour
windfalls by lawful permission, it very nearly was. The mother turned half away, shelling
with sudden unnecessary concentration. The man wrote something small in his ledger, his
hand not entirely steady.

"You'll be wanting through to the middle," he said. "Nobody wants through except folk with
business at the Throne. Good luck to you — and I mean that in the full and ruinous sense."

"May I borrow a billhook?" The wheat ahead had already closed over the lane like water over
a dropped stone.

"Take it, and welcome — the one thing here that's mine to lend." He drew out a worn, curved
orchard blade, handle first. "Whether the path makes *you* anything back is between you and
the wheat." His dryness softened by one degree. "Mind how you go, friend. And — thank you.
For the little ones. A man remembers a debt like that longer than most things."

*  *  *

Cutting a path with a borrowed billhook was, she discovered inside twenty strokes, a great
deal more work than it looked, and a great deal less useful. She swung; stalks parted — and
before she'd gone six more paces, the wheat she'd felled was standing again. Not new growth.
The same stalks, rising back up out of whatever had held them down, as though the billhook
had never passed that way.

*Watch behind you*, said the Querent. *Three hundred years of gardeners have tried to cut
this region down to something a person could live in, and it grows back faster than a body
can swing. There's a fellow up ahead who never learned to stop. Do be gentle with him. He
means it.*

She swung on, and learned the futility of it: the path ahead opened, real gold falling; the
path behind sealed shut, polite as a door that had never considered staying open. She was
not being allowed to un-arrive. Sweat stood on her face by the time the wheat gave way to
orchard, and her arm ached like the first honestly-earned ache of her three days of
existence.

The orchard was worse, the way things dressed as blessings are worse than things that only
look like curses. Rank on rank of fruit trees bowed to the grass, whole branches split clean
along the grain and fruiting anyway straight out of the wound, sap running down in slow
amber ropes. The whole place groaned, gently, ceaselessly, under its own love.

In the middle of it, on a stool that had worn its own hollow into the turf, sat an old man —
hands like knots of applewood, long shears across his knees, trimming a stray briar.

"Mind the sap," he said, without looking round. "And mind the stool — she made it grow up
under me one afternoon, kind as you please. Can't rightly leave it now. Roots."

"I've come to see the Lady," she said.

"Course you have. They always have." He looked her over, not unkind. "Nothing in this garden
was ever improved by hurrying at it. Now. I know that look. That's a *helping* look. Put it
away."

"I only want to reach the Throne."

"Aye, and you'll get some fool notion in your head about *fixing* her, and it'll break your
heart and hers besides. I've pruned these briars sixty years, and look how *well* it's
going." He gestured with the shears at the drowning orchard. "She doesn't take kindly to
help. The most a body can do for the Lady is trim a little and love her and let her be."

She felt the fork in the road under her feet: go round him, or wait to be asked. The hollow
worn into the ground by his own body decided it. "Will you take me? You know the way better
than anyone."

Something complicated crossed his old face — surprise, then something almost pleased. He got
up with the deliberation of a man whose knees had opinions, and did not put the shears down.

"Gaffer Nettle," he said, walking, and named the trees the whole way down — the Melrose
row, the Pippin walk, the Warden pear inclined to drop without asking — the way a man might
name his children, and she understood this was his whole remaining kindness to give.

*  *  *

The trees thinned and the canopy closed overhead all at once, light swallowed into a green,
heavy dimness thick with hanging pollen. A trodden path wound inward, marked every few yards
by heaps of fruit left in wooden bowls to rot sweet and untouched. Bower folk, slow-eyed and
dreamy, shuffled fruit up and laid it down and drifted back for more. "Tribute for the
Lady," one murmured, not quite looking at her. "I forget, now, what we did before the
bowls." Then the path heaved where the bowls stood thickest, and heaps of fallen fruit
straightened up out of their own rot: soldier-shapes, tabards green with ivy, cards faded
past reading, bloated soft on three centuries of unharvested plenty.

They came at her without hurry, which was worse than speed. She met the first clean, and
felt the world slow by half a beat, a held breath she was learning was hers to spend, and
stepped inside a lurching swing the way she'd stepped inside a soldier's on the Cliff, a
lifetime and three days ago. She did not call the reaching hand often; it spent Fortune,
and Fortune came back to her only by daring. Twice, when a blow came too fast to duck
honestly, she felt for it anyway — closing on a shoulder and hauling it off balance, just
enough for the Bindle to finish what her hands alone could not. Pip worked the stragglers
with brisk economy, herding two into a low bough that showered them in apples and briefly
buried them. She laughed once, badly timed, and felt better for it.

Each one, felled, slumped the way they always did — folding gently, like something set down
by a hand that meant to come back for it — and the faded cards drifted away through the
canopy on a wind that was not there, while the pollen-heavy air did not stir at all.
Nothing here ended. Not yet.

*  *  *

The canopy broke all at once, and the light on the far side was a different animal
entirely. A great sunken bower opened before her, and at its heart stood the thing she had
been dreading since Gaffer Nettle first said the word *Throne*.

It was not a chair. It was a swelling of the garden itself — root and bramble and flowering
briar risen up chest-high and wide as a cottage, every inch of it fruiting and blossoming at
once. Grown through the middle of it sat the Empress. Briars laced her sleeves like living
embroidery; roses had opened along both bare arms, rooted in her own skin; a crown of them
sat at her brow; roots went down past any finding. She was entirely still — not the world's
flat, breathless hold, but the stillness of someone who has genuinely, for three hundred
years, tried with her whole strength to move and could not.

In one open hand, wrist gone soft with a gesture held three centuries past its use, she
offered out a single perfect peach — never fallen, never reached for, exactly as ready and
exactly as unreachable as the day the world stopped. The Fool thought of a toffee apple and
a child's hand six inches from it for three hundred years, and understood she had walked
from one kind of offered gift straight into another.

The Empress was smiling — the warmest, and without contest the worst, expression the Fool
had yet seen in this frozen country.

"There you are," she said, no coldness at all in it, only flooding warmth. "There you
*are* — oh, come in, out of the draught, not that there's a draught, I'd never allow one.
Let me look at you." Her eyes went over the Fool, tender and cataloguing, a mother counting
ribs through a coat. "You've come a long way on very little, haven't you. When did you last
eat? Never mind. Eat this. Eat it here where I can see you do it."

The hand did not move.

"Why can't you stand?" the Fool asked.

"Stand? Whatever for? Everything I love is right here, in reach. If I stood I might leave
something un-tended. Sit down, you're making me anxious."

"The whole region is starving."

"Starving?" A bright, astonished laugh. "Nobody starves in *my* garden — they only have to
wait for me to call it. I'll call it soon."
