"Doesn't holding still hurt?"

Something old and enormous flickered behind the smile and went straight back under, and the
smile came back firmer, as if she'd caught herself falling and set her own face back with
her own two hands. "Love doesn't hurt, child. Love *stays*. I stayed. That's all a mother
is, in the end — a thing that would not leave. Now. Have you eaten or haven't you."

The Fool looked at the peach, held out and unreachable, and understood there was no gentler
way through this than the true one.

"I've come to prune your garden," she said.

The warmth did not fall away so much as *crack* — a fine seam through the middle of that
smile, the way frost finds the flaw already waiting in good glass.

"...*Prune.*" As though tasting something spoiled. "You're one of *those*. The same look
they all had, the ones with the shears and the kindness. You want to *cut things down*.
Because you think it'd be *better*." Very quiet now, wounded past anger. "I made all of this
so that nothing would ever have to end. And you've walked into the middle of it to end it.
Sit *down*." A long, terrible pause. "...No, you won't, will you. Then I'm sorry, little
one. I truly am. But I won't let you take my garden from me. Not while I can hold it."

The briars reared. At each corner of the Throne a great crown-rose flushed from sleeping
pink to hard, furious red, and the whole sunken bower seemed to inhale.

*  *  *

She had thought she was fighting a woman. She was not. The Empress never moved — could not —
and it was the Throne that fought for her, a devotion indistinguishable, from the receiving
end, from love turned weapon. Lash-vines whipped from the bramble in long, readable arcs;
pollen-drunk soldiers hauled themselves from the roots, and Pip peeled them toward the
bower's edge.

"You'll tire before I do, little one," the Empress said, never raising her voice, which was
worse than shouting. "I've held all this three hundred years. I can hold it through one more
headstrong child with a knife."

The first crown-rose stood at the Throne's northern shoulder. She was thrown back twice —
once flat on her back in loam that smelled of honey a day too far gone — before the world
took its breath and held it, that syrup-slow stillness opening like a door, and she stepped
inside a vine's arc and put the billhook, and the Bindle's whole weight behind it, into the
crown-rose's stem. It parted with a sound like something enormous exhaling. The north side
of the canopy came down in a slow rain of leaf and blossom, and through the gap a bar of
real daylight fell clean and hard across the bower — gold and ordinary, nothing like the
honeyed, held light a moment before.

The Empress went very still. "— Thank you," she said, and the silence after surprised her
more than it surprised the Fool. "...Why did I *say* that. You've cut a thing I grew and
I — the light. It's so bright where you cut. I'd forgotten bright. Go on, then. Don't you
dare go on. Go on."

The second crown-rose, at the western flank, was uglier work — lash-vines faster now, one
blow catching her shoulder hard enough that the White Rose gave up a petal in a small silent
flare of warmth. Two petals left, in a country that would not let anything grow to a finish.
She fought through, calling the ghost-reach once to drag a strike wide, and severed the rose
in a spray of thorn.

"Thank you," said the Empress, slowly, with something almost like defiance. "There — I've
said it a-purpose now, so it's mine, not torn out of me. It is the hardest thing I have ever
done, and I have held a garden up with my own body since the world stopped." Her smile was
wet at the edges, still, unmistakably, a smile. "You're not cruel. That's the pity of it. If
you were cruel I could hate you, and hate is *warm*. But you're kind, and kindness doesn't
stay, kindness *moves you along*—"

She did not finish it aloud. The last crown-rose, trembling at the southern face, put out
every lash-vine it had left, all at once. She fought it with her arms shaking and the Rose
down to its final petal, spending the ghost-reach twice more on Fortune she had not yet dared
back, spent anyway because there was nothing left worth being careful with. The last blow landed
clean. The final crown-rose parted at its root with a long, tearing sigh, and the whole roof
of the bower came down at once, and full daylight reached the Briar Throne from top to
bottom for the first time since the Stall.

*  *  *

The Throne, unfed at last, gave all at once. It did not shatter. It *split* — slow and
clean, like a seedpod come, three centuries late, to term — and folded open around the
woman at its heart. Briars withered from her sleeves. Roses folded shut and let go. The
roots released her with a sound like a held breath finally allowed out.

"Oh," said the Empress, swaying upright on legs that hadn't held her weight in three hundred
years, both hands coming up before her face as if she barely recognised them. "Oh, there's
so much of it. For the longest time I couldn't feel where I *stopped*—"

Something came up under the crack in her voice the way a name always did — a thing long
buried, coming up for air.

"...Damson." She said it the way a person says a word in a language they spoke as a child
and had long stopped trusting their mouth to remember. "That's *me*." A laugh came out of
her, astonished, breaking halfway into something not quite a laugh any more. "I had a name
before the garden had me. I was a girl who liked things to grow. That was all."

She looked at the Fool properly then — not the cataloguing tenderness of before, but a
plain, unguarded attention, no longer smiling because she had to.

"You're still far too thin," she said. "I noticed the whole time, I couldn't say it and hold
the garden both." She reached into the open ruin of the Throne and drew out a card — no
flourish, only a mother handing over the one thing worth having. "Trump the Third. Bloom,
they'll call it, if anyone writes it kindly. It'll make things grow for you — a snare, a
vine, a rose that comes back even where the ground says no. Grow something you can bear to
cut down, this time. That's the trick I never learned. Everything I loved, I loved so hard
it couldn't end. Don't do that to a garden. Don't do that to anyone. Now go on. *Eat*, first.
Then go on."

The Fool opened her coat. Only the one pocket had ever taken a card — the Present one, over
the heart, where Manifest already sat warm and certain — and she swapped it for Bloom, just
to know she could. It settled into the seam exactly as the first card had: not power
exactly, not yet, but a kind of green permission, the sense that something close by would
grow if she asked it to. She drew it back out and returned Manifest to its place — the
reaching hand had got her through the tribute-path, and she wasn't yet brave enough to walk
on without it — and slid Bloom into the Bindle unslotted, to carry rather than wear, for
now.

*  *  *

The change was already running ahead of her through the trees: stranglevines sliding off the
boughs, split branches letting go their loads at last — three hundred years of harvest
arriving on the grass at once, until the ground rang faintly like rain on a roof.

She found Gaffer Nettle standing in the middle of it, shears in hand, and he did not lift
them. There was nothing left to prune. The briars he'd tended sixty years were simply gone.

"Well," he said. "There it is." He turned the shears over. "Sixty years of mornings, and now
the trees do it themselves and don't want me." A dry, wet sound came out of him, trying
gamely to be a laugh. "Funny thing about a job you never asked whether it needed doing." No
anger in his old face, which was somehow the harder thing to watch. "Don't mind me,
young'un. It's a *good* thing you've done. I'll want a day or two to remember what my hands
are for otherwise, is all."

She stood with him a moment in the falling fruit, then went on, and looking back once saw
him still standing there, shears loose in hand, watching an orchard finish something
without him.

*  *  *

The stall stood at the lane's end with its granary doors flung wide — not burst, *opened*,
swinging on hinges that had not lost their manners for the wait. From deep in the garden and
everywhere at once, a sound was going up the Bower had not heard since the Stall: long,
ringing, joyous, the harvest being *called* at last, by a woman who had only just remembered
she was permitted to let a thing be finished.

"That's —" The stallholder checked his ledger from pure reflex, then set it down and left it
there. "That's the call. I costed it out years ago as a nil return." A ragged, undignified
laugh broke out of him. "Children. *Children.* You may touch the grain. It's finally,
blessedly lawful."

The children were already running the grain through their fingers, laughing at nothing and
everything. "And you—" He turned to the Fool, his flat dryness gone from his voice. "You fed
mine when there was nothing in it for you and no law said you must. If you're ever hungry on
this road again, friend, you'll not be. That's a debt, and I keep my books."

*  *  *

The children were still running grain through their fingers when the Querent let the quiet
sit a moment before speaking — fond, unhurried, the way a person lets a good thing finish
being good before they say anything about it.

*Three hundred years she held all that up so nobody would ever have to say goodbye to a
summer*, said the Querent. *And it took a stranger with a dog and a borrowed billhook to
show her that a garden only means anything* because *it ends. You've been doing that all
along, you know. Pruning. The whole Spread, card by card — and I've been standing just off
the edge of every one, holding the shears steady while you learn the weight.* A small
silence, in which the voice was audibly smiling — not the terrible warm smile that had held
out a peach for three hundred years, but something plainer, aimed, for once, at her. *Don't
look at me like that. Somebody has to hold the shears. Go on, little Excuse.*

The lane out of the Bower, choked to the axles when she'd first walked it, lay open behind
the falling harvest — cut, this time, and staying cut.

*  *  *

She walked south with the last of the honeyed light going ordinary and gold and mortal, Pip
ranging well ahead like a professional with somewhere particular in mind, a second card
riding unslotted in the Bindle beside the first she wore, and a debt written into a ledger
by a hand finally given leave to stop counting only what was lawful.

Ahead, where the road fell away toward country she could not yet see the bottom of, the land
itself seemed cut once, cleanly, by something larger than a billhook, and never healed. And
far off, small and silver and patient at the centre of everything, the Axis sat where it
always sat, glittering like a coin dropped into still water and never fished back out — and
in the heart of it, turning, the small far figure went on with whatever it had been doing
since long before there was a Fool, or a dog, or a garden, to watch.

She looked at it a while. Then she whistled up the dog, and went on toward the canyon
country of the Divide, where — though she did not know it yet, and I am not, this evening,
in any hurry to tell her — a wedding was waiting that would want an honest answer to a
question about love that does not hold on quite so hard it forgets how to let go.


---

# Chapter Four — The Longest Engagement

South of the Bower the road was busy for the first time since she had woken at the Cliff.

Wagons went by her going the other way, heaped with the Bower's first harvest in three
hundred years — marrows the size of cradles, sacks of grain sagging at the seams,
children riding the tailgates with the particular shade of pleased that comes only from
being allowed to sit atop a wagonload of something enormous. Pip trotted alongside the
last cart for a quarter mile, accepted a heel of bread from a girl who seemed to think
this was owed him, and rejoined her the way a professional returns from a small errand.

*They'll be doing that the length of the Spread, before long*, said the Querent. *Wagons
on every road, carrying off a summer that only just found out it was allowed to happen.
Terribly ordinary thing to like, given everything, but there it is.*

The road climbed and thinned, the last of the carts falling away behind a shoulder of
hill, and somewhere in that stretch the birds stopped.

She noticed it the way you notice a held breath — not an absence, but a change in the
weight of the air, and she had learned, in three days of walking, to trust that particular
quiet the way other people trust weather. The grass ahead stood taller and stiller than
the grass behind. Then the road gave out at a lip of bare rock, and the world dropped away
into a canyon so deep the bottom was lost in blue haze, and she understood she had walked
out of one held breath and directly into another.

Bunting hung the whole visible length of both clifftops — strung pole to pole, faded to
the colour of old bone — and it was not still, exactly. It was worse than still. Every
pennant was caught at the precise violence of a snap, one whole ribbon frozen mid-crack
like a whip a heartbeat before the sound arrives — as though a wind had come through here
once, furious and glad, and simply never been allowed to finish saying so.

"There's no wind," she said, out of habit now, the way you'd name a coin before you spent
it.

*There never is, down here*, agreed the Querent. *But somebody's kept the shape of one
going a very long time. Wait till you hear the reason for it — it's rather sweeter than
the tent was, and rather sadder, too.*

Above the near edge, an unfinished bridge reached out from each clifftop and stopped — two
grey half-spans, arm's length apart, over nothing at all — and between them the empty air
held a shape she could not yet name, though she would come to know it well by the end of
the day.

*  *  *

The water-stair down to the landing had been worn into the rock by more feet than the
canyon now needed, and at the bottom a flat-bottomed ferry knocked its post in a river
that lay glassed and motionless — until the boat's own rocking broke it into small dark
rings that spread and vanished and did not renew themselves, because nothing was renewing
them.

A broad, unhurried old man sat in the stern with a pole across his knees and the settled
look of somebody who has heard everything twice and most of it three times. He lifted a
hand without getting up.

"Fresh face," he said, "and both feet still on the same bank. Rare thing round here. Most
folk have picked a side before they've picked their nose." He looked her over, and Pip,
already at home on the middle thwart. "Sit in the boat. I only know the one story, but I
know it very well."

She sat. He pushed off with a single lean of the pole, and the canyon opened around them,
vast and hushed, the two half-spans sliding overhead.

"Three hundred years ago," said Ferryman Pell, in the tone of a man beginning a favourite
grievance, "the two best houses on this canyon agreed to join. East gives her daughter,
west gives his son, and the bridge gets finished for the wedding procession to walk
across. Grand idea. Everybody wept. Bunting went up."

"And then?"

"And then the world stopped, friend, right on the day. Bridge half-built. Bride on one
side, groom on the other, and the small matter of *whose town holds the wedding* never
quite settled." He poled on, content. "Been not-settling it ever since."

"Whose fault was it, really?"

"Ask the east and it's the west's, for wanting the wedding in their fine cold hall. Ask
the west and it's the east's pride. Ask me and it's nobody's, which is rather why it's
lasted."

"Why not just finish the bridge?"

"Oh, they've tried. Old Cutwright Fenn's crew tries every day of their lives — three
generations of them now — and they stick on the same plank every single time. Bridge
isn't waiting on carpentry, friend." He looked at her sideways, and there was nothing
unkind in it. "It's waiting on a choice."

"Can the two just marry anywhere?" she asked, and meant it plainly.

"Bless you for asking it plain." He almost smiled. "No. It has to be *somewhere*, and
somewhere means a bank, and a bank means a town wins and a town loses. That's the whole
knot, friend, said as kindly as I know how to say it."

The ferry bumped a landing on the east side, gentle as a full stop.

"There," said Pell. "Two banks, two towns, one wedding that won't happen. Go and let both
sides tell you it's the other lot's doing. Then go up top, to where the bridge stops.
That's where the pair of them are." He settled back into the stern as she climbed out, Pip
already three steps up the stair ahead of her. "Whistle when you want ferrying. I'm never
far — it's a boat, not a career with prospects."

*  *  *

The east town climbed the cliff in salt-bleached tiers of blue and shell-white, a cup set
out on every threshold as though hospitality were a fact of architecture here rather than
a habit of people. Wedding garlands hung dry above every lintel, and every window faced
the sea, glassed and patiently still.

At the top stood the bride's hall, and beside it a chapel whose bell had not sounded once
in three centuries — the town saving its one great noise for a wedding it had never been
allowed to hold. The Elder who rose to meet her had eyes the pale of someone who has spent
a long life looking at one horizon, and a folded garland kept in her lap like a christening
gown she had never got to use. She took the Fool's hands in both of hers before she had
said a word, because that, apparently, was simply what this house did.

"Blessings on your road, and sit, and be welcome — no, don't refuse, we've a cup out and a
cup out means it's poured." She poured. "You've come about the wedding. Everyone comes
about the wedding, eventually."

"Tell me the east's side," said the Fool.

"Our girl. Our chapel, blessed by every one of our dead in the ground beneath it. The
wedding was promised *here*, with the sea to witness — and if it goes across to that cold
stone hall, our own dead go unhonoured by the one ceremony they were owed, and our girl
sails away, and this town has nothing left to wait for. We are the poorer town, you'll
have seen. When pilgrims come for a wedding by the sea, they come here. That is all we
have coming. That, and her."

She set out a second cup as she spoke — not for the Fool. For someone across the water. It
had been set out, and never once drunk from, for three hundred years, poured with the
weight of a chore she had long since stopped believing would ever be needed, and had never
once considered stopping.

"What is she like?" the Fool asked.

"Kind. Frightened. In love across a canyon with a man she cannot reach or refuse. Same as
the day it froze. She's been almost-brave for three hundred years. It's a wearing thing to
watch."

"Would you forgive the west?" the Fool asked, because it seemed the kindest of the
questions available to her.

The Elder took a long breath, and let out half a laugh with it. "Ask me at the wedding. If
it's over there, I'll be the old woman crying in the wrong hall — and I'll mean every tear,
half grief and half gladness. That's Cups for you. We weep at both."

She poured the untouched second cup back into the jug, gently, and set the empty vessel
out again. Ready. Always ready.

*  *  *

The west town was a short crossing and a long climb, stone where the east had been timber
and salt: a proud grey terrace rising to a great ancestral hall, built — she understood
this before anyone told her — by a lineage with no other purpose but the one wedding it
had never got to host. Its doors stood open on a floor swept to a shine and standing
entirely, perfectly empty. The Elder met her at the threshold with a stiff, weary bow, a
ledger open on a lectern behind him, every course and toast and seating arranged in ink
that had not faded because nothing here was permitted to.

"You'll have heard the east's version first," he said, before she had said anything at
all. "Everyone hears the east's first — they've the prettier chapel and the sadder eyes.
Hear ours now, and judge with a full hand."

"Tell me the west's side."

"This hall was raised for it. Stone on stone, generation on generation, every one of them
building toward the single day their line would host the joining of the two towns. That
is not pride, whatever the east calls it. That is three hundred years of purpose, cut off
one plank short of the door. Our dead built a promise, and if the wedding goes east, the
promise gets kept somewhere else."

"What is he like?"

"Dutiful. Proud. So afraid of choosing wrong that he has chosen nothing for three
centuries, and calls it honour. He loves her. He has simply never once been able to say
*where*."

"Which town deserves it more?" she asked, and meant it as earnestly as she had meant every
question so far.

"*Deserves.*" He almost smiled, a thin, dry thing, real approval in it, as though she had
passed some small test without knowing she was sitting one. "There's the foolish word, and
I mean that kindly — you've the right of it by asking it. Neither. Both. That is precisely
why nobody has been able to say it for three hundred years."

He closed the ledger. Then, as though the gesture would be unlucky otherwise, opened it
again to the very same page. The arrangements waited. Everything here waited.

*  *  *

There was no proper road to the bridge's broken end, because there was no proper bridge
for a road to lead to. The way up was Cutwright Fenn's own work: ladders lashed to the
cliff face, guy-ropes strung between them, board-and-plank scaffold climbing the half-span
like ivy that had decided, against all sense, to be useful. She went up hand over hand,
Pip already three rungs above her, professionally unbothered.

A tally was carved into the scaffold's main upright — hundreds of scratches, cut in a
dozen hands over a very great many years, the newest still pale where the knife had barely
finished with it. The last legible line read: *same plank, gods turn its card.*

She put her palm flat to the wood, the way you'd touch a gravestone you didn't know the
name on, and climbed the rest of the way in a respectful silence she hadn't planned.

*  *  *

The traverse ended on the flat of the east half-span, where the stone simply stopped — a
clean broken lip, and beyond it the gap: an arm's length of nothing, and the west
half-span reaching back the same short, hopeless distance.

On the two lips, facing each other across that gap, stood the Betrothed.

They were dressed to be married, and had been for three hundred years — a gown gone the
grey of the fog below, a coat gone thin and soft, garlands crumbled at both collars to a
scatter of thread. One hand each, lifted, reaching across the gap toward the other,
fingers a hand's breadth apart, exactly as far apart as the bridge that had never been
built to close it. Everything the bride did, the groom did, in the same breath — mirrored,
perfect, frozen mid-reach.

Pip stopped at the traverse's end and sat, watching the two reaching figures with the
frank unbothered attention he gave to weather.

*There they are*, said the Querent, quiet in her ear. *Closest two people on the whole
Spread, that pair. Three hundred years reaching, and never once a hand's breadth closer.
It'd be funny, if it didn't ache so.*

She stepped onto the span. Both heads turned — the same turn, the same tilt — and regarded
her with the same weary displeasure, worn in perfect unison.

"No," said the one on the east lip. "This is not for watching."

"There are no onlookers here," they said, both at once, the two voices arriving as one
voice split down a canyon. "There were never meant to be onlookers. We have refused a
great many things for three hundred years, witness. An ending most of all."

They lowered their reaching hands — the same motion, mirror-clean — and raised them again,
together, as duelists.

*  *  *

She went for the bride first, and the Bindle rang off nothing at all: the blow passed
through the space where a body should have taken it, no more resistance than a hand meets
a reflection, and across the gap the groom flinched at a strike that had never touched
him, in the exact same instant.

*While they're mirrored, they can't be hurt*, said the Querent, the play gone out of the
voice. *You'll want to think about that, little Excuse, and not with your arms.*

She thought about it with her feet. A guy-rope of Fenn's scaffold hung within reach; she
hauled it and swung the west span's edge a foot wide of true, and for one staggering
half-second the two figures fell out of time with each other — and a blow that had done
nothing all fight *landed*, cracked across the bride's shoulder, actually hurt — and then
both of them turned back toward each other and closed the sync like a wound healing
itself.

"You cannot separate what refuses to be separated," they said together, mid-motion.
"Pull us apart and we will only reach harder. That is the whole of the last three hundred
years, witness, and you will not undo it with a stick."

She sent Pip in low and fast at the groom — *harry* — pinning his attention for one held
breath while she worked a counterweight loose and dropped the bride's footing a half-step
behind her mirror. The two of them hung apart, straining, longer this time, both faces
open with something that looked almost like relief and almost like terror in equal
measure. Then, because relief and terror both know the way home, they closed it. Again.

"Better this than to choose," said the east. "Better forever than to lose the other's
town its wedding," said the west. "Better to reach," they said together, and it was the
saddest sentence she had heard since she woke at the Cliff, "and never touch, than to
touch and know what it cost."

She hit them again, cleaner this time, a Fool's Chance slipping her inside the half-second
the sync broke — and it made no difference, because a bruise was not an ending and they
both knew it, and so, more slowly than she would have liked, did she. However well she
fought, they only re-mirrored. The span came to its own kind of standstill: Fool and
Betrothed and dog, all held, in exactly the shape the whole canyon had been held in for
three hundred years.

The Bindle could not finish this. She let it hang.

*There*, said the Querent, gently, the way you'd speak into a room where someone was
finally about to say something they'd put off for years. *Feel that? That's the fight
ending without being won. Some knots don't cut, little Excuse. They're waiting on the one
thing nobody in three hundred years would say out loud. You're nobody, you know. You
belong to neither bank — which means you're the only soul who* can *say it. Go on. Answer
the question they've been dying not to ask.*

The reaching hands lifted once more and held over the gap. Both Betrothed turned their
faces to her. For the first time since she had climbed onto the span, they were not
asking her to leave. They were waiting.

"Say it, then," they said, together, nothing left of the duelists' weary displeasure in
it, only three hundred years of tiredness finally arriving somewhere. "Say where. We have
never once been able to."

*  *  *

"Understand what you are saying before you say it," they said, both at once, before she
could speak, as though the rule had to be spoken aloud to be fair. "An answer cannot be
unsaid. Name a bank, and the other bank loses forever — the reaching stops, the pause
breaks, and whatever we are now, we will no longer be." A beat, and in that beat she
understood, entirely, what she was about to do. "Your word is the blow. There is no other
kind of ending for us."

She thought of the sea-eyed woman pouring a cup that would never be drunk, and the dry
grey man opening a ledger to a page that would never be needed, and understood there was
no way to say the word that did not close a book someone had kept faithfully open for
three hundred years, and did not do it to somebody who deserved better than to hear it
from a stranger. Both futures ached. She could feel the weight of the one she wasn't
choosing already, like a hand on the back of her neck.

"Here," she said. "Marry here, by the sea."

The words crossed the gap like a struck bell.

The groom's reaching hand closed on nothing, and understood, and did not pull away.
Instead he *stepped* — off his own lip, out across the gap that no bridge had ever spanned
— and the instant his weight left the west stone the mirror broke, because for the first
time in three hundred years the two of them were no longer doing the same thing. One had
crossed. One had stayed. The reflection came apart like ice off a thaw, in one long bright
crack she felt in her own back teeth.

*  *  *

They stood together on the east span now, the one who had stayed and the one who had
crossed the gap no carpenter had ever closed, and for the first time in three centuries
they were not mirrored, because one of them had chosen the other. Their joined reach
finished itself. A hand's breadth became nothing at all. They touched.

And the office cracked in both of them at once, the frozen bride and the frozen groom
splitting along the same seam like a single garment coming apart at the shoulder. Three
hundred years of reaching, of nearly, of *no* — all of it flaking away like old paint off
good wood.

Something surfaced under the crack in each of them. Not a wound. Two names, rising the way
a held breath finally lets go, rising *together*, because they had always been going to
arrive in the same breath or not at all.

"...Elsbeth—"

"—and Wystan."

"Elsbeth." She said it twice, tasting the shape of it. "That's mine. I'd forgotten the
sound of my own—"

"Wystan. Say it again. Say them together, the way they were always meant to be said."

"Elsbeth and Wystan," they said, together, and it landed like a vow — the one owed and
never given, spoken three centuries late and not one word less true for the wait. Two
people now, holding hands on a broken bridge in ruined finery, plainly and simply married,
in the only way that had ever counted.

Wystan looked back once across the gap, at the hall he had left and his name's whole
unfinished errand — then looked at Elsbeth, and did not look back again.

Elsbeth turned to the Fool, no office left in her voice at all now, only a person,
mortified and grateful in roughly equal measure.

"Three hundred years," she said, "and it took a stranger with no dog in the fight —" Pip,
at the traverse's end, tilted his head at the word — "no offence to the actual dog — to say
the one word we couldn't manage. You unwelcome, impossible, necessary witness. Thank you.
We are so sorry you had to see us like that. We are so glad you did."

Wystan lifted their joined hands, and there was a card between their two palms, without
any flourish at all.

"Trump VI," he said. "Union, they'll call it. It's the two of us, so it takes the two of
us to hand it over — that's rather the whole point of the thing. Take it. And take this
with it, from people who'd know."

"Reaching is not the same as choosing," they said, together, one last time. "We reached
for three hundred years. It was choosing that let us touch."

*  *  *

Over the stretch it took the canyon to notice what had happened to it — measured, this far
south, in candle-stubs and meals and the ache of a body that has climbed a rope ladder
twice in one span of waking — the bridge did what three generations of Fenns had not
managed. It grew: the two half-spans crept toward each other by nothing she could watch
happening and everything she could measure by leaving and coming back, the gap that had
always been the choice made slowly, patiently physical — and somewhere in a stretch of
rest against Fenn's own scaffold post, the last plank went in with a sound like a lock
turning. She woke to it. She was fairly sure she was the only living soul who had.

Cutwright Fenn stood at the western foot of the new span, chisel still in hand, staring at
a bridge he had spent his entire life failing to build.

"Done overnight," he said, to nobody, to her, to the bridge, to all three at once. "By a
*wedding*. Three generations of us and the same cursed plank, and it finishes itself while
I sleep, on account of two people finally making up their minds." He looked a long moment
across the span, and huffed out something that was almost a laugh. "Well. A finished
bridge is a finished bridge, however it got finished. First Fenn to walk it. That'll do
nicely."

He crossed it — first of his line ever to reach the far side on his own two feet — and at
the rail carved one last word beneath three generations of failed tally-marks: *done.*

Beyond him the two towns were already becoming one thing, and not shared out evenly. The
sea-facing chapel's bell rang for the first time in three hundred years and did not stop,
ringing out the wedding the east had waited its whole existence to host, pilgrims already
turning up the coast road. Across the bridge, the great grey hall stood open on its swept
and shining floor, and would go on standing open forever, its wedding kept somewhere else.
Somewhere in that stone terrace, a family was folding banners into a chest, packing to
cross and live where the town's life had gone.

*  *  *

At the foot of the cliffs the old landing stood empty, traffic going over Pell's head now
instead of through his hands, and the flat-bottomed ferry knocked its post with no one in
it. He sat in it all the same, pole across his knees, watching the crossings go by above.

"There it is, then," he said, when she found him. "A bridge. Grand thing. Everyone
crossing, nobody sinking, everybody pleased." He patted the gunwale, the way you'd pat a
horse too old to be ridden. "And me with the one trade the canyon doesn't need any more,
on account of it having two banks that talk to each other now instead of one man who
carried the talking. Never thought the far bank would come to *me*, mind. Reached right
across and put my boat out of a job."

Then, because he was Pell, he found the dry end of it.

"Still. I knew every soul on both banks by their business for three hundred years. A man
who knows a canyon that well doesn't stop being useful just because it got easier to
cross. There's a toll-house wants keeping on that bridge, I hear. Same gossip, drier feet."
He looked out at the water. "Might take it up. Might just sit and watch a while first.
I've earned a sit."

She sat with him a while on the empty landing and said nothing, and that turned out to be
the correct thing to have done, because he did not ask her to leave.

*  *  *

The finished bridge stood whole for the first time in three hundred years, spanning a gap
that had never really been a matter of stone, and she walked out onto it with Pip ranging
ahead — past the seam where the two half-spans had finally met, over the empty air that
had held a wedding's shape for three centuries — and reached the far bank on her own two
feet, the way exactly one other person ever had, a few days before her, chisel still warm
in his hand.

*First person ever to walk clean across, you know*, said the Querent, warm and a little
sly. *Everyone before you either stopped at the gap or paid Pell to go round it. You just
— chose a side and kept walking.* A pause, fond. *Funny thing about a bridge, little
Excuse. It's only ever finished once somebody decides which shore they're standing on.
The rest is just very hopeful carpentry.*

Behind her the canyon carried its two sounds at once — a bell that would not stop ringing,
and under it, quieter, a hall gone dark and still. One town had got its wedding. One town
had got the bridge. Both had got a person married, and lost a future, and she suspected
neither would ever be entirely sure the trade had been fair. She walked on anyway, because
that, she was beginning to understand, was rather the whole job.

*  *  *

She made the Waystation shrine on the canyon's inland lip before the ache in her legs had
fully caught up with her, and sat on its worn step with Pip warm against her thigh, and
opened her coat to see what the day had left her.

Three cards now. Manifest sat where it always sat, the near pocket over her heart, alive
since the carnival. Bloom rode loose beside it, waiting on a home that had not existed
yet. And between them, for the first time since she had gone through this coat's lining by
firelight and found three empty shapes with no name for what went in them, the middle
pocket had woken.

She felt it before she understood it — a small, private give, like a held breath let out —
and looked down to find the middle seam of her coat lying open, patient, exactly the size
of a card.

*There's your third pocket, little Excuse*, said the Querent, something underneath the
dryness of it. *Three cards make a spread. Nobody tells you that going in. Everybody works
it out the same way you just did.*

She took Bloom from where it had ridden loose against her ribs, and set it into the middle
pocket, and it went in the way the first one had — as though the seam had been cut for it,
because it had.

Nothing rang out. No power came alive in her fingertips the way the reach for a hat had,
that first night. What arrived instead was quieter and stranger: a kind of knowledge she
now simply had, the way she knew her left hand from her right — that the next forage she
turned up on a road like this would come doubled, and that somewhere behind her ribs, very
slowly, in country that had learned to breathe again, the White Rose at her belt had
begun, of its own accord, to grow.

She looked down at it. Three petals still, closed and modest as they had been since the
morning she woke. But there was give in the stem that had not been there yesterday, and
she understood, a beat late, that this was not a thing she would ever reach for and spend.
It was simply, now, a true thing about her, the way her hair was cropped rough or her
boots were creased to her own walking. She wore it. She did not use it.

*Three*, said the Querent, quietly, almost to itself. *Past, present, and — one day, when
you've earned the last of it — whatever's still to come. You're becoming rather a
well-appointed sort of Fool, little Excuse.*

"Is that good?"

*Ask me again in a few more cards.*

She sat a while longer, letting her legs forgive her, and somewhere past the canyon's
inland lip, faint enough to have been a trick of distance, she heard it: a bell. Not the
east's new one, still ringing joy into the coast behind her — a different bell, further
off, striking the same single note over and over, patient and unmistakably wrong, the way
a clock is wrong when it has struck the same hour for three hundred years with no
intention of striking another.

She had been hearing it, she realised, since before the road climbed to the canyon's lip —
under the wagon-noise, under Pell's story, under two names said together on a broken
bridge — ringing on and on, asking nothing, stopping for nothing, utterly unlike the bell
that had just, at long last, been allowed to ring for something.

She got up. Pip was already three steps down the inland road, looking back with the frank
patience of a professional waiting on a slower colleague. She whistled him on, and walked
the finished bridge into memory behind her, and went south toward the bell that would not
stop — because it was there, and because that, she was learning, was the whole method.


---

# Interlude — The Letters That Cross Anyway

*Carried by messenger-bird across the Divide, except where noted.*

*  *  *

**PERPETUA, EAST BANK, TO OSRIC, WEST BANK**

My dear brother-in-honour (I shall go on calling you that; you have never once told me
to stop, in three hundred years, so I take your silence for consent, which is the only
kind of consent a west-bank man ever gives anybody),

Word has come up from the water-stair and I am writing before I have properly finished
being astonished by it. A stranger came. A stranger with a small white dog, if you can
credit it, and stood the pair of them on the broken span, and did the one thing three
hundred years and both our whole grieving towns could not do between us. Said a bank
out loud. Ours, Osric. Our chapel. The bell that has waited so long it forgot it was
allowed to make a sound is to ring, and ring properly, for our girl.

I do not know how to tell you this and be glad in front of you at the same time, so I
am simply going to be glad in the letter and trust you to forgive the letter for it. You
know I have never once forgiven your hall its swept, empty floor without also loving it
a little for staying swept. Write back and tell me you are not too wretched. Then, when
you have finished being noble about it, tell me you are a *little* wretched, because I
shall not believe you otherwise, and I have known you far too long to be lied to gently.

Your Perpetua

*  *  *

**OSRIC, WEST BANK, TO PERPETUA, EAST BANK**

Dear Perpetua,

I read your letter three times before I let myself feel anything about it at all, which
you will recognise as exactly the sort of thing a west-bank man does, so I shan't
apologise for it.

Here is the truth, offered once, so we needn't revisit it: it aches. This hall has been
swept every morning of my whole remembered life toward a wedding that has just, very
definitely, decided to happen somewhere else. I stood in the doorway this afternoon and
looked at the floor and thought, well. That's that, then. Three generations of polish for
nothing.

And here is the rest of the truth, offered in the same breath, because it is also true:
I am glad. He loves her. I have watched that boy love her, uselessly, hopelessly, for as
long as I have been old enough to notice such things, and if the price of his happiness
is that my floor stays a floor and never a dance, I find I can meet the price. Grief and
gladness, it turns out, fit in the same chest without much crowding. I don't know why
nobody warned me.

Tell your girl her uncle-in-honour sends his blessing, and — since you will only ask if
I don't say it first — no, I have not touched a drop, I am simply an old fool with
something in my eye. We will be sending a family across directly the bridge takes
weight; my second cousin's lot, packing already, gone to live where the town is going.
I shall miss having them under this roof more than I expected to.

And before you say it: yes. Your chapel will do a *finer* wedding breakfast than we ever
managed here, because your coast has the fish for it and we have only ever had stone and
stubbornness. I concede the point in advance, so you needn't spend a whole letter making
it.

Osric

*  *  *

**PERPETUA TO OSRIC**

You concede nothing, you old fraud, you have never once conceded anything in your life,
you have simply run out of time to argue before the wedding arrives, which is not the
same thing and you know it. Your hall's game pie is the one dish on either bank I have
genuinely missed the smell of these three hundred years, and I shall say so to no one but
you, so mind you don't repeat it to the whole west terrace, or I shall have to deny it
under oath.

The bridge is finished, Osric. Finished in a single night, they say, the last plank
seating itself like a lock turning while decent people slept. I mean to walk it. I have
never once in my whole long life stood on the same ground as you, did you realise that?
Three hundred years of your handwriting and I could not tell you the colour of your eyes.
I find, now that I could simply walk across and find out, that my nerve has rather gone
out of me. Isn't that a foolish thing, at our age.

Come to the wedding. Come and stand where I can see you do it.

P.

*  *  *

**OSRIC TO PERPETUA — carried by hand, folded small, given to a west-bank family
crossing to their new home, who were asked most particularly not to read it**

I have stood at my own end of the bridge twice today and turned back twice, which is a
poor showing for a man who spent thirty years insisting the crossing was only ever a
matter of a choice being made. Turns out knowing a thing and doing it are not, after
all, the same trade.

I will come. I will stand at the back where the light is bad, in case my face does
something my letters never had to account for. Look for the tallest fool in a coat too
warm for the weather. That will be your correspondent of three hundred years, finally, in
person, and appallingly nervous about it.

O.

*  *  *

**PERPETUA TO OSRIC — written the evening of the wedding, sent by bird out of pure old
habit though the messenger is by now entirely redundant and knows it, and sulks
accordingly**

Osric. Osric, the bell *rang*. I have heard that bell only ever silent, my whole life,
and tonight it rang and rang and did not know how to stop, and neither, I confess, did
I. Our girl walked out under it with the sea behind her doing that show-off thing it
does at a wedding, and I looked across the chapel for the tallest fool in the wrong coat
and there you were, at the back, exactly as promised, and you did not look wretched at
all. You looked like a man who had crossed a bridge.

I have never embraced you. I realised it halfway down the aisle and nearly missed my
own cue for thinking about it. We shall have to remedy that, I think, before either of
us grows too proud to manage it.

Your Perpetua, still, and now also — at last — your neighbour.

*  *  *

**OSRIC TO PERPETUA — the last letter for some while, or so he claims**

My dear Perpetua,

I write this knowing full well I could simply walk it across in the morning, that the
bird sitting on my sill is looking at me with an expression I can only call reproachful,
as though she too has worked out that her trade is finished, and that we are both, bird
and man, rather too old and too fond of our habits to let a perfectly good bridge talk us
out of them.

So: no. I shall not be walking it across in the morning. I shall be writing to you, as I
have written to you for three hundred years, and having it flown, because the flying was
never really the point of the letters and I find I am not ready to discover what was left
over once the flying stops. We embraced at the wedding, briefly, both of us laughing to
cover it, and I have thought of very little else since, which at my age is either a
tender thing or an alarming one, and I have decided not to enquire too closely which.

Keep writing to me, Perpetua. By bird. Slowly, the old way, the way that let two old fools
love each other's towns without either town noticing until it was too late to mind. We
have a bridge now. I find I would rather keep the habit than the shortcut.

Your Osric, across the gap, exactly where he has always been glad to be found.


---

# Chapter Five — The Same Old Song

She heard the Chantry a full day before she saw it.

It came across the fields the way smoke does, thinning and thickening on nothing, four
beats over and over — a low note, a higher one, a held one, a rest — arriving out of the
haze at the edge of hearing long before there was a tower to hang it on. By the second day
of walking it had stopped sounding like distance and started sounding like weather, a
thing the whole sky seemed to be doing rather than a bell.

"That's not stopped," she said, "since yesterday."

*It's not stopped since a good deal longer than yesterday, little Excuse.* The Querent, in
her ear, sounded almost apologetic about it. *You'll want to brace yourself for the
particular flavour of company that keeps.*

She had left the Divide three days back with a wedding ring of laughter still going in her
ears — the east bank's whole green turned out for it, fiddles and a bonfire and Wystan
crossing the new bridge in his good coat, hair combed flat and immediately ruined by the
wind, because there was wind now, real wind, all along that canyon, now that the Lovers
had let each other go and chosen. A new card rode folded in the Bindle that she hadn't
found anywhere to put — Union, she'd taken to calling it in her head — loose, unslotted,
alongside Manifest at her heart and Bloom worn close as a promise, because her small sewn
spread had only ever had the two pockets filled. The White Rose at her belt had gone from
three petals to three petals and stayed there the whole three days, opening by the barest,
grudging fraction with every camp she made on unbound ground — slow, the Querent had
warned her, terribly slow out here where nothing but a Waystation gave it back all at
once — but opening, which was new, and which she checked on more often than she'd admit to
Pip.

The road climbed a low rise, and there below her, sunk into a green fold of hills like a
coin dropped in grass, lay the Chantry.

It rose in tiers the way a choir does, low roofs climbing to a cloister, the cloister
climbing to a great pierced drum of stone that stood over the whole town like a held
breath made architecture — every window in it a throat, every throat singing the same
four beats. Golden light stood behind stained glass the colour of held jewels, ruby and
sea-glass and a deep unmoving amber, and did not flicker, because nothing in the Chantry
had been permitted to flicker in three hundred years.

She came down through the lychgate with Pip trotting a half-step ahead, nose up, and felt
the particular held stillness of a bound place close over her like water: no wind here,
though the fields behind had had plenty; no dust drifting, though the light lay thick
enough with it to have been powder; a banner over the gate hung in a fold that had chosen
its shape once, three centuries ago, and had never once reconsidered it. Grass parted
around her boots and settled behind her, the only motion for a hundred yards in any
direction, and Pip's passage cut a second, smaller wake beside her own.

The hymn was everywhere and came from nowhere. It rose off the flagstones under her boots
and the gravestones worn round as river stones and the gutters and the very stone of the
lychgate arch, so that walking through it felt less like entering a town where people sang
than walking into a town that had, itself, over three hundred years, quietly become the
singing — the hymn rising out of the Chantry rather than being sung by it.

A young woman broke from a procession that never quite arrived anywhere — choir-shift,
sheet music rolled tight in one fist though she plainly had not needed to read it in her
whole life — and crossed to her at something close to a run.

"A new face!" She said it the way other people say *fire*. "Do you hear it the way I do,
I wonder — the hymn, I mean. You'll not have heard it before. Nobody's not-heard-it-before
in three hundred years but you."

"It's very loud," the Fool said, which was true, and also the only thing she could think
to say.

"Isn't it just." The young woman laughed, delighted rather than embarrassed. "I'm Linnet.
Chorister, third row, second voice — that's me, that's the whole of me." She fell into
step without being asked, the way you fall into step with something you've been hoping to
fall into step with. "I've sung this hymn every day I've been alive, which is every day
there's ever been, and I love it. Truly I do."

"But?" said the Fool.

Linnet's stride hitched, very slightly.

"But nothing. There's no *but*. It's perfect. It's the same perfect thing it was
yesterday, and the thousand yesterdays before." She looked down at her own rolled music as
if it might, this once, have a different answer folded into it than the one she'd sung
ten thousand times. "...I should like to hear one new note before I die. Just the one. I
don't even know what it would sound like." She flushed, all at once, badly. "Isn't that a
wicked thing to want."

"It isn't wicked to want it," the Fool said.

Linnet stopped walking entirely.

"...No? You say that so plainly." She turned the sheet music over in her hands as if it
had grown heavier. "Nobody's ever said it plainly. Everyone here would tell me to pray it
down. You just — let me keep it."

She asked her other questions on the walk in — who led the hymn (*the Hierophant, up in
the colosseum, hands on the great organ; you can't reach him, nobody reaches the middle*);
why the song couldn't change (*because it's* right, *and a right thing doesn't change, or
so we're taught, every day, in the hymn that says so*) — and by the time they reached the
nave doors Linnet had tucked her music back under her arm and was walking a half-step
ahead, glancing back to be sure the Fool followed: the first time in three centuries she
had led anyone anywhere.

*Listen to it properly, little Excuse,* said the Querent, low, as they went in. *The whole
town keyed to one note like a nail keyed to one board. You'll not fight your way to the
middle of that. You'll have to learn the tune, and then ruin it. Cheerful work.* A dry
pause. *My favourite sort.*

*  *  *

Inside, the service was always in progress and never in conclusion.

Rows of choristers held a chord that resolved and re-began without a seam between the two,
a congregation sat shoulder to shoulder in the long patience of people who had stopped
waiting for an ending so long ago they'd forgotten one had ever been promised. It should
have felt like a prison. It felt, instead, like being tucked in — which was so very much
worse.

Linnet drew her to a place at the aisle and kept the measure with two fingers tapping soft
against her own palm.

"There — feel it? One, two, three, four, and around." She was not performing this; she was
teaching it, and something in her had gone quietly serious to do so. "What matters for you
isn't the beat, though. It's the *space* between — the off-beat, the little gap where
nobody's singing yet. That gap's the only unguarded moment in the whole Chantry."

Pip sat, and thumped his tail against the flagstones, and — she noticed it a full measure
before she believed it — thumped it *on the gap*, not the note, steady as a second
metronome nobody had asked for.

"Good," Linnet breathed, when the Fool found it too, a held breath let loose sideways into
a rest nobody else in the whole nave had ever thought to use. "*Good.* You've the ear for
it. Most here couldn't find the off-beat with both hands and a lantern — they've been on
the beat so long they think the beat's the only place there is."

An elder chorister nearby — robe gone to a shine at both elbows, eyes shut, face lifted
into the chord like a man warming his hands at a fire — spoke without opening them, in the
easy certainty of long doctrine.

"Do not let the child teach you to hear the gaps, pilgrim." His voice was kind, and that
was the worst part of it. "The gaps are where doubt gets in. The hour holds because we
sing the sound and not the silence. Three hundred years it has held. It will hold three
hundred more."

He believed it entirely. It was the most comforting thing anyone had said to her all week,
and it was a cage with the door painted to look like a window.

"Is he happy?" the Fool asked Linnet, low.

"He's *certain*," Linnet said. "He'd tell you that's the same thing. Some mornings I even
believe him."

She turned back to her music, and the Fool watched her turn it over once more, and thought
of what it had cost her to say *isn't that a wicked thing to want* out loud to a stranger,
and made up her mind about something small and, as it would turn out, not small at all.

"Sing it," she said. "Just the one. I'll listen."

Linnet went very still.

"Now?"

"Now."

For a moment Linnet's mouth opened and shut on nothing, the way a person's does at the top
of a jump they've already committed to. Then she sang — one note, off the hymn entirely,
flat and wobbling and hers, cutting straight across the chord that had not once in three
hundred years admitted a stranger into it.

Nothing fell. No window broke. Down the aisle, an old woman's head turned a fraction and
turned back, and that was the whole of the catastrophe.

Linnet clapped both hands over her own mouth.

"Oh," she said, from behind them. "*Oh.* The sky's still up. The bells didn't stop. I did
a wrong thing and the world just — let me." She lowered her hands slowly, staring at them
as if they belonged to someone braver. "I think I've been more frightened of that one note
than of dying."

Three rows off, the elder chorister's certainty faltered for the length of a breath.

"...The third measure wandered," he murmured, to no one, eyes still shut, deeply troubled.
"It never wanders. I must have miscounted. I have never once miscounted."

*  *  *

Above the nave, the town's music was fed from copper throats — great choir-pipes mounted
along the ridgelines and the bell-towers, each one piping a single held voice up into the
endless hymn. Three of them, the Querent told her, could be reached and turned; turn a pipe
and its voice bent out of true, thinning the harmony that kept the middle of the colosseum
untouchable.

Each was guarded. Not by a soldier exactly — by something built along the same lines as
one, blank-faced, tabard-plain, but wearing for a head a cracked handbell that swung and
tolled with every turn of its neck, ringing the beat back into true if it ever caught her
moving on it.

*Turn the pipe on the gap, not the note,* said the Querent, at the first one, *same as the
child told you. Get greedy and clang about on the beat, and that bell-headed fellow rings
the whole town down on you. Patience, little Excuse. The song's teaching you its own weak
spot; rude not to listen.*

She learned the trick of it on the roof-ridge above the almonry: watch the bell-Blank's
slow patrol, count the four beats in her own chest the way Linnet had taught her, and on
the gap — only ever on the gap — reach with the third hand Manifest had given her, the one
that went out fifteen feet past her fingers and needed no crossing of open tile to get
there, and wrench the pipe's copper throat a half-turn off true. The held note soured. Far
below, faint but unmistakable, the perfect chord wobbled, and a single distant voice
faltered and found its place again a half-beat late.

The second pipe she took at a dead crouch along a gutter with Pip sent ahead to worry at a
bell-Blank's flank on the beat, drawing its slow turn away just long enough; the third from
inside the belfry itself, breath held, boots soundless on a floor she'd swept clear of
loose slate with her sleeve first, because a slipped foot on the beat would have brought
the whole tower down on her in cracked-bell alarm.

*Three down,* said the Querent, as the last pipe's note bent and soured. *Hear that? The
whole town's a hair off true now and hasn't the first idea why. That wobble is every door
you're about to walk through. Go and prove it in the square before you climb to the
middle — no sense learning to swim in the deep end.*

*  *  *

In the square below the colosseum's shadow, something had come loose from the hymn
entirely.

A bell-Blank stood at the centre of it, staggering in slow circles, its cracked handbell
head tolling a half-beat behind everything else — ringing an alarm nobody answered,
because the note it rang was simply *wrong*, and the Chantry had no word ready-made for
wrong. Passers-by drifted around it, uneasy, unable to name what troubled them, the way
you cannot name a held breath until somebody lets it go.

Linnet was already there, drawn like everyone else, but unlike everyone else she knew
exactly what she was looking at.

"That's — that shouldn't be possible." She had gone very pale and very bright at once, the
two things sitting oddly together on one face. "A bell out of true, ringing on and on and
nothing struck down for it." She caught the Fool's sleeve. "Go on. *Please.* Silence it. I
have to see it silenced and the sky stay up. I have to see it with my own eyes or I'll
never believe the middle can break."

She fought it in the square's centre, and it was, in its small way, the most naked fight
she had yet had — no rooftop, no shadows, every soul in the square watching a thing they
did not have words for get quietly undone, on the offbeat, over and over, until the
staggering bell-headed figure simply stopped moving. It did not shatter. It sat down where
it stood, like a marionette with its strings gone slack all at once, and its cracked
handbell rolled off across the flagstones, ringing nothing at all.

The square held its breath. The sky stayed exactly where it had always been. The other
bells tolled on overhead, entirely untroubled, over a crowd that had just watched a piece
of its eternal music switch off — and survive it.

"Nothing ended," Linnet whispered, barely a whisper at all, radiant with it. "Something
*stopped*, and nothing ended. Those aren't the same. Nobody ever told me those weren't the
same."

"Now you tell everyone else," the Fool said.

"...I might." Linnet's eyes had not left the fallen bell. "When the song's free, I might
tell the whole square. Go up, then. Go and stop the biggest bell of the lot."

*  *  *

The colosseum's doors stood open, and had always stood open, because no one who
understood the hymn had ever once thought to walk through them meaning to end it.

Inside was a great round of tiered stone with no audience in it at all, ringing a floor
inlaid with staves like a page of music laid flat, and at the centre of it — raised on a
console grown into the very walls, mitre and worn cope and both hands laid on the
manuals — sat the Hierophant, conducting the eternal hymn without ever once looking up,
because in three hundred years nothing had arrived worth looking up for.

She walked the whole ring. Every strike she tried slid off the held harmony like rain off
slate; nothing landed; nothing so much as marked him.

Then he lifted his head — the way a shepherd counts a flock and finds the number wrong by
one.

"A newcomer." His voice filled the drum without effort, the way water fills a bowl. "On
the staves themselves. You have walked into the middle of the Rite, child, which cannot be
done, and so you have not done it. Stand in the seats. Be sung to. That is what the middle
is *for*."

"The song's already wobbling," she said. "Did you hear?"

"The song does not wobble." It came a fraction too quickly. "The song is the one thing
that has never wobbled. You are new, and the new mishear — that is not a sin, it is merely
a lack, and the hour is patient with lacks. Come. Let it correct you."

He said it with the practised patience of a man who had corrected ten thousand novices and
feared not one of them — over the smallest flicker of something that was not patience at
all, gone before it could properly be named.

Then the wobble she'd bought him, pipe by pipe, roof by roof, opened its first true gap in
his guard, and she stepped into it.

The fight kept the hymn's own time. His attacks came in strict metre, organ-stabs that
struck the floor's inlaid staves like struck strings, a sweeping chord that crossed the
whole drum in a wave she had to duck under on the beat and rise from on the gap; twice
more bell-headed figures rose out of the tiered stone at his call and had to be silenced —
off-beat, always off-beat, exactly as the square had rehearsed her — before their ringing
closed his windows again.

"Back to the seats," he told her, mid-chord, patter worn smooth as a psalm. "It is not a
punishment, child, it is a *place* for you — everyone has a place, that is the whole
comfort of the hour."

"You strike in the gaps," he said, later, breath shorter. "Who taught you the gaps? There
are no gaps. There is only the measure and the measure and the measure."

She struck him on the third beat's rest, exactly as the boarded, syrupy-slow half-second
Manifest's power lent her told her to — the world going obedient and thick the way it had
above a haywain and beneath a striped tent, only now the syrup had a metre to it, four
counts and a rest, and she rode the rest in like a held breath finally let out — and his
playing grew harder, faster, the patience visibly thinning under it like ice going to
water.

"Stop," he said. "*Stop.* You are unpicking three hundred years with a — with a
*tune-deaf child's* trick, you cannot, the hour is not *yours* to—"

He heard himself. The doctrine cracked for the length of one bar, and the fear beneath it
stood plain in his voice for the first time in three centuries.

"...What if the child is right," he said, very quietly, hands faltering on the manuals for
the first time she had seen. "What if there was always a gap. What if I have been singing
over the gap so long because I could not bear to hear what was in it."

She reached the master pipe at the colosseum's heart on that falter — the organ's own
great voice, the one all the others answered to, fourth and last and never once meant to
be touched by anyone but him — and turned it, with both hands and every ounce of Manifest's
borrowed reach, on the final off-beat of the whole broken measure.

The great voice bent. Cracked. Guttered.

And the whole hymn, town-wide, three centuries deep, broke off mid-note.

Silence.

Not a rest. Not a held breath waiting on its resolution. Genuine, total silence — the
first the Chantry had known since the Stall — and somewhere far below, a congregation that
had not heard silence in three hundred years made a sound like an entire town flinching.

*  *  *

The Hierophant's hands lifted off the manuals as though the keys beneath them had gone
suddenly, unbearably cold.

He looked at the office around him — mitre, cope, the whole architecture of certainty —
and it split, top to bottom, with a sound exactly like an organ pipe rupturing: one long
tearing overtone, and then nothing at all.

"There is a gap," he said, wonderingly. "There was always a gap. And in the gap there is—"

He drew breath to name it, and the breath brought something up with it that was not
doctrine's to give: a name surfacing mid-inhale, the way a drowning man's head breaks
water.

"...*Bede.*"

He said it as if he'd caught it before it could slip away again. Tried it a second time,
gentler, to be sure it would hold.

"Bede. That's — that was mine. Before the hour. Before I was a *when* instead of a *who*.
I had a name and I set it down to carry the office, and I never — nobody told me I could
pick it back up."

He stepped down off the console a smaller man than had sat at it, not beaten so much as
*unburdened*, the mitre gone crooked and no longer minded, and looked at her without a
scrap of doctrine left in his face.

"First new note in three hundred years," he said, "and it turns out to be a *name.* Mine.
You gave an old shepherd back the one thing his own hour took off him. That's — that is a
better sermon than I ever preached."

He lifted a card from the folds of his cope. No flourish, no rite — just a man handing over
what was, at long last, simply his to give.

"Trump V. Rite, if anyone writes it down kindly. Blessings, and a circle nothing unwelcome
may cross, and one litany for when your last petal's spent." He set it into her hand and
folded her fingers closed over it. "Take it. A rite's only ever been a kindness people
forgot to stop performing. Perform it better than I did."

She folded it away in the Bindle beside Union, loose, unslotted, because her small sewn
spread still had only the two pockets filled — and felt all the same the small, exact
weight of a truth she would now be able to carry on purpose.

*  *  *

She came down out of the colosseum into a town that had never once needed to know what to
do with quiet, and was finding out — and into a town that had started, ever so slightly,
to *move*. A votive candle sent up a thin curl of smoke that leaned, actually leaned,
sideways, the first uncommitted thing it had done in three hundred years. Wax frozen
halfway down its own candle let go and ran. A banner folded stiff around one corner since
before her grandmother's grandmother gave a single soft snap and hung differently.

For a long moment the bells hung still. Then, tentatively, badly, gloriously, a
bell-ringer's mate somewhere up in the nearest tower tried a peal that was not the old
one: three notes, a fumble, a laugh, and three notes again. The town flinched at it. Then,
one by one, it leaned toward it.

Overhead, the light that had lain nailed to one unmoving instant of afternoon for three
hundred years let go by the smallest possible degree — not the sun; the sun over the wide
Spread would keep its own long counsel for a good while yet, and had no part in this — only
the local, narrow mercy of shadows across the flagstones finally allowed to lengthen a
finger's width, because the one held hour that had owned them was, at last, over.

Linnet stood in the square where the bell-Blank had fallen, sheet music unrolled across
her knee and, for the first time in her life, being written *on* rather than read from.

"Listen to us," she said, laughing, wet-eyed. "We're *terrible*. Nobody's ever been
terrible here before — you have to be allowed to change to be allowed to be terrible, and
we were never allowed either. It's the best sound I've ever heard."

She sang it then, out loud, unafraid — the one wobbling note she'd stolen in the nave grown
up into a whole ragged phrase of her own, nothing like the hymn at all.

"I've had that one saved up since you let me keep it," she said, breathless. "Turns out it
had friends."

"It's a beautiful note," the Fool said.

"It's a *wrong* note, is what it is." Linnet was laughing properly now, both hands over
her own heart as if to keep it in. "And it's *mine*. And those turn out to be the same
wonderful thing."

*  *  *

She found Brother Tolliver alone in the emptied nave, hands lifted to warm at a fire that
had gone out, the fumbling new peal reaching him faintly through the stone and doing
nothing at all to his face.

"I knew the hour," he said, without turning. "Do you understand me — I *knew* it. When to
rise, when to kneel, when to hope and when to let hoping be. Sixty years, and then three
hundred more, and every one of them I knew exactly where I was in the song."

He listened to the peal a while. It was joyful. It was plainly, entirely, not his.

"Listen to that," he said. "Flat as a dropped plate, and off the beat besides. Sixty years
I'd have had a novice scrubbing the crypt for that." A small, helpless, wet laugh got out
of him before he could stop it. "And now it's the sound of everyone being happy, and me
being wrong to mind. There's a joke in there. I was never any good at the jokes."

He wiped his face with the back of a robed wrist, and the certainty did not come back.

"And now there's a new song, and everyone's so *pleased*," he said, "and I don't know when
to kneel any more. What do I pray *by*, if the hour won't tell me? How does a man keep
faith with a clock that's allowed to be wrong?"

She had no answer that would have fit in twelve words or fewer, and did not try to find
one. He did not curse her for it. He was not owed a resolution, and she had none to give
him; he only lifted his hands again to the cold air where the chord used to be, and held
them there, and let her go without another word.

*Not every door opens onto somebody glad you knocked,* said the Querent, quiet in her ear
as she crossed the empty square. *You'll get used to the shape of that. I never quite did,
and I've had three hundred years longer than you to practise.*

*  *  *

She left by the south-western road with a new card warm against her ribs, three petals
still closed at her belt but, she'd have sworn it, a fraction less sulky about staying
that way, and the last of the wrong, wonderful new peal chasing her out past the lychgate
in fits and starts.

Along the way she passed a wedding party assembling on a green that had plainly waited a
very long time to be allowed to — banns finally read to an hour of their own choosing,
somebody's aunt already crying happily into a handkerchief that had clearly seen this duty
before — and, further on, another, and further still a third, because word travels faster
than a bride's nerve, and the whole settled country between here and the Maw seemed to
have decided, all in the same handful of days, that today was finally, actually, allowed
to be the day.

*One shepherd, one name, back where it belongs,* the Querent said, comfortably, somewhere
around the fifth mile. *On to the next stuck card. There's always a next card.* A pause,
warmer than the words strictly needed. *There's always one more.*

Far off across the whole width of the Spread, small and silver and perfectly still, the
Axis sat at the centre of everything, and the tiny turning figure at its heart went on
turning, unhurried, as if it had all the time there ever was.

She did not look at it for very long. Ahead, the land was already climbing toward pale
limestone and a country of teeth-white crags, and somewhere in it, so she'd heard, there
was a woman who had held a lion's mouth shut with her bare hands for three hundred years
and had not once, in all that time, let it bite.

She whistled up the dog, and walked on south-west, into the Maw.


---

# Chapter Six — The Gentlest Hand

The road out of the Chantry climbed for two days before it thought to level off, and
somewhere in the second the hedges gave up, and the grass gave up after them, and what was
left was bare grey stone, scored and pitted, as though something enormous had chewed the
country and spat it back out still standing.

She smelled the Maw before she saw it: woodsmoke, hide-fat, the mineral bite of a
tanning-pit, and beneath all three, faint and constant, an animal that had not been far away
for a very long time. Pip's nose had been up for a mile already, shoulders set a fraction
lower than usual — the posture of a dog revising his opinion of the neighbourhood.

The border camps were hide-frames strung with drying skins and snares coiled on pegs.
Everyone here was built for the taking of something, and everyone moved with the wary economy
of people a little afraid of the thing they took.

High on the far ridge stood a shape her eye kept sliding back to: two figures locked together
and dead still — a kneeling woman, both arms raised, and the great pale head of a lion, jaws
stopped open around nothing. Even at this distance it read wrong the way the dead tree on the
Cliff had read wrong: the one thing on the whole mountain not moving.

*There she is*, said the Querent, mild in her ear. *A whole region that hunts monsters for
its supper, and the one monster none of them will look at straight on is a tired woman
holding a cat. Savour that, little Excuse.*

"Is that what I think it is?"

*Exactly what you think. I'll let it speak for itself. It's rather better at that than I am.*

A man straightened from a snare-line as she came level with the nearest frame — tall,
weathered thin the way rope goes thin, a culling-spear slung easy across his back, watching
her come with a hunter's flat, measuring patience.

"New boots on the border," he said. "Mind the ledges. Mind the cats, too — up here the ground
bites about as often as the beasts do."

"Thank you." She looked past him, up the long grey shoulder of the mountain. "Who is that?"

Something crossed his face too quick to name — not a flinch, more the look of a man touching a
stove out of habit, to check it's still hot.

"You've the look of someone come to see the ridge," he said instead. "Everyone does, once.
Nobody goes up." He put out a broad, scarred hand. "Coz Yarrow. Hides, mostly. Honest work,
and the only work the Maw's got. Mountain doesn't grow crops. Grows teeth."

She shook it. "Someone has to hunt the monsters?"

"Someone has to *cull* the monsters," he said, a whole education folded into the word. "A hunt
you can lose. A cull you finish, or it finishes something of yours later." A glance at the
ridge, quick and involuntary. "Mostly."

She asked who the woman was. Nobody knew her name, he said; nobody had got close enough to
want one badly enough to ask. She'd been holding that lion since before the camps were camps
— three hundred years, jaws open, not a muscle moved. Why won't anyone go up, she asked, and
he told her plainly: because the day somebody climbs up and she *finishes* — lets go, or
tires, or blinks wrong — that's the day the biggest cat on the mountain wakes up hungry and
free, three hundred years of appetite behind it. "We like her exactly where she is. Frozen's
safest. For everybody, her included." Had anyone tried to help her? "Help her *how*? Pull the
lion off? It'd eat the puller and then her. Kindest thing anyone's managed in three hundred
years is to leave her be. That's the Maw's kind of kind."

He hitched the spear and turned up the trail, the matter settled without her having said yes.
"Keep your dog close and your hands off the wildlife. Provoke a cat up a crag on my watch and
I'll not be climbing after you."

Pip fell in at her heel — ears up, unbothered by a country that smelled, to her nose, like a
held breath before a fight. He had, she thought, the look of a dog who'd already read ahead
and knew how the story went. Not the first time she'd thought that about him.

*  *  *

The trail pinched into a single-file ledge, sheer drop on one side, fissured limestone on the
other, and halfway along it a mountain cat was wedged snarling into the only gap through —
lean, ash-grey, hackles up, tail lashing. It wasn't hunting. A fresh rockfall had boxed it
against the cliff, and it had decided she was the reason.

Her hand had already found the Bindle. Every road so far had gone the same way: something
snarls, you hit it, the snarling stops.

*Every road in this world so far has rewarded you for hitting the thing in front of you*,
said the Querent, and something in the voice made her pause with her weight still forward.
*Hold that thought. Now put it down. And back away from this one. Slowly.*

She eased back instead of forward. The flattened ears came up a degree. She gave the gap room
rather than taking it, and the hackles along its spine settled by fractions, the way a kettle
comes off the boil. She held still. It watched her a long moment, weighing something she'd
never know the shape of — and then unstuck itself from the wall and slid past her close enough
to brush her coat, gone up the crags in three long bounds. Not one mark on either of them.

*There*, said the Querent, warmth under the dryness now. *Nothing to it but doing the exact
opposite of everything you know. Remember the feel of that. Sooner than you'd think.*

She walked on, feeling as though something in her had been quietly rearranged.

*  *  *

The shelf beyond opened onto broken ground where a deadfall trap had done its work: a young
bear, one hind leg pinned under a baited log-weight, ribs going too fast, too spent to roar —
just a low, worn-down sound, over and over, at nothing. Two camp-hands stood off, spears
grounded, waiting for it to tire enough to finish safely. Coz Yarrow came up behind her,
unhurried, a man keeping an appointment he'd kept a hundred times before.

"That's a fortnight's hides and a month's meat, that is," he said. "Don't mind the noise.
They quiet down, once they've stopped hoping."

She did not believe, for one moment, that it had stopped hoping at all.

"I want to free it."

"Your business. Trapped fair, and mine to take, but I'll not stop you being a fool about it."
He folded his arms and stood back, and the hands did the same.

She could have driven the log off by main force and had it done in a moment — the bear
bolting half mad the instant the weight lifted. She thought of the cat's hackles settling by
fractions and did the other thing instead: knelt, set her palms flat, and did not shove. She
