let the weight come up slow, timed against the bear's own straining — brace when it strained,
give when it eased — and when a spasm of panic sent it lurching half onto her, she went still
instead of flinching, because flinching would read as one more thing trying to finish it off.
It cost the better part of an hour and every scrap of patience she owned. Her arms were
shaking by the end, the small unglamorous shake of a job done slow on purpose.

The weight came up. The bear did not bolt. It tested the leg, found it whole, gave her one
long look — entirely without gratitude, the plain assessment of a wild thing deciding you are
no danger — and loped off up the scree at an easy pace, hide intact, without a backward
glance.

Coz Yarrow's jaw worked once, twice, before he spoke. "That was mine. Trapped fair. Mine to
take. And you let it walk off like it cost nothing at all."

"It wanted to live," she said.

"They all want to live. That's not a reason up here. That's just the sound they make." He
stood a moment, watching the empty scree, and then something in his shoulders let go — not
agreement, quite, but adjacent to it — and he picked his spear back up. "Gentle's a fine thing
to spend when it's not your winter you're spending it out of." A grudging pause. "Slow work,
that, mind. I'll give you the arms for it. Not many'd have stayed down that long."

He did not follow further. At the shelf's edge, where his ground ended, he stopped and looked
up at the small far shape on the ridge.

"This is as far up as I go. Never had a reason. You'll have your own, I expect. Mind the
shrines. They get stranger the higher you climb."

*  *  *

The trail steepened into a switchback stair lined the whole way with shrines that changed as
she climbed, the way a story changes in the retelling. Low down, neat: tidy cairns, a worn
rosette, a roofed niche with a candle stub gone to wax dust. Higher, rougher — chipped
offering-bowls, spirals scratched with something that hadn't been a proper chisel, and near
the top, pressed into old grey clay and never once labelled, a single handprint, small, its
owner lost.

Nobody up here had left an offering to a god. They'd left it to *her* — the way you leave
something at the foot of a thing too large and too sad to be understood any other way. One
stone read: *For the one who does not tire. — left by a hunter whose hands did.* Another: *I
climbed to ask her to stop. I could not make my legs do it once I saw her face.* A third, by a
heap of stones that had once been called bread: *We do not know your name. We brought bread
anyway.*

She read them all. Pip sniffed each in turn with the thoroughness of an inspector and moved
on, unmoved — the correct response, and not hers.

*Three hundred years of people who walked all this way up to watch a woman suffer and called
it a pilgrimage*, said the Querent, an edge under the dryness now. *Folk are like that.
They'll queue for a wonder and never once offer to carry it.*

She didn't answer. There wasn't one that wouldn't have made it worse.

Near the top, on a ledge just below the tableau, she found the goat: dozing against the
lion's frozen flank, udder heavy, entirely at ease, curled to the great still beast as though
it were the warmest rock on the mountain. It lifted its head, considered her placidly, and
went back to sleep. Pip trotted over and sat beside it, equally serene. Neither offered a
single word of explanation, and by now she had more or less stopped expecting one.

*  *  *

Up close, the stillness resolved into detail no shrine had managed to hold. The lion was
enormous, pale gold gone grey with weather, jaws stopped open around a bite it had never once
finished. And the woman kneeling into it, both arms raised, hands flat against the roof of
that terrible mouth, was *shaking* — the fine constant tremor of a bowstring held past the
point any string should hold.

Her eyes were open. They had been open, the Fool understood with a lurch, the whole climb.

"Closer," said the woman, her voice worn smooth as a step under three hundred years of the
same foot. "Slowly. The paws still know how to move, even if the rest has forgotten." A
tremor, mastered. "Three hundred years, these hands have stayed open. Do you understand what
that costs? No. Nobody who climbs up here does. They come to look at the *holding*. Nobody
ever asks after the hands."

"I'm asking," the Fool said.

Something moved through the woman's face that might, a very long time ago, have been a laugh.
"...So you are. First in a long while."

She knelt near her, close enough now to see the grey dust worked into every crease, the
calluses gone past callus into something like bark. How long had she held it?

"Since before the shrines. Since before the men who left them. Long enough that the holding
stopped being a thing done and started being a thing that simply *is*. There's a difference.
You learn it round about year one hundred."

"Why not just let go?"

"And do what — watch it eat the border camps? The herds? The child who leaves bread that's
gone to dust before it lands? No. Letting go is the one mercy this cannot afford. So the hands
stay open. That is all the hands do."

"Doesn't it hurt?"

"...Everything hurts." Said so plainly it took a moment to land. "That stopped being the point
somewhere in the second century. Gentleness that costs you nothing is only a preference. This
is the other kind. Do not mistake it for peace."

"Couldn't the lion be killed?" she asked at last, hating the question even as it left her.

The tremor sharpened into something unmistakably angry — old, tired, banked to embers and
stirred now against its own will. "No. Never that. It did nothing wrong but be born with
teeth, in a world that froze before it could learn better. It does not deserve an ending. It
deserves *holding*. There is a difference. It is the whole one."

The arms shuddered again, worse this time, mastered more slowly. "There is one thing," she
said, barely above a whisper. "One turn. Three hundred years and no one has ever offered, and
pride is a luxury the hands gave up around the same time as feeling. Hold it for me. Just a
turn. Exactly as it is held now — no tighter, no crueller, and gods help you, do not let go —
and let these hands remember what empty feels like. One turn. That is all that is asked."

She thought of the cat that had only worsened for being struck, and the bear that had only
trusted her for being given time.

"Show me how."

"Come under the arms. Set your hands where these hands are. Feel the shape of it before the
weight comes." Something almost gentle moved under the discipline of her voice. "And then —
do not fight it. Never fight it. Hold it the way you'd hold something you loved that had gone
dangerous."

She set her hands beside the woman's, on the warm dry roof of that vast mouth. For one held
moment four hands shared the weight — and then, slowly, deliberately, with the care of someone
setting down a thing they've been terrified to drop for three hundred years, the woman drew
hers away.

The tableau broke.

*  *  *

Three hundred years of stillness came off the lion in a single vast shudder, and the whole
living, furious weight of it dropped into the Fool's arms.

She had known it would be heavy. She had not known it would be a *fight* — not the clean quick
business of a staff meeting a blank soldier's tabard, but a wrestling match with no edges and
no clock she could see the end of. The lion heaved in surges, jaws snapping at air that had
forgotten, in three hundred years of stillness, that it had ever been let close on anything at
all — and she held, and held, and felt her shoulders begin to burn in the first minute and
simply kept burning after, because there was nothing to do with the burning but carry it.

She understood, within the first surge, that this was the crag and the cull-trap again, only
with the safety stripped away and her own strength on the scale. Grip harder, brace, meet
force with force — and the panic in the great body spiked every time, the jaws coming closer
to something that mattered. So she learned, across one endless afternoon, to do the opposite
of everything her arms wanted: ease when it strained, firm up only when it eased, let go at
the peak of the worst lunges rather than cling through them, meet fury with a stillness she
did not, in her body, remotely feel. Once she held too hard out of plain fear and felt it
seize with a panic that nearly threw her off the ledge. Twice her arms went to water and she
caught the hold again a half-second before the jaws closed on everything that had brought her
here.

Through it all the woman did not leave. She knelt an arm's length off in the churned dust, and
— for the first time in three centuries — her hands were empty, and she plainly did not know
what to do with them. They opened and closed on nothing, reaching halfway toward the lion and
stopping, as if some old discipline forbade her touching what she'd only just set down. It
was, the Fool thought through her own burning shoulders, plainly the worse ordeal of the two.
The holding, at least, had been a task.

"Not the strike," the woman said, low, coaching, never once using the word *I*. "It's the only
thing this truly understands, and it will answer you in kind. Gentleness is not the weak grip.
It is the harder one." Later: "Steady. Let it tire against you, not you against it. Three
hundred years taught these hands that much: you do not win this. You outlast it. Kindly." And
once, quietly, almost to herself: "...The hands don't know what to do, empty. All this while
wanting them free, and now— no." A catch, smoothed over. "Mind the beast. Not the woman."

Not once did the great head turn toward Pip, who sat calm at the tableau's edge through the
thrashing — some old gentleness in the beast, older even than the Stall, that the mountain had
forgotten it still had.

The last surge was the worst: one furious heave, jaws snapping a hand's breadth from her
wrist. She did not grip harder. She wanted to, arms screaming — and instead went utterly
still, the stillest thing on the mountain, and held it through with nothing but patience and
the plain refusal to let go.

The strain went out of the lion all at once, the way a held breath finally, unbearably, lets
go. The great head lowered into her hands — not pinned now, *resting* — and the body folded
into the dust with a groan almost, impossibly, content. The jaws closed. Gently. On nothing.
For the first time in three hundred years.

She knelt there afterwards, her whole body one long ache, and did not move, because moving
felt like it might undo it.

*  *  *

The woman knelt in the settled dust and stared at the sleeping lion as though she had never
once seen it before. Her empty hands lay open in her lap, palm up, waiting for something to
be put back into them.

Around her, at the edges — the office began to crack. Not a wound: the sound old clay makes
coming apart from a face that had been underneath the whole time, the frozen posture and the
terrible patience going all at once.

"It's — held," she said, voice worn through in a different place now. "It's *held*, and the
hands are—" She turned them over, staring. "They're mine. They're mine. When did— how long
since anything was—"

She stopped. Something came up through the crack — not injury, a name, surfacing the way
feeling comes back into a limb that has slept too long: all at once, and it stung.

"...*Maud.*" She said it slowly, tasting the shape of it, the way a man off a long desert road
tastes water and has to stop a moment to believe in it. "Maud. I'm—" the word caught,
unfamiliar after three centuries, and did not quite fit her mouth. "—I'm Maud. That's— gods.
That's *me*. That was me the whole time, under the holding. I'm Maud, and I am so *tired*, and
I am— free. I'm free."

She said *I* three, four times over, tasting each one. She got to her feet a different woman
than the one who'd knelt: the grim, joyless discipline gone off her like the crack off the
office, leaving something older underneath, gentler, finally allowed to be either.

"Three hundred years I asked the mountain," Maud said. "The shrines. The pilgrims. The sky
itself. *Would nobody take a turn.*" She looked at the Fool properly, for the first time
without three centuries of holding between them. "And a fool climbed up and did. Just — did.
Held a lion for a stranger because she asked."

She reached into the crook of her own empty hand, where a card had surfaced the way the name
had, and held it out, both hands, personally, the way you hand over something you've carried
so long you can hardly believe you're allowed to set it down.

"Trump VIII. *Tame*, if it's ever written down proper. Take it — and take the lesson stitched
into it, because it cost me everything to learn it: the gentlest hand is the strongest one.
It's just the tiredest, too. Nobody warns you of that part." A ghost of that almost-laugh
again. "I'm warning you."

The card went into the near pocket of her coat, the *now* pocket over the heart, and settled
as though the pocket had been cut for it.

Maud turned to the sleeping lion. She knelt one last time — no weight in it now, no holding —
and laid her open hand once against the great pale mane, light as a leaf coming to rest. The
ear flicked. It did not wake. She let the hand rest a moment longer than she needed to, and
lifted it, and that was the whole of the goodbye.

"Go on, then," she said. "Both of us. Three hundred years is long enough to hold anything.
Even each other."

*  *  *

Maud walked down off the ridge the ordinary way, on ordinary legs, and did not look back —
already, within a dozen steps, indistinguishable from any woman who had climbed up to see the
tableau and come back down disappointed. The lion woke at its own pace, stretched enormously,
regarded the Fool with the frank incuriosity of a cat that has decided you are furniture, and
ambled off along the crag in no hurry at all, threatening nothing — the clearest picture she'd
seen yet of what freedom looked like for something that had never been political about
wanting it.

The goat got up and trotted off after it. Neither found this remarkable. Pip watched them go
with an expression of such focused longing that she laughed out loud for the first time since
the border camps — one honest, surprised bark of it, arms still aching, a card new in her coat.

*You'd only slow them down*, she told him. He did not look convinced. He did not, however, go.

*  *  *

Near the highest shrine, tucked into a crack in the weathered rock where no pilgrim had ever
thought to look — because none had come to plant anything, only to grieve — she found a wild
white rose growing straight out of bare stone with no soil she could see, small and stubborn
and alone, its petals the same close, modest white as the one at her belt.

She knelt by it a while, working the ache out of her shoulders, and thought that a flower
growing wild on ground given over so completely to holding and grief was its own small
argument against everything the mountain believed about itself. She took the graft the way
she'd learned everything that day — gentle, unhurried, no more force than the stem needed to
give — and bound it against her own with a strip of cord, and waited.

It took. A fourth petal came open against the other three, slow and private, with the same
small sound of somebody letting go of a breath held out of politeness. She thought, without
quite knowing why, of Maud's empty hands not knowing what to do with themselves.

*A good place for one to grow*, said the Querent, quiet, none of the usual performance in it.
*Rather the point of the whole mountain, if you ask me. Though nobody up here would think to.*

*  *  *

She found Coz Yarrow days later, the border quiet in a way it had plainly never been in his
lifetime. He sat on an upturned trap-log with the culling-spear across his knees, turning it
over, looking at it the way you look at a tool for a job that has quietly stopped existing.

"Whole mountain went soft overnight," he said, without looking up. "Beasts won't run, won't
fight, won't do a blessed thing but stand there being *harmless* at you. Nothing left in the
Maw that needs culling. Which means there's nothing left that needs *me*. Not the way I was
needed."

"I'm sorry it cost you this."

"Don't be. Wouldn't take it back if you offered — that's the hell of it. Saw the lion amble
past yesterday with a goat at its heel and couldn't have lifted this spear if you'd paid me.
Can't cull a thing that's just... going about its day."

"You could learn the rescue trade."

A long look, not unkind, not quick to agree. "Coax a bear out of a bog instead of dropping a
log on it. Aye. Maybe. Same eyes. Same read of the ground. Other end of the spear." He didn't
commit, not out loud. "Old dog. New trick. We'll see if the trick takes."

"Was it worth it, do you think?"

"You're asking the wrong man on the wrong day, son. Ask me in a season, when I've worked out
what a hunter is with nothing left to hunt. I'll have an answer by then, or I'll have a new
trade. One of the two."

He stood at last, and did not sling the spear back over his shoulder the way he had every day
she'd known him. He stood it upright in the turf instead — not thrown down, not put away, only
set standing, the way she had once seen a walking-stick stand driven into the turf of a high
meadow, a lifetime ago, as if its owner meant to come straight back for it, and wasn't
entirely sure, this time, that he would.

She crossed the high pass some days later and found him coming the other way — no snares, the
culling-spear still on his back, unslung for days. Below them both, in the flat unmoving
light, the lion ambled along the lower crag with the goat trotting at its flank, close enough
to hear, threatening nothing. Coz Yarrow watched it the whole length of the crag, his hand
never once straying to the spear. He didn't say why. He nodded to her — hunter to fool, one
professional to whatever it was she'd turned out to be — and walked on, and did not explain
himself, and she did not ask him to.

*  *  *

The trail down off the highlands ran long and easy, switchbacking through grey stone gone
green again, and somewhere past the last shrine she stopped, the way she was coming to stop
most days, and looked out.

Below her the whole shape of the Spread lay under its unmoving sky — fields, a black wood,
water catching a flat unchanging light. And ringing the entire visible world, a great pale
road, wider than any she'd walked, worn smooth and white as bone, in one long unbroken circle.
Along it, small at this distance and endless, moved a dust-cloud that did not thin, did not
settle, did not, watching it a long minute, ever once stop.

"What's that?"

*That*, said the Querent, something in the voice she was learning to listen for — not quite a
warning, not quite an invitation — *is the Longroad, little Excuse. That dust doesn't stop
because whatever's making it hasn't stopped marching in three hundred years.* A pause. *You'll
want your boots on properly for that one. Go on. Down you go.*

Far off, past the causeway and past everything else, small and silver and steady, the Axis sat
at the centre of the turning world and glittered, and in the smallest part of it, so far away
it was barely a flaw in the light, something went on turning, and turning, and turning.

She looked at it a while, the way she was coming to. Then she whistled up the dog, and went
down off the mountain, toward the dust that never stopped.


---

# Chapter Seven — The Triumph

The last of the Maw's living wind died against her back the moment her boot found the causeway stone, and after that there was no wind.

She had grown used to the wilds being loud about themselves — gorse hissing on the crags, a lion who now lay in her shadow of an afternoon and let her scratch the scarred place under his chin, because a Trump called Tame rode in her Past pocket, and beasts had stopped minding her the way the Maw, eventually, had. All of that fell away on the downward path, and what came up to meet her instead was a road so wide it looked built for something other than people.

Four wagons abreast, raised on dressed stone, curving away left and right until the curve became a ring, and the ring a rumour of distance with no end she could see. To the inward side, past a low parapet, the land fell away in long green shelves toward the middle of everything, and there — closer than she had ever seen it — sat the Axis. A still, silver eye with no shadow in it, and at its heart a single figure turned, and turned, and went on turning.

*You've been squinting at that thing since the Cliff*, said the Querent, not unkindly. *Get used to it looking back. You're on its doorstep now, more or less. Every road on the Spread comes to this one eventually — it's the world's own spine, the Longroad. Rather appropriate you'd meet it walking on your own two feet, given how much use you've had of them.*

And then there was dust.

Not weather — she had learned better than to call anything on this road weather — but a long low banner of it, kicked up out of sight, moving fast and away. Before she heard it, she felt it: a tramp of feet coming up through the stone into her boots, steady as a heartbeat, and a drum keeping time to a verse worn away and left only the beat.

Pip's ears went up together, which they did for almost nothing. He looked at the dust, then at her, and something in his stance said, quite plainly, *that*.

*  *  *

The toll-fort straddled the road like a man standing with his legs too far apart: a squat gatehouse, a counterweighted arm, a brazier gone to cold ash. Nobody had paid a toll there in longer than anybody could count, and nobody had stopped there either, except the one soldier on the step with his boots off and his helmet upturned in his lap like a beggar's bowl. He looked up at her the way a man looks at rain he doesn't expect to amount to anything.

"New boots," he said. "New face. Neither of them marching. You're the strangest thing I've seen on this road in three centuries, and I've seen the same road three hundred times a year."

"Hello," she said, because it always seemed to work.

"Corporal Pike. Third of the line, or I was, when there was a first and second worth counting." He turned the helmet over in his hands, not looking at it. "You'll be wanting to know about the parade. Everyone new wants to know about the parade. Then they stop being new, and stop asking, and one day they're marching in it and can't remember why either."

"What are you all marching for?"

"A triumph. A great one — the General's triumph, the day the war was won and the Longroad was ours end to end." He said it crisply, out of drill, the way a man recites a thing he has stopped believing in but not stopped saying. Then something went out behind his eyes, the way a lamp goes out when the wick finally gives up. "I'll tell you the honest of it, since you're new enough to hear it. I don't recall which war. I don't recall the winning. I recall the *marching*. Three hundred years, and the marching's the only part that stuck."

She sat down on the step beside him, because there did not seem to be anything smarter to do with her legs just then.

"How do I get aboard?"

"On foot, poor devil. Nothing on this road's faster than the column, and there's no mount in the world yet." He nodded to where another fort's gate-arm stood raised against the sky. "Catch its tail and leap. Off a gate-arm's your best shot, if you've a mind to try something that stupid."

"I've a mind."

That got the smallest thing out of him — not quite a laugh, but its cousin. He cocked his head, listening to something in the stone, and jerked his chin down the road.

"There. Hear it? That's the tail of it, coming round again. If you're catching it, catch it now — it won't be back past here for a lap, and a lap out here is longer than you've been alive."

*  *  *

The dust arrived ahead of the column and then the column arrived inside the dust, and she ran.

She cut the bend the way Pike hadn't said to, because there hadn't been time: down the embankment in three strides mostly falling with intention, over the low toll-chain without breaking stride, taking the inside of the curve where the column had to take the long way round. Pip went ahead of her, low and delighted, born for exactly this, and for a few strides the gap held even, and then — grudgingly — began to close.

*You've crossed the entire Spread on those two feet, little Excuse, and never once complained to me about it — which I've noticed, and appreciated.* The Querent's voice had gone breathless on her behalf, which was new. *Catch this and you'll not have to walk it again. Consider it the world's way of apologising for the mileage.*

The first gate-arm hung out over the road at exactly the wrong height to be sensible and the right height to be tried. She went up it hand over hand and threw herself at a roof already gone. Her hands found the running-board of the next carriage instead and held — held for three scraping yards that took most of the skin off her palms — and then didn't, and she was rolling clear across the road with the column thundering on without her, the specific fury of a near miss burning worse than the graze.

She was up and running for the second fort before the dust of the first attempt had settled.

This time the gate-arm gave her a rigging line instead — a trailing banner-rope, frayed, three centuries weathered, hanging low off a passing wagon like an afterthought. She jumped for rope rather than wood, and it held, cracking taut with a sound like a knuckle popping, and dragged her bodily up onto the roof of the war-train with her arms screaming and her heart somewhere it hadn't been before.

Pip was already there. He'd landed neat as a cat on the roof above, and sat looking back at her with the specific patience of a colleague who has been waiting slightly too long.

The world of solid, stopped ground dropped away behind her, and everything now was moving, and would not stop moving until somebody made it.

*  *  *

Up close, the triumph was a corpse that had forgotten to fall down.

The banners overhead — great regimental colours, once every shade a proud army owns — had bleached to a single uniform bone-white, the devices on them worn to ghosts of themselves. The gilt on the carriages had gone the green of old pennies. A trumpeter stood frozen mid-fanfare on a passing roof, cheeks puffed for a note three hundred years unblown, the bell of his instrument dented too deep to ever sound true again.

And the roofs were held. Parade-armoured Blanks — blank ovals where faces belonged, tabards bearing the column's higher ranks in grey — wheeled and presented arms in perfect drill, inspecting a rank that would never be inspected, on a march with nowhere left to arrive.

*There's your welcome party*, said the Querent, with the particular good cheer it kept for things about to be difficult. *Same as ever, dressed for the occasion. Fell one and its card blows straight off the back of the train, gone into the dust behind you. Nothing ends out here, remember. It only relocates.*

The first rank turned from its phantom inspection and levelled halberds at her, in step, because they had never learned to do anything else.

She fought forward, because forward was the only direction the roofs allowed. Each felled Blank folded at the knees and the waist, slumping the way a puppet slumps when the hand goes out of it, and each card peeled off the falling tabard and tumbled — not up, this time, but *backward*, snatched down the length of the moving train and off the tail into the road behind, already blowing toward some far stretch of causeway to raise a bearer where she was not. She stopped watching them go. There wasn't time, and there was always another rank.

The gaps between carriages had to be leapt outright, the roofs sliding and swaying underfoot with the whole column's gallop. Where a roof was barricaded she went up into the banner rigging instead, hand over hand through bleached canvas gone soft as old sailcloth, and from the top of one long climb the whole procession opened out beneath her at once: carriage after carriage after carriage, and at the very front of it, small with distance and wrapped in his own private cloud of dust, a single figure stood at the reins and did not look back.

*Forward*, said the Querent, mid-fight, easy as breathing. *Always forward — it's the only direction this whole army understands, so borrow it. Don't try to hold ground; there isn't any out here. There's just the next roof. And the man at the front who hasn't turned round in three hundred years.*

*  *  *

The head of the train was a high open platform, more throne than driver's box, and on it stood a man in tarnished ceremonial armour with a laurel gone black on his brow. One gauntleted fist was wrapped white-knuckled around a fistful of reins that ran forward into the dust to a team she could not see. His other hand held a cavalry sabre, loose and expert and entirely unbothered.

He heard her come up the last carriage. He did not turn his head — the reins would not permit it — but he spoke forward, into the wind of his own passage, in a voice built for carrying down a mile of marching men.

"A runner. Good. About time the column got fresh legs — the last recruit's been marching so long he's forgotten his own name, and so, if we're honest, has the man in front of him." The sabre came up, still without a backward glance. "You want a place in this triumph? Earn it. Come forward. Everyone in this column came forward once. It's the coming to a halt that none of us ever managed."

He fought one-handed, sabre only, disciplined and punishing, the rein-fist never loosening by so much as a finger. It was as much a fight against distance as steel — he gave ground along the traces, never toward her, always deeper into the procession's own momentum, so every clean parry that should have ended it only kept the reins where they were.

"Better," he said, mid-exchange, almost admiring. "Much better. But you're fighting the wrong war, runner — you keep striking for a finish, and there is no finish. There's only the road, and the road goes round."

She landed the blow that should have ended it — a full, committed strike, clean through his guard, the kind that had felled Blanks all morning — and it changed nothing. He rocked, recovered, drove forward another stride, sabre already back up, entirely undefeated, because a sabre had never been the thing holding any of this together.

The rose at her belt stirred then, turning of its own accord toward the fist on the reins — the one thing on the whole thundering train that was not, in fact, moving relative to itself.

*You can't cut him loose, little Excuse*, said the Querent, and there was nothing playful left in it now. *Nobody's holding* him *— he's holding the road, and he'll hold it till his arm comes off before he lets it stop on a sword-stroke. There's only one way this parade ever halts, and it's the same way it started. Somebody's hand on the reins.* Take them. *Take them.*

*  *  *

She dropped the Bindle across her back and went for his free hand instead of his blade, and closed her fingers over his on the leather, and the reins came alive under her grip the instant they did.

It was like taking hold of a river — a living, dragging weight, the pull of an entire army's forward want, three centuries deep and still accelerating — and for one long moment she was lifted onto her toes by it, hauled along, losing, boots skating on the platform boards.

Then Pip set his teeth in the hem of her coat and hauled too, all four paws braced, adding an ounce of furious dog to a war of centuries, and she dug her heels in beside his and pulled, and the Charioteer — for the first time in three hundred years — felt the reins go backward in his hand.

"No," he said, and his head came round at last to look at what was stopping him, appalled. "No — not yet — just a little *further*, we're nearly — we have always been nearly there —"

She won it by inches. Only by inches, and only barely, her whole body one long refusal, Pip a small furious anchor at her ankle, until the reins came back into her fist the way a held breath finally lets go.

And three hundred years of momentum began, at last, to die.

It did not stop all at once. There was too much of it, too long a run of it, and the halt went down the train like that same held breath, only slower: the lead carriage's wheels locked and *screamed*, a bright line scored down the causeway stone, and behind it carriage after carriage shuddered and slewed to a stop it had never once practised, the whole column telescoping down into one vast unstoppable stillness.

The dust that had hidden this procession for three centuries came forward, overtook it, and hung — then began, softly, to settle. The banners overhead, deprived all at once of whatever had kept them flying, folded. One after another, down the whole visible mile, they lost their shape and came down over the carriages, bone-white, gentle, like sheets drawn up over furniture in a house being closed for the last time.

The drum stopped. The cadence that had lived in Corporal Pike's own feet from a fort away simply ceased, and the silence it left behind was the loudest thing the Longroad had heard in three hundred years.

The train, at the very end, gave one last long groaning skid, and was still.

She stood in that stillness with the reins slack under her hands and the Charioteer's hands slack under hers, and it did not feel, for the length of one held breath, like a victory at all. It felt like something being laid out.

*  *  *

The office cracked first.

The black laurel, the tarnished armour, the whole hard carriage of *the General* — it came apart the way the halt had come down the train, front to back, and underneath it was only a soldier, old, appallingly tired, looking at his own halted triumph the way a man looks at something he had been certain could not exist.

"It stopped," he said. "By the Deal — it *stopped*. Three hundred years I held it forward because a commander does not halt a triumph —" He stopped himself, the way the train had. "It turns out all it ever wanted was someone to pull the other way."

Something surfaced then, the way a name surfaces — not a wound, only a man coming up for air after a very long time under water. His grip on the reins went loose entirely.

"...*Cassian*." He said it the way a man says a word in a tongue he was born to and hasn't heard aloud in centuries. "That's — yes. That was the name before the rank ate it. Before I was *the General*, before I was *the Charioteer*, before I was three miles of dust going nowhere." He laughed once, nothing like the crisp drill-laugh of an hour before. "Gods, the *sound* of it."

He looked down the length of the halted train — the folded banners, the stilled marchers, a drummer's raised sticks come at last to rest — and the horror of it and the relief of it arrived in him at exactly the same moment.

"Do you understand what you've done? You've let it *arrive*. Three centuries I marched a victory with its destination cut clean out of it — momentum with nowhere left to be, which is the cruellest thing you can do to a man who only ever knew how to advance. And you simply stopped. Turned round. Looked at me." He shook his head slowly. "I'd forgotten a person could."

He unwrapped the reins from his hand — the leather had left three hundred years of itself pressed white into his palm — and where they had been was a card, warm, held out to her.

"Trump VII. Triumph, if anyone ever writes it down properly. Take it — and take this with it: it will answer when you call it, and carry you anywhere you please, at any speed you like." He folded her fingers over the card. "But it only ever learned one hard lesson, same as me. *Stopping is the difficult part.* Mind that, and you'll not end up a General."

*  *  *

Stopped ground was a different country than moving ground had ever been.

Where the dust had circled there was only a long settling haze and a mile of halted train, its marchers standing about in ones and twos, blinking, holding parade-armour with nowhere left to belong to. Some were Blanks, and simply stood, patient as furniture. Some were people — a musician turning a dented instrument over as if seeing it for the first time, a colour-bearer folding a bone-white banner with unpractised care — and those had begun, slowly, to walk off wherever pleased them.

Away up the slope toward the Maw's crags something tawny and enormous lay along a ledge, watching the stilled road the way a landlord watches a tenant finally settle a debt. She lifted a hand to it out of habit more than hope. Its tail went once against the stone, and that seemed answer enough from something that no longer had to answer to anybody.

Ahead, where the ring met a region road, a caravan sat stopped at the edge of the causeway — drivers who had waited three hundred years for a parade to clear a junction, unable quite to believe the road was theirs. The lead wagon eased out onto the stone, wheels loud and ordinary and wonderful in the new quiet, and she knew the painted sides of it before she knew anything else.

"OI!" Flick was hanging half off the tailboard, waving both arms as though she might miss an entire touring show trundling past at eight feet away. "It's HER! Tomkin, it's the *sky-woman*!"

"Seen her," said Old Tomkin comfortably, from the driver's bench, five clubs going round in a lazy figure without once dropping. "Told you the roads'd take us somewhere worth being." He caught all five in one hand, briefly, to touch two fingers to his brow at her, and went straight back to it.

"We're going *everywhere*!" Flick shouted, already turning to shout something else at Tansy Quill in the wagon behind. "Fresh eyes for miles — mind the wheels, sky-woman, and MIND THE WHEELS, PIP—"

Pip did not mind the wheels. He watched the wagon go with the satisfaction of a dog updating a list of acquaintances now travelling the world with him.

*Hear that?* said the Querent, gently. *Wheels, going somewhere, because somebody decided somewhere was a place you were allowed to reach. And every Waystation you've rested at just woke up properly — rest at one now and step from it to any other, quick as drawing a card.* A pause, drier. *You've made the world a great deal smaller. That's a kindness. Mostly.*

*  *  *

A little way round the ring stood a roadside inn, long and low and built wrong, she realised — built to serve a road going *past*, not a road going anywhere. Its whole front was a serving-counter open to the causeway, a hundred tin cups on hooks, a great cold range built to feed a marching band at the same bar of the same tune every evening, forever.

A woman stood behind the counter with a ladle in her hand and nothing in the pot, watching the mile of stopped train the way you watch weather doing something it has never once done before.

"Every evening for three hundred years the band came past at the same bar of the same tune," she said, without being asked, "and I had a hundred cups poured and waiting. They never stopped to drink them. But they came *past*. You could set your grief by it."

"I'm sorry," the Fool said, not sure yet what she was sorry for.

"Don't be. Not yet." Widow Tallow set the ladle down at last. "I built this place around a parade that would never once stop for me. Do you understand how much easier that is than the alternative? You can love a thing that never stops. It never leaves. It never dies. It never gets round to breaking your heart, because it never gets round to *anything*."

"The road's alive now." The Fool said it carefully, because it seemed to want carrying carefully. "That's a good thing."

"It is. It's the best thing." A beat, and then, despite herself, a wet laugh escaped her. "Listen to me — three hundred years pouring cups for men who couldn't drink them, and now the road's free, and my one complaint is nobody warned me the marching band had a *tune*, and I only ever heard the one bar of it, and now I'll never hear how it ended." She wiped her eyes with the back of her wrist, briskly. "Grieving a parade. There's a fool in this exchange, and by the Deal it isn't you."

She waved the Fool off, not unkindly, and turned back to her cold range — and the Fool, going, saw her take one cup down off its hook, slowly, and pour it full, and set it on the counter for whoever the new road brought next.

*  *  *

Back at the near fort, Corporal Pike sat exactly where she had left him — boots off, helmet in his lap — but he was no longer watching the road for dust. There was no dust left to watch for.

"It's quiet," he said, looking at his own bare feet as if seeing them for the first time in three centuries. "I keep waiting for the cadence to come round and put my feet right, and it — doesn't. Caught myself standing up to fall in three times since it stopped. Habit. Just habit, marching a man about with nobody left to give the order."

She sat down beside him again, and didn't tell him what to do with any of it. It didn't seem hers to tell.

He turned the boots over in his hands for a long while, the way a man considers a decision that has finally, after three hundred years, come round to being his.

"No, don't tell me to go," he said at last, though she hadn't. "I've had three centuries of being told where to put my feet, and I find I'd like to be the one who decides the last of it." He looked down the freed road, then back at his cold brazier, and stayed. "Maybe I'll keep the fort a while yet. Sweep it. Greet whoever the new road brings, the way somebody once greeted me and I've plumb forgotten who." He glanced, almost shyly, at the unlit lamp on the fort's own step. "Might be, further down the road, I'd keep something smaller. A lamp. A step swept for tired feet. There's a kind of standing post to that too, I'd wager." He raised a hand toward the stilled train and held it there — not quite a salute, and then, deciding, exactly a salute. "When I march again it'll be because I chose the road. Not because the drum did. That'll do nicely."

*  *  *

She went out onto the open causeway to try the thing Cassian had told her would answer, half certain it wouldn't. She reached for Triumph the way she'd reached for Manifest that first night at the Prestige — not with her hand, with something behind it.

The sound came first: hooves striking causeway stone like a drumroll starting from nowhere. Then the Chariot arrived exactly as absurd and exactly as glorious as three hundred years of somebody's unbroken belief in it — a high gilded thing, laurel-worked, drawn by a team that gleamed like poured metal and cast no shadow, thundering up out of empty road with far more ceremony than any traveller had any right to. It stopped precisely where she stood, its wheels never quite settling into the stone the way an ordinary cart's would.

Pip was up on the platform before she'd got a foot on the step, sitting bolt upright at the rail with the satisfaction of a small dog claiming the finest chariot on the Spread as, essentially, his own idea.

"Oh, sit *down*," she said, laughing before she could stop herself — the first proper laugh the Longroad had had out of her — and took the reins that had cost so much, and let the ridiculous glorious thing carry her as fast as she liked, going nowhere in particular, because for once that was allowed.

*  *  *

She brought it up short — she had learned that part too, now, same as Cassian had — at a wayside shrine off the ring where the causeway met a lesser road: a low stone arch gone soft with ivy, a basin, offerings gone to moss. It could have been the very shrine from the Cliff-meadow's edge, or its cousin twenty times removed; she had stopped being surprised at how alike they all were.

The rose at her belt was already turning toward it before she'd sat down. Then, all down the ring in both directions, small lights began to wake — one after another, unhurried, as if something enormous and long asleep were counting itself awake finger by finger: a lantern here, a wayside flame there, a glow rising off a Waystation two regions distant that she had rested at only once, weeks and a whole different life ago. She felt each one land somewhere behind her ribs as it caught, faint and warm, like a hand laid gently on a shoulder she hadn't known was tired.

*There*, said the Querent, very quietly, for once without a joke standing ready behind it. *The whole network, awake at last. Rest here tonight and it's exactly as though you'd rested at every one of them. Step from any lit lamp to any other, quick as drawing a card. You've walked the whole Spread on your own two aching feet since the Cliff, and I don't think I ever once said how much I noticed it. I noticed it.*

She built a small fire out of habit more than need, because the Rose had already unfurled its fourth petal fully open the moment she sat down, healed and idle, and there was nothing left that evening for the flames to mend. Pip curled against her leg and was asleep before the second stick caught.

She took the cards out of her coat, one at a time, and laid them on her knee in the firelight, the way she had begun, without deciding to, to do at the end of every long day now. Manifest. Bloom. Union. Rite. Tame. Triumph.

Six.

She had never counted them all in a row before, not properly, not with nothing chasing her and nothing left to fight that evening. She looked at them a long while — a showman's warm hand round hers, a garden learning to grow again, a wedding finally crossed, a hymn finally sung true, a lion finally let go of gently, and now this: a parade, mercifully, made to stop.

Six endings, and every one of them, she saw now, laid out plain like a hand she hadn't asked to be dealt, had been put right by *stopping* something. The show had had to end. The garden had had to let its winter come. A whole life on one bank had had to be left behind. The lion had had to be let go of. And now three hundred years of marching, brought to a dead and mournful halt on a road built for nothing else.

She had thought, at the Cliff, that she was here to fix things.

She sat very still with that thought, the way you sit still with a stone in your boot you haven't decided yet whether to stop and remove.

"What's this all for?" she asked, into the fire, not really expecting an answer, which was generally the surest way of getting one from that particular voice.

There was a pause just a fraction too long to be nothing.

*For the world*, said the Querent at last, lightly, in the tone of a man setting a very full cup down very carefully so as not to spill a drop of it. *What else would any of this be for? Go to sleep, little Excuse. You've earned it six times over tonight, and there's a great deal of road left before we're through counting.*

She let it go. I will tell you, since she could not yet, that it was not quite an answer — and that I have never once lied to her. Only, on occasion, set the cup down very carefully.

Far off across the ringed width of the Spread, past the last of the waking lamps, the Axis sat closer than it had ever sat, a still silver eye with no shadow in it, and at the heart of it, patient beyond patience, something small went on turning. She watched it longer than she had let herself before. Then she banked the fire, and put six cards away against her heart, and slept.


---

# Chapter Eight — Set in Stone

The Longroad had never been ridden before. Marched, yes — three centuries of it, one
grinding parade wearing a groove into stone that should, by rights, have worn the
marchers down first. But nobody had simply *ridden* it, for the pleasure of going
somewhere, until the morning she called the Chariot the way the Querent had taught her
— a thought more than a word — and it came up out of the standing air like a held
breath finally let go: a horse and a rig both brand new and, impossibly, the same
weary team that had hauled a triumph in circles for three hundred years, delighted now
to be asked. Her hands found the reins the way they'd found the Bindle on her first
morning alive.

*There we are*, said the Querent, warmly amused, over the drum of hooves. *The Chariot
answers now — anywhere outdoors, any time. You could think yourself between
Waystations and skip all this scenery entirely, if you liked.*

"I don't want to skip it."

*No*, said the Querent, and there was something under the word she didn't have the
road-miles yet to recognise, though I did, and do. *No, I didn't imagine you would.*

Pip rode in the bindle-basket behind her, ears back, tail going, plainly delighted
that everyone else had finally caught up to what he'd always known was coming. Behind
them the last toll-fort dwindled to a smudge, Corporal Pike still watching in its gate
the way a man watches weather he has decided, after all, to like.

She passed a Waystation at midday and reined in just long enough to feel a strange new
thing about it: her White Rose — four petals now, all hers — came back brimming the
instant she dismounted, and the little shrine itself seemed to know there were others
like it strung all down the road, lit lamps aware of other lit lamps, a network waking
to its own shape after three hundred years. She could not have told you how she knew
that. She simply did.

*  *  *

North, the country changed its manners. The soft green haze of the Longroad's verges
gave way to grey — stone walls instead of thorn, fields laid to the geometry of a man
with a rule and a grudge — and the travellers changed with it: fewer stragglers, more
people walking with something in their hands they meant to file. She had grown used,
these last weeks, to being nobody in particular. That had stopped, she noticed,
somewhere back around the Maw.

"You're her, aren't you," said a Coins peddler, drawing his cart alongside at a
crossroads, not quite keeping the wonder out of his ledgerly voice. "The one from the
carnival. And the bridge. I heard tell you stopped a whole procession dead in its
tracks, not a fortnight since." He looked at her the way a man prices something he has
no idea how to charge for. "Where's it stop, is what I want to know. All this —
undoing. Man likes to know where a thing's headed before he commits to liking it."

She had no answer that fit in a sentence, so she gave him none, and rode on with the
question sitting in her chest like a swallowed stone.

*They do ask, sooner or later*, the Querent said, quieter than usual. *I'd tell you it
gets easier to answer. I would be lying, and I did promise you the truth.*

*  *  *

The wall came up out of the grey country like a held argument: dressed granite so
precisely cut the joints looked drawn with a rule rather than laid by hand, every
block shot through with hair-thin seams of exact gold, as though somebody had decided
even the mortar should have manners. A gate the height of six men stood open to the
width of exactly one cart, no wider, ever, on principle. Above it hung a gong the size
of a coach, black bronze gone green at the rim.

It struck as she reached the threshold. Once. Enormous. She felt it less in her ears
than in her back teeth — a single note that seemed to have been waiting for her
specifically, so that it could go through her.

And the city answered it. Every shutter on every street she could see opened at once,
one flat clap of wood on wood. A market awning unrolled itself in a single practised
sweep. A line of clerks stepped off a kerb together, one shared footfall, like a
creature with forty legs. It lasted perhaps two seconds — the whole granite grid
lurching into obedient motion — and then the note died and everything went still
again, holding its next position: a bound place startled into moving all at once, on
command, like a puppet whose strings had never once been allowed to tangle.

"There's no wind here," she said, out of habit now, "and no wind was needed."

*Very good*, said the Querent. *You're learning to read a place before it's had the
chance to explain itself.*

She looked up, expecting the low sun-angle to be throwing long evening shadows down
the streets — and found that it was, precisely, raked to the inch. The sky itself held
the same flat unmoving daylight that hung over every bound region she'd yet walked
through. The shadows believed in an evening the sky had never once agreed to.

*The gong tells the city what hour it is*, the Querent said. *The sky was never
consulted. Somewhere in there is a joke, and I promise you it isn't a kind one.*

A GATE-WARDEN stepped into the gap the instant she reached it — a card of office worn
at his breast like a magistrate's chain, a ledger chained to his belt, and where his
face should have been, the same smooth blank oval she'd learned to expect and never
quite got used to.

"Papers," he said. "Purpose. Duration of stay. In that order, if you'd be so kind —
the form prefers it."

"I only want to see the big fellow."

The blank oval conveyed, somehow, enormous and specific patience. "The big fellow is
His Stillness the Emperor, and one does not *see* him. One is *granted audience*. That
requires a Writ. The Writ requires the Registry." A pause, in which the badge itself
seemed to sigh. "Third bell, second stair, the queue that does not move. Welcome to
the Bastion."

*  *  *

The Registry Hall was the size of a cathedral, given over entirely, without apparent
regret, to queuing. Rope-railed lines folded back across a granite floor worn into
shallow troughs, and at the head of each queue a clerk stamped, stamped, always
exactly on the beat of a smaller gong that marked the seconds the way the great one
marked the hours.

In one queue that had plainly not advanced within living memory stood an old woman
with a petition worn soft as cloth, holding her place with the serenity of somebody
who had long since stopped expecting to reach the front of anything. She nodded to the
Fool as she passed — kindly, unhurried — and did not give up her spot to do it.

At the tallest window sat CLERK ANSELM: grey-sleeved, precise, ink worked into the
second knuckle of both hands as though it had grown there. Beside him a younger clerk
stamped writs to the gong's beat with his eyes half closed, plainly the happiest man in
the hall, safe inside a rhythm he had never once had to invent for himself.

"Next," Anselm said, without looking up. "You're next. You've been next for some time;
the queue simply hadn't caught up to informing you."

"I've come about the Emperor. An audience."

He looked up then, and something in his tired face made room, briefly, for surprise.
"It comes up. It has come up, in fact, once, precisely: a volunteer, the week of the
Stall, whose paperwork I am still, technically, processing. You would be the second
applicant in three hundred years."

"Sounds like a short queue."

"It is the *longest* queue." He said it without heat, the way you'd correct a child's
sum. "The Writ of Audience requires an Edict of Standing, which requires the Emperor's
amendment of the rolls, which the Emperor's law forbids. Do you see the shape of it.
Everyone here sees the shape of it. We are all extremely good at seeing the shape of
it, and doing nothing whatsoever about it."

"Doesn't it wear on you?" she asked, and meant it, and watched something in him go
very still.

"...No one has asked me that in three hundred years." His pen did not move. "It is not
on any form. Yes. Since you ask. It wears. Down to the nib, some days."

"You could stop."

"And be what?" He said it without self-pity, which somehow made it worse. "A clerk who
does not stamp is a man in a chair. The stamp is the part of me the Bastion kept. I'd
not know where to put my hands without it."

He set a single blank writ on the counter, resting two fingers on it without quite
sliding it across.

"Here is the whole of it, and I will deny saying it. To reach him lawfully you need
this writ stamped at four windows — Standing, Cause, Hour, and Seal — and the fourth
will not stamp without the third, and so on, forever. *Or.* The curfew barrier on the
Monument Stair changes guard on a rota, and a person who could read a rota might cross
in the gap without troubling any window at all. I did not tell you that. I am a clerk.
I tell no one anything that isn't stamped."

*The Querent-endorsed path*, murmured the voice in her ear, with real relish. *Straight
up, no papers, the way a Fool's meant to travel.*

She looked past Anselm at the old woman's queue, unmoving, three centuries deep.

"I'll do it your way," she said. "The proper way. Four windows."

Anselm looked at her a long moment with an expression she couldn't name, and then —
carefully, as a man handles something he isn't sure he's still permitted to feel —
almost smiled.

*  *  *

I could tell you the Four Windows took an afternoon. It felt, to her, like a great deal
longer, and I mean that as the highest compliment the Bastion's bureaucracy ever earned
from anyone.

The Standing clerk held the correct stamp and no authority whatsoever to use it. The
Cause clerk held the authority and the wrong stamp. The Hour clerk held both, and could
not proceed until the Seal clerk countersigned — who could not countersign until the
Hour clerk proceeded: two entirely reasonable men, each holding precisely half of what
the other needed, both scrupulously correct, both hopelessly stuck. She carried the
writ between them four times and it came back no wiser than it left, and by the third
round she had begun, despite herself, to laugh — the helpless laugh of watching a
machine built entirely of good manners grind itself to a standstill.

It was on her fourth crossing that the old woman spoke to her, unasked, the way people
do when they've finally decided you are worth the breath.

"I've a word wants correcting on my husband's plaque," she said. "Wrong guild. A
clerk's slip, the week he died — thirty years gone, he is, and the letter's still
wrong, and the law can't touch it." She folded her soft petition along its old crease,
unhurried. "I file it every lawful morning. Not because it'll take. Because the filing's
the only place I still get to put him."

She said it lightly, the way you'd mention weather, and rejoined her queue. The Fool
stood in the aisle with the writ going warm in her hand, and understood, at last, what
the joke in this building cost.

The chain closed, back at Anselm's window: the writ complete but for the one seal the
law forbade granting.

"Standing, Cause, Hour," Anselm said, turning it over. "Three windows in an afternoon.
That has not happened in — that has not happened." He looked at the stamp in his hand,
at the small gong, at her. "And now the Seal, which I have held for three hundred
years and never once been permitted to press, because pressing it grants a standing
the Emperor's law does not allow to be granted. The correct action is to deny you. The
correct action has always been to deny you."

The second-gong struck. He did not stamp on it. He stamped a half-second late —
deliberately, wrongly, the first wrong thing he had done in three hundred years — and
the seal came down crooked on the writ, canted a full ten degrees off true,
unmistakably, gloriously improper.

"There." His voice had gone strange around the edges. "Crooked. Improper. Valid, near
enough, and I shall lose sleep over the *near enough* for the rest of my days. Take it
before I remember who I am."

He slid the writ across. Beside him, the younger clerk had stopped stamping entirely
and was staring at his senior as though the man had caught fire.

*Did you see his hand shake?* the Querent said, once she was clear of the hall. *Three
hundred years steady, and it shook. That's not a flaw, little Excuse. That's the first
honest tremor in this whole marble box. Keep the writ. You earned it the long way.*

*  *  *

The city climbed toward its own centre in tiers, and to follow it she had to climb the
Bastion itself: a stair of civic monuments, each a stern granite figure ten feet tall —
magistrates, lawgivers, founders — arms and shoulders offered as the only holds on the
sheer terraced walls. Scaffolding still laced the higher statues, planks and pins left
exactly where the masons had set them down the week of the Stall. She climbed grip to
grip, ledge to ledge, her arms learning a rhythm they would need again very shortly.

At the top, a broad terrace opened, and from it she saw the plinth for the first time:
a granite cube alone in a square kept scrupulously empty, and seated on it, unmoving,
a shape so large her eye simply refused it — took it for architecture, until a pigeon
landed on one vast knuckle and was dwarfed to a speck.

*There he is*, said the Querent. *Bigger than the rumour, aren't they always. The whole
city built itself around the sitting, like a scar grows around a splinter it's decided
to keep. One checkpoint left. Mind the guards.*

The last terrace before the plinth was sealed by a curfew barrier, and across it
marched blank-faced guards, halberds shouldered, on a patrol exact as music — four
beats out, four beats back, a gap opening and closing at each turn like a door the
clockwork didn't know it was holding.

She presented the writ. The checkpoint captain read it against his rota, found it
valid, crooked seal and all.

"Sealed *crooked*, but sealed. I have no procedure for a crooked seal, or for *any* of
this." The closest thing to alarm his flat voice could manage. "Cross on the third
beat. Do not make me improvise; I am not built for it."

She stood at the mark, counted, and crossed on the third beat, lawfully — and found
the strangest part of walking through an open door was how orderly, how entirely
un-thrilling, it felt to be let through instead of getting through. Even trespass here
had a proper procedure, and she had just followed one.

*  *  *

The empty square, up close, was not a statue of the Emperor. It *was* the Emperor,
granite gone to living stone, no seam anywhere between throne and man. Four Edicts
were carved into him in letters a foot deep — one banded about each wrist, one yoked
across both shoulders, one crowning his brow — cut into the lawgiver himself, unbroken
since the Stall. He had not moved in three centuries, and the city below had mistaken
that for peace.

As she crossed the square, the great head turned — the first movement it had made in
three hundred years, grinding, slow, a sound like a quarry waking up angry at whoever
had woken it.

"Unscheduled," he said, in a voice with no give in it anywhere. "Unwrit. Unheralded.
Or writ, and late. It makes no difference to the seat. The seat does not receive
petitioners. The seat has one office, and the office is to hold. To hold is the whole
of the law, and the law is the whole of the seat."

"I've come to unbind you."

"That word has no filing." Dust sheared from his shoulders as he began, impossibly, to
rise. "There is no Edict permitting release, and what is not permitted does not occur.
You are, at most, an irregularity. A clerical fault, come to the plinth on legs. Faults
are corrected. Stand still and be corrected."

He did not say his own name — there was no *I* in him to hang one from, only the seat,
only the office — and as he came fully upright, forty feet of graven law casting off
three centuries of stillness, the four Edicts along his body brightened in their
carved grooves, as though law itself had just remembered it could bite.

She asked him why he wouldn't stand down. *The seat does not stand down. The seat
stands. That is the verb the seat was cut for.* She told him, because it was true and
she couldn't help herself, that he must be so tired. There was a pause then — a beat
too long, something alive trapped inside it — before he answered that fatigue was not
a lawful condition of the seat, was not on the rolls, and therefore was not felt. He
said it twice. The second time was quieter, and I do not think he noticed that
himself, though she did, and filed it away for later.

"Climb, fault," he said. "Get it over."

*  *  *

The climb was the fight, and the fight had the terrible clean logic of everything else
in the Bastion: he attacked on a rota, and the rota never once surprised her, which
was somehow more frightening than any ambush would have been. She learned to read the
count the way she'd learned to read the Four Windows — climb here, cling there, strike
in the gap his own order left behind — and his voice kept up its own commentary
throughout, decree rather than taunt.

"The first hold is unlawful," he said, as she found one. "The second hold is unlawful.
There is nowhere on the law you are permitted to stand."

The right-wrist Edict cracked under her staff and went dark, crumbling to grit on no
wind at all. The rota did not change — that, she understood now, was the entire point
of him. "One Edict spent," he said, unhurried, almost instructive. "Note that the seat
does not adjust. To learn is to change, and the law forbids the seat to change. It is
the one mercy the Bastion offers: it will never surprise you."

The left wrist broke. Then, higher, harder — up the great chest to the yoke of granite
across his back — the shoulder Edict cracked dark with a sound like a courtroom gavel
the size of a church bell. Three of four. Only the crown remained, impossibly high
above, and the rota still held: perfectly, punishingly regular, the safest terrible
thing in the granite world.

She started the last climb on the count that had worked eleven times running. The
colossus did not move on the count. The great gong below struck the hour, and he held
— through it, one beat, two, breaking his own rhythm for the first time in three
hundred years. Dust froze mid-fall on his shoulders. Somewhere under her feet the
whole city's second-gong stuttered, and a thousand citizens she couldn't see missed a
step in unison, because the thing that kept their time had just, for one impossible
instant, kept none at all.

"...The seat amends the schedule."

He said it the way a man confesses a murder, because it was one — the one law beneath
all the others, the law that forbade all amendment including its own. If *this* could
change, nothing in the Bastion had ever been safe. The floor of the world had just
shown itself to be a lid.

*Just when you'd learned your steps*, murmured the Querent, wry and warm and gone
before it could land too hard. *Cruel, isn't it — teach you the pattern for a whole
fight, then tear it up on the last stair. Present company most decidedly included.
New count, then. Read him like he's brand new. He nearly is.*

She climbed the amended rhythm with her heart going harder than her arms, reached the
crown at last, and struck the final Edict. It split from brow to base with a sound like
a courthouse falling down, and all four Edicts dark now, the law cut clean out of the
lawgiver, the colossus's grip on his own rota failed entire. The vast stone hands, held
rigid on his knees for three hundred years, finally, slowly, lowered.

*  *  *

He sank back onto the cube throne, but it was not the seat sitting any longer. It was
a man, granite sloughing off him in slow plates like a coat grown too heavy to have
worn this long. The office cracked — the throne, the holding, the terrible level voice
— and underneath, surfacing the way a name surfaces when a man is finally, at long
last, allowed to remember he has one:

"There was a — before the seat," he said. "Before the law. A man who *wrote* the law,
which means a man who once believed a thing could be made better by the setting-down
of it. He had a —"

He stopped. The last of the granite fell away from his face, and what was left was
old, and tired, and entirely unperformed.

"...*Aldric*." He said it the way he'd once have entered it on the rolls: formally, a
name pronounced into law. And then, again, the way a man says his own name when there
is no one left to hold it against him: "Aldric. Yes. I wrote the perfect law and then
I could not amend it to let myself go home. Three hundred years, and the last Edict I
ever broke, I broke on *purpose*, to lose to a fault come up the plinth on legs."
Something close to a laugh moved through him. "That is — jurisdictionally speaking —
the finest outcome I could have hoped for. I simply lacked the standing to hope it."

He rose properly this time — a man off a chair, not a seat off its holding — and
looked at her not as an irregularity but as a person, which cost him something, and
which he paid.

"A fault, they'd have called you at the gate," he said. "A clerical error. The one
thing a perfect system cannot survive is a single error it cannot correct — and here
you are, uncorrected to the last. Take this. It was always meant to be *given*, and I
never once had the standing to give it."

He drew a card from the air — no flourish in it at all, a document handed across a
counter — and set it in her hand.

"Decree. Order that *serves*, for once, instead of holds. Fix a fair price. Stand a
ward. Make a thing hold true just long enough to matter, then let it go." He looked at
the card a moment longer than he needed to. "That last part — the *letting go* — is the
clause I never wrote in. Do write it in. It's the only law worth the ink."

*  *  *

The gates opened in earnest over the following hours: I watched the Bastion come
unstrung by degrees, the way a rope comes undone once the one knot holding the coil
finally slips. The great gong struck the hour, and for the first time nobody moved to
it in unison — a shutter here opened early, a queue simply dispersed because someone
in the middle of it had thought of somewhere better to be. The gate that had admitted
exactly one cart's width stood thrown fully open, wide enough now for three abreast.

At a market stall near the terrace steps, something small and catastrophic was under
way: an apple, lifted by a fumbling hand, two guards converging with the awkward
hunger of men handed a task whose shape they had entirely forgotten. She never saw it
clearly — a scuffle, a laugh unsure it was allowed, a figure gone at a run before
anyone decided to give proper chase — and by the time she looked twice it was already
only a story other people were telling each other, badly, with enormous relief. *Some
clot's had off with an apple*, one guard said to another, shaking his head like a man
remembering a taste he'd forgotten he missed. *First crime in three hundred years, and
it's an apple.* Nobody in the whole granite square seemed entirely sorry to see it.

*  *  *

The Registry was half-empty when she went to close the matter with Anselm. Queues that
had stood three centuries had simply dissolved; rope-rails hung slack across troughs
their own waiting had worn into the floor. At his tall window Clerk Anselm sat before
a single sheet — the last writ of the old order — and did not stamp it, because for
the first time in his professional life he could not work out what it *was*.

"I have processed this city's every event for three hundred years," he said, without
looking up. "Births, deaths — well, not deaths, we hadn't any — grants, denials, the
same denials, forever. And I cannot for the life of me determine what to file *this*
under." He turned the sheet toward her. Blank but for a heading: the Emperor's name.
"*Amendment*. That's the drawer it wants. The one drawer the Registry never built,
because the law said we'd never need it. Now everything's an amendment. Every hour.
The ledgers used to *add up*, do you understand — the same in at the top as out at the
bottom, three hundred years, balanced. They'll never balance again. A city with theft
in it is a city whose books don't close." He looked at his own ink-stained hands. "I
built my whole self on books that closed."

She had nothing to offer that would put that right, and did not try to. Some griefs
wanted only to be heard all the way through, not mended.

"Pell asked me this morning whether he might stamp a thing *wrong* on purpose, just to
see. I told him certainly not." The ghost of a smile crossed his face. "Then I did it
myself, yesterday, on your writ. Do as I stamp, not as I say.

"You did it the long way," he added, more gravely. "Four windows, the whole futile
round, and you let old Nan hold her place besides. I'll remember that you queued. It's
a foolish thing to respect. I respect it entirely."

He took up his stamp, looked at the blank amendment sheet a long moment, and — with a
hand that shook exactly as much as it had on her writ — brought it down. Crooked. On
purpose. The first record, in three hundred years, of a thing allowed to change.

"There'll be windows in other cities, all waiting for you to decline their queues.
I'll stay. Someone has to learn what to file it all under. Might as well be the man
who lost the old drawers."

*He'll be all right, that one*, the Querent said, once she was clear of the hall. *Or
he won't, and that'll be all right too — it's allowed to be, now. That's the whole
gift you just handed this city, little Excuse: the right to not be all right, and to
change it after. Costs more than the frozen kind of peace. Worth more, though.*

*  *  *

She rested at the Waystation just outside the thrown-open gate, and it was there, back
against the shrine's old stone and Pip's chin heavy on her boot, that she felt the
third pocket of her coat wake up. It had sat empty and unaccountably patient the whole
journey; now, with Decree in her hand and six other Trumps besides, it opened the way
a held door opens — not a sound, more the sense of a room she'd been walking past
finally letting her in.

*There it is*, said the Querent. *Past is what you carry without asking. Present is
what you spend, and you'll always know when you've spent it. Future doesn't answer to
a button at all — it answers to *fate*. Slot it, forget it, and one day, when its own
hour comes round, it will simply fire, whether you remember putting it there or not.*

She slotted Decree, and felt something settle into place entirely unlike Manifest's
grabbing reach or Triumph's readied gallop — quieter, stranger, not remotely hers to
call on. A law that would keep itself, once, when it was needed, and not one moment
before.

"What does it do?" she asked, half-suspecting the Querent wouldn't tell her plainly.

*You'll know it when it happens. It isn't yours to watch for. It's only yours to have
earned.*

*  *  *

She rode out through the gates near evening-by-the-gong, though the sky above the
Bastion's grey rooftops still held its same flat unmoving daylight, giving nothing
away. Behind her, off its schedule for the first time in three centuries, the great
bronze gong struck an hour that belonged to no hour at all — a single stray note,
unhurried, unashamed, ringing simply because somebody down there had finally rung it
whenever they liked.

Ahead, the granite road ran on, north and east, toward country the Querent had not
troubled to describe yet, under a sky that — for all its stillness — she had begun,
without quite noticing when, to search for stars that weren't there.

*Wheelhouse next*, said the Querent, lightly, into the quiet the gong left behind. *A
whole city built on luck, half of it drowning in it, half of it cursed to the bone,
and neither half allowed to change seats. You'll like it. You'll also hate it, rather
a lot, before you're through.*

She whistled up Pip, who came at a run, plainly of the opinion that granite cities and
colossi and crooked stamps were all, in the end, simply more of the same fine walk,
and swung up onto the Chariot's waiting back. Behind her the gong rang again, off its
beat, glad of it. She did not look back a second time. The road went on being a road —
which, after the Bastion, she had begun to understand was no small thing for it to be
allowed.


---

# Interlude — The Amendment

*From the Registry Hall's standing files, the Bastion. Reproduced in order, with stamps
and marginalia as found.*

*  *  *

**PETITION FOR CORRECTION OF PUBLIC RECORD**
*Filed by: Goodwife Nan Ostler, of the second terrace*
*Concerning: the memorial plaque, east colonnade, honouring the late Hale Ostler*
*Nature of correction sought: one (1) word. The plaque records the deceased's guild as
Wrights. The deceased was, for the whole of his working life, a member in good standing
of the Coopers. The error is understood to be a clerk's slip, made the week of the
Stall, and has stood uncorrected since.*
*Nature of restitution sought: none. Correction only.*

DEFERRED. — *stamp, Registry Hall, first filing*

DEFERRED. — *stamp, refiled*

DEFERRED. — *stamp, refiled*

*[a great many further stamps follow, each identical, each dated by gong-count rather
than day in the old fashion, running the full width of the sheet and down the margin and,
where the margin ran out, onto a second sheet pinned behind the first. The last stamp
before the file breaks off reads, like all the others:]*

DEFERRED.

*  *  *

**AMENDMENT NO. 1**
*Being the first instance, in the whole history of the Bastion's Registry, of a public
record altered from its original filing.*

Petition of Goodwife Nan Ostler (see attached, three hundred and some years of filings,
which this office declines to count precisely, out of what it can only describe as
mercy toward itself) is **GRANTED**.

Let the record show: the deceased Hale Ostler was a Cooper. Let a plate be cut and set
beside the existing plaque, bearing the correction, that both the error and the truth
may stand together in the colonnade, since it is this office's considered view that a
truth arriving late loses nothing by arriving beside the mistake it corrects, rather than
erasing it. The old plaque is not removed. The old plaque was never the enemy. It was
only wrong, for a long time, in a city that had no drawer marked *wrong* to put it in.

We have that drawer now.

— A., Clerk of the Registry

*[The seal beneath the signature is struck crooked — canted a full quarter-turn off the
true, the ink smeared at one corner where the stamp was lifted a half-instant early. A
note, in the same careful hand, is added below it, small, as though the writer expected
to be asked and preferred to have the answer ready:]*

*I am aware the seal sits crooked. I am aware I could strike it again, true, and no one
would think the less of me for it. I find I do not wish to. Every seal I pressed for
three hundred years landed exactly where the law required and corrected nothing at all.
This one is the first that ever did anything worth doing, and I would rather it wore the
mark of the hand that finally let it. Leave it crooked. File this note with the rest.*

*  *  *

**REGISTRY NOTICE — TO ALL CITIZENS OF THE BASTION**
*Re: the return of certain irregularities, so-called "petty crime," previously unknown
to this city within living or recorded memory*

The Registry wishes to advise the public, in the interest of transparency and because
several citizens have written in some alarm demanding to know what is to be *done* about
it, that the Bastion's first theft in three centuries has occurred, and that the matter
has been handled, after a fashion, and that the public may rest as easy as a public can
reasonably be expected to rest under the circumstances.

The item taken was one (1) apple, from a stall on the second terrace, in full view of
its owner, at a time of day with no fewer than nine witnesses. The offender did not flee.
The offender did not, in point of fact, appear to have formed any plan whatsoever beyond
the taking, and upon completing the theft was observed standing quite still, apple in
