hand, with the visible expression of a man who has climbed a stair expecting one further
step and found none. He offered no resistance to the two guards who eventually converged
upon him, largely because neither guard could recall the correct procedure either, there
being no living guard who had ever arrested anyone, and a certain amount of the ensuing
delay is attributable to both parties consulting each other, at length, on what was
meant to happen next.

The Registry records this as **the first offence, by the first offender, in every
possible sense the city has**, and enters his name as Loft, called *Sixpence* on his own
street for reasons no clerk has yet succeeded in establishing. There is some feeling among
the clerks that a man's very first attempt at wrongdoing, made in good faith and appalling
technique, is not quite the same class of record as a seasoned villainy, and the file has
been marked accordingly, in pencil. He is warned.
He is, by several accounts, already regretting the whole undertaking rather more than
the apple was worth. The Registry wishes him, in the driest terms available to it, better
luck, or failing that, less of it.

*  *  *

**AMENDMENT — BLANK FORM**
*Printed this season, in quantity, for the first time in the Registry's history*

To be completed by the petitioner in full. Amendments will be processed in the order
received, subject to the ordinary business of a city that is, the Registry is pleased to
report, no longer required to pretend it has none.

*Printed at the foot of every copy, in the same small careful hand as the seal note
above, a line that was not asked for by any authority and was set into the plate anyway:*

*It could be corrected. So we corrected it.*

*  *  *

Appended to the file, beneath the final blank form, in handwriting that does not match
the clerk's — rounder, older, pressed hard enough that the nib has caught the paper twice
— a line was found some while after the Amendment closed, added by no authority anyone
could name and removed by none:

*Thirty years I have filed that word. It was never really the word I was keeping. It was
somewhere to put him, of a morning, that wasn't just missing him. I don't rightly know
where I'm to put him now it's done. I expect I'll find somewhere. I expect that's the
whole of what everyone in this city is about to have to learn to do.*

*— N. Ostler*


---

# Chapter Nine — Round and Round

The Longroad ran level and pale under an unmoving sky, and for the first stretch of it she rode.

She had not known she could, until she tried it: a thought, half a wish, the shape of reins in an empty hand — and the Chariot answered, the way Triumph had promised it would, an outline of gold dust and old momentum gathering into something with a back broad enough to sit on and a gait like a held breath let out slowly. It did not need to be fed, or thanked, or believed in particularly hard. It simply came when called, all down the long grey ring of road — and away to the south the war-train lay where it had been stopped, a mile of bleached banners folded down over stilled carriages, its marchers gone off wherever pleased them, nothing in front of it any more that it had to almost arrive at.

Pip rode the first half-mile sitting bolt upright between her knees like visiting royalty, then got bored of dignity and ran instead, a small white flag out ahead of her the whole way north-east.

*Seven cards in your coat, little Excuse*, said the Querent, comfortable in her ear. *Seven, and every region behind you that little bit less patient than the one before. Everyone where you're going has been counting rather longer than you have.*

"Counting what?"

*Oh — everything*, said the Querent, lightly, which was not quite an answer and was, I promise you, not a lie either. *Wait till you see the place. It's rather good at counting.*

The skyline told her before the gates did: something vast stood up out of the middle distance, filling half the sky the way a cathedral fills the end of a street, iron-ribbed and spoked in perfect radiating lines — and utterly, wrongly still, a rose window laid flat and stopped mid-turn, as if some enormous hand had reached up and caught it by the rim.

*  *  *

The gutter ran straight down the middle of the street, and on one side of it the world was gold.

Fresh paint, unbroken glass, awnings that still smelled faintly of new dye. A fountain ran wine into a basin already brimming, by the look of the dust caught glittering and motionless on its surface, since before her grandmother's grandmother was a rumour. On the other side of the same gutter — close enough, she thought, testing it, that she could have spat across and hit either kerb — the world was ruin. Sagging roofs. A signboard hanging by its one remaining nail. A puddle that seemed to have arranged itself directly under the one dry step available.

The line between the two did not so much as waver. It had not moved, she understood, looking at it, in three hundred years.

Pip trotted straight down the gutter itself, one white paw in fortune and one in calamity, entirely unbothered by either, the way he was unbothered by most things that troubled people with fewer legs and less certainty.

A man was sweeping it. That was the strange part, more than the gold or the ruin — a lean, grey-templed fellow in a croupier's visor, working a push-broom in long unhurried strokes straight across the divide, lucky side to cursed side and back, as if the gutter were an ordinary thing that collected an ordinary sort of dirt.

He clocked her without surprise, the way a man clocks weather he was already expecting.

"New face," he said, not stopping his stroke. "Don't stand with your feet either side of the line, friend. The House hates a hedged bet. So do my knees."

"Which half's better off?"

"Ask me on a full stomach, I'll say the lucky. Ask me at three in the morning, I'll say the cursed — at least they've got each other. Luck's a lonely thing, guaranteed."

"Why doesn't the Wheel turn?"

He looked up at it then — really looked, the way you look at something you have stopped seeing on purpose. "Same reason the sun won't set two regions over. Somebody stopped playing and called it winning." He set the broom against his hip. "Deuce Halloway. Only croupier who works both districts, which makes me either the fairest man in the Wheelhouse or the maddest, and I've had three centuries to decide which and I still haven't."

"Nobody ever crosses?"

"Born your side, die your side — except nobody here does the dying part either." He said it plainly, the way a man reads out a rule he stopped resenting once resenting it stopped working. "Lucky can't lose. Cursed can't win. And me, I walk it both ways every morning, and I'll tell you the truth I don't tell the paying customers." Something in the visor's shadow went very tired. "It stopped being funny about two hundred and ninety years ago. A joke you can't get out of isn't a joke. It's just the room you're in."

He nodded up at the great stopped rose window of iron filling the sky.

"Turn that, and maybe a cursed man gets one good morning. Maybe a lucky one learns what a bad one costs." He picked the broom back up. "Tunnels are down the cursed side, past the falling signs. But see both halves first, or you won't understand what you're breaking. Gilded Parlour's that way. Mind your head over there. Mind everything, really."

He went back to sweeping, cursed to lucky and back, one long stroke at a time, and did not watch her go.

*  *  *

The Gilded Parlour was a wonder and a horror wearing the same coat.

Chandeliers dripped light onto felt tables green as spring grass that had never once been rained on. A crowd of the eternally fortunate sat about them in the dead-eyed serenity of people who have not felt a stake in three hundred years, winning chips stacked in careless towers nobody troubled to spend, because there was nothing left in that room anyone still wanted. A gentleman in staggering, improbable finery was betting a fortune he plainly did not have on the roulette wheel, and plainly did not need to.

"Everything on black," he announced, to nobody, radiant. "Ha! Black again! Never *not* black, is it just —" a flicker, quickly smoothed over, the smile going back on like a coat, "— isn't it just going to go on forever."

The house dealer at the dice table was a Blank, visor over a smooth blank oval where a face should have gone, a printed Ten of Coins on its tabard. It set the dice before her with a courtesy that had worn all the way through to mechanism, and waited.

She meant to lose. She told herself that plainly, the way you tell yourself a thing you rather suspect won't hold — *I will throw badly, on purpose, and see what happens.* She threw the dice off the edge of the felt where they had no business landing true. They landed true. She called out a number so absurd it was nearly a joke, just to watch the House be forced, for once, to disappoint her.

It did not disappoint her. It never once disappointed her. The dealer raked her impossible winnings across the felt with a small mirthless flourish and set the dice back, waiting for a throw that would also, inevitably, win.

There is a particular kind of dread that comes from wanting to fail at something and being refused even that, and she felt it now, small and cold, under the chandeliers.

*Feel that little sag in your chest, every time you win?* said the Querent, quietly, as the pile grew taller and meant less. *That's the sound of a game with the danger taken clean out of it. Turns out guaranteed fortune is the exact shape of no fortune at all.* A pause, and something in the voice went carefully light, the way a person goes light on purpose over something that is not light at all. *And do you know, someone's had their thumb on these particular dice a good deal longer than anyone in this room. Not mine — I only ask the questions. But the hand holding all of this up, every roll, every step you've taken since the Cliff, has never thrown honest either. Funny old game. Do go easy on it.*

She did not know, then, what to do with that. She would, later. She left the winnings on the felt, as — she understood, looking round the room at all those careless untouched towers — everyone here eventually did, and the Blank pointed a flat dealer's palm at the door, because there was nothing in that gilded room worth carrying out of it.

*  *  *

Across the gutter, the city turned to slapstick made permanent.

A signboard dropped the instant she passed beneath it — she felt the wind of it go by her ear — and a cobble tipped underfoot two steps later with the malicious timing of a thing that had been rehearsing. A ladder that had, she was almost certain, been flush against a wall a moment before was now standing precisely where her shin wanted to go. None of it was deadly. All of it landed, over and over, the exact tireless way bad luck lands on people who have had nothing but.

And yet — this was the part that undid her a little — the cursed-siders were the warmest people she had met since the Cliff. They called warnings a half-second before disaster and caught each other's dropped baskets without looking up, and laughed, honestly, every time the street did its worst, the way you laugh at a joke a difficult old relative tells for the thousandth time because you love the relative more than you mind the joke.

"Mind the bracket, love — no, the *other* — there." An elder woman, up a ladder mending a neighbour's roof that would surely leak again by the week's end, waved her past a sign already leaning to fall. "See, you learn the rhythm of it. Bad luck's only a stranger the once. After that it's just a neighbour with terrible timing, and we've made our peace with worse." She came down the ladder with the easy economy of someone who had done it ten thousand times and expected to do it ten thousand more. "Della Quill."

Against the wall of Della's own leaning house, in a crack between two cobbles that had no business supporting anything, grew a rose bush that had clearly been struck, dropped on, and rained through for longer than the Fool had been alive — and was, against every reasonable expectation, in furious bloom.

"That old thing." Della followed her look and softened, the gallows-humour going somewhere gentler underneath. "Roof's come down on it four times I've counted. It just keeps coming up again, meaner than before. We graft slips off it for anyone who's earned a bit of stubbornness." She snapped a small green shoot off with practised fingers and held it out. "You've earned it, walking both our streets the once and not once looking down your nose at either. Hold your rose out, love."

The Fool unpinned the White Rose from her belt. Della bound the slip against the stem with a twist of red thread, murmuring something half instruction and half habit, and stepped back to watch.

For a moment nothing happened, the way nothing happens with any grafted thing, out of respect. Then the new shoot shivered, and took, and where four petals had been closed and modest at her belt, a fifth unfurled slowly alongside them — smaller than the others, a little ragged at the edge, the exact shape of something that had survived rather than merely grown.

"There." Della nodded, pleased, wiping her hands on her apron. "Cursed-side rose. Doesn't grow *easy*. Grows *anyway*. Suppose you'll want that, where you're headed."

"They say the wheel might turn," said a boy on an upturned crate, ducking a falling shutter without looking up from his whittling. "Won't believe it till a good morning happens to *me*. But it'd be nice. Very nice."

The tunnel mouth waited at the street's dead end: a black iron door set into the base of the Wheel itself, unlocked and unguarded, because in three hundred years nobody in that district had had the luck to reach it, or the curse to want to.

*  *  *

A carriage that cost more than the whole cursed street stood parked, absurdly, at the tunnel mouth, and Baroness Fettle stepped down before the Fool had gone three more paces — jewelled to the throat, poised, and, beneath the poise, more frightened than that charmed face had ever had cause to look.

"There you are." She planted herself squarely between the Fool and the door. "I had my people watch every road in. One does not become the richest woman in a rigged city by leaving things to chance, dear. That's rather the point of me."

"You'd keep the whole city frozen? For you?"

"Don't say it like that." Something flickered, fast, behind the jewels. "I'd keep it frozen because frozen is *safe*, and a fair wheel is the most dangerous thing I can imagine. You've seen the cursed side. You think a real chance is a kindness." She held the Fool's eye, and did not blink. "Dear, a real chance is a thing that can go *wrong*. I have spent three hundred years never once finding out what that feels like, and I should like to keep it that way."

The Fool was quiet a moment, turning that over the way she turned over most things, slowly and all the way round.

"What are you so afraid of?"

Fettle's composure did something complicated and did not fully recover it. "A roll I can't call." Her gloved hand closed, briefly, on nothing. "One honest throw where the wheel owes me nothing at all. I'd sooner be cursed, if you must know — at least the cursed know how their story goes."

She was waiting, the Fool understood then, to be bargained with — for a purse to be named, a price to be found, some familiar shape the moment could be persuaded to take. The Fool had nowhere to spend a coin and no idea how to say so kindly, and so said nothing at all.

"...No." Fettle's laugh had no humour in it whatsoever. "No, of course not. What a horrifying thing you are — a card with nothing at all to lose. There's no thumb heavy enough for those dice."

She did not physically bar the door. She could not; luck had never made her strong, only comfortable, and she stepped aside, and her voice dropped, on the way past, to something almost honest.

"Go on, then. Break the one thing that ever kept me safe." She did not look back. "But when it's spinning and you're gone, remember I *told* you — nobody down here ever asked to find out what a fair chance feels like. We just lost the argument."

The Fool opened the iron door. Cold, still air breathed up out of the dark beneath the Wheel, and Pip went in first, ears forward, entirely undaunted by any of it.

*  *  *

Under the city the noise finally stopped.

The tunnels ran between the great brake-shoes that held the Wheel from below: iron wedges the size of wagons, clamped against the rim, each glowing faintly at the point of contact, as though even standing still cost the machine something. Chains as thick as her waist hung slack that had once, unimaginably, run taut.

A little maintenance shrine sat at the tunnel's midpoint, and the White Rose stirred against her belt as she approached it, the way it always did near a Waystation — five petals now, all of them full, all of them hers. She rested there with Pip's chin on her boot, and slotted her three cards the way her hands already knew: Triumph over her heart, Manifest ready in her palm, Decree waiting for whatever the fight would ask of it.

Ahead, the tunnel opened into the Wheel's true heart: a vast chamber floored in inlaid segments like a roulette wheel laid flat, ringed by a silent gallery of Blank croupiers motionless at their stations. It was the first true quiet the whole Wheelhouse had offered her — the held breath before a throw.

*Listen to that*, said the Querent, hushed. *Nothing. First quiet this town's had since it forgot how to turn. Savour it, little Excuse. You're about to be the one who ends it.*

At the chamber's centre, on a dais that rotated a slow half-inch and caught, straining against its own seized bearing, every gaslamp in the chamber snapped alight at once, and the presiding spirit of the Wheelhouse rose grinning to meet her — bound, theatrical, radiant, and, under the glitter, bone-tired in a way three centuries of showmanship could not quite paint over.

"*Well* now." The voice filled the whole chamber like a game-show host walking out to the biggest crowd of a career that never got a second night. "Round and round and round she'd go — if only somebody would give her a *push!* Three hundred years the House has kept every chair, dealt every hand, called every number, and never once — not *once* — got to see which way a card would fall on its own. And here comes a stray little nothing with a dog, wandered in off the losing streak of the whole world."

"I came to make it fair."

"*Fair!*" The Wheel of Fortune threw her painted head back and laughed, delighted, the way you laugh at the loveliest bad joke of your life. "Oh, that's the loveliest filth anybody's said in this chamber in an age. You think fair and *fun* are on the same side, do you? Everyone does, right up until the odds sit down across the table. But the House has a soft spot — the House cannot *help* a soft spot — for a variable it can't price. So here's the wager, wanderer.

"Knock the shoes off the rim. Every last brake. Do it, and she turns. Fail, and you join the gallery, and the House deals you in *forever*. Place your bets. Let's! *Play!*"

*  *  *

The floor moved under her the instant the fight began.

It ground, and shuddered, and then — smoother, as if remembering how — began to turn: the first true motion in the Wheelhouse in three hundred years, and she fought on a floor rotating beneath her boots while the whole gallery of stationed Blanks came off their marks around her, rakers and dice-callers and croupiers in dealer's visors, dealt into the wager the same as she was.

The weather came with the turning. A wash of gold light swept one segment of the wheeling floor and steadied every hand it touched — hers included, a warmth in her arm that made the Bindle feel weightless and true. A squall of grey struck the segment behind it and fouled the footing of everyone standing there, and it did not care whose footing it fouled: a Blank caught in the bad-luck squall slipped on air that was not there and went down, its card fluttering free to raise a bearer elsewhere in the city; a Blank washed gold a moment later steadied and came on harder than before.

She learned, the way she had learned the falling signs on the cursed street, to watch the segments come round rather than fight where she stood — good weather, strike hard; bad weather, get clear; let the wheel bring the next one to her instead of chasing it. Manifest's conjured hand hauled a croupier clean off its feet and into a squall meant for her; Decree's stored patience let a heavy blow glance off her raised guard when it should, by rights, have put her down.

"Ohh, you're *reading* it!" the Wheel of Fortune called from the shifting dais, delighted past all sense. "Look at that — riding the good, ducking the bad, dancing with a wheel instead of shoving at it! Nobody's played the House like a *partner* in three hundred years! More! MORE! Round we GO!"

*There it is*, said the Querent, easy, over the din. *Stop fighting the luck and start surfing it. Fair and random were never enemies, little Excuse. They're just two hands of the same dealer.*

One by one the brake-shoes cracked and fell, and each time one went the ring turned faster, freer, the whole fight accelerating with it, until the last wedge of black iron split under her staff with a groan that shook three centuries of dust down out of the gallery rafters — and the ring lurched, and turned. Truly turned, smooth and enormous and unstoppable, carrying her and every Blank in the chamber and the Wheel itself round together in one long, giddy revolution.

The Blank crew simply stopped fighting.

One by one, mid-swing, they looked up — blank, visorless faces somehow watching all the same — at the gallery wheeling past overhead, and the fight did not end in defeat. It dissolved into something closer to wonder, croupiers and rakers setting down their tools to feel the floor move beneath them for the first time in lives none of them could properly remember.

*  *  *

At the centre of the turning ring, the Wheel of Fortune stood with her arms flung wide, laughing — and then the office cracked.

It went the way the glitz always went, in the end: gilt flaking off a fairground sign, three centuries of patter coming apart along seams that had been holding far too much for far too long, and underneath was only a person, dizzy and delighted and, for the first time in longer than she could guess, entirely unsure what came next.

"It's — oh. Oh, it's *moving.*" She put a hand to her chest as the ring carried her slowly round. "One does forget. One forgets there was ever a before the stopping, and then it moves and there's a — there's a *name* under all the noise, there's —"

She stopped. Something surfaced the way a coin comes up bright out of the bottom of a fountain — hers, and long, long lost.

"...*Penny.* Penny Farthing." She said it, and laughed at the smallness of it, tasting the daft little music of a penny and a farthing, the least and littlest coins in any purse, attached to the biggest wheel in the world. "Of *course.* I'd forgotten I was ever a person and not just a *verdict in a nice frock.* Oh, that's much better."

She turned to the Fool, and there was nothing bound left in her delight at all.

"Do you know what you *are?* Three centuries the odds ran this town and every soul in it had a number stamped on the day they were dealt — lucky, cursed, rich, ruined, sorted and settled and never once surprising. And then in walks *you.* No number. No odds. Nothing the House could price. The first unpredictable variable in three hundred years, and it never saw you coming — because there was no *coming* to see. Oh, I could kiss you. I shan't. But I could."

She reached into the turning air, no flourish, no patter, just a spin of the wrist — and there the card was.

"Trump the Tenth. *Spin* — one throw off the House's own table, one of eight ways it can land, and you'll learn the whole set if you play it enough. That's the trick nobody ever learned down here: the wheel was always *knowable*. Fair and random, dear, dancing partners the pair of them." She pressed it into the Fool's hand herself, the way you pass dice to the one player at the table you actually like. "Go on. Give the world a good turn on my account."

The Fool opened her coat and let the card find its own place among the others, the way they always did. Her hand came alive with a small bright certainty, like a coin she suddenly knew how to flip and call true — and behind her, all around the wheeling gallery, the Blank crew got slowly to their feet and simply stood, watching the floor go round, in something that was very nearly wonder.

*  *  *

Up top, the whole city had felt it.

The great Wheel filled the sky and it was *turning* now, slow and majestic, and along the split street the impossible was happening in real time: the gutter-line, unmoved for three centuries, was drifting. A cursed shutter that had never once shut properly in living memory swung neatly closed on its hinge, for no reason it had ever been given before. On the lucky side, the wine fountain — for the first time anyone there could recall — ran a little short.

Baroness Fettle stood where her carriage had been, a single die turning over and over in her gloved hand, and for the first time in three hundred years could not tell the Fool what it would show.

"I've fought duels," she said, not quite to her, not quite to anyone. "Ordered ruin on rivals. Stared a mad Arcanum in his stopped-clock eyes and did not blink." She held the die up. Her hand was not quite steady. "And *this* — a die I might *lose.* An ordinary roll that owes me nothing. It's the most frightened I've been since before the world stood still, and it hasn't even landed yet." She looked at the Fool, for the only time. "That's your gift to me, card. You didn't curse me. You did something so much worse. You made me *play.*"

She rolled it, once, against the cobbles — and could not bear to watch it land, and turned away before it settled, and walked back toward a house that would, from that evening on, only ever be as lucky as the next honest morning.

Across the drifting line, the opposite was happening at full, cheerful volume. A signboard stayed *up.* A mended roof, for once, simply did not leak. Somebody won a hand of cards fair and honest and could not stop laughing about it, and the whole street spilled out to meet its own good fortune with the disbelieving joy of people who have waited three hundred years to be allowed to have any.

Della Quill stood in her doorway with a fat, battered book under one arm, watching her neighbours dance in a street that was not, for once, trying to kill any of them.

"Three hundred years I've written down every rotten thing that happened on this street," she said, grinning, opening the book to a clean page, "so we could laugh at it together of an evening. Suppose I'd best start a second column. Good luck's going to need writing down too, now — else who'd believe us."

"The sign stayed UP!" somebody was shouting, somewhere close, ecstatic. "Get everyone, get *everyone* — the sign stayed up!"

Deuce Halloway leaned on his broom at the drifting gutter-line, watching both halves of his street at once, and something at the corner of his weary mouth was doing something it had plainly not attempted in a very long time.

"Well." He set the broom aside altogether. "Two crowds on one street, and I can't tell you which of 'em's the lucky one tonight. Reckon I'll leave the line unswept a while. See where it wanders." He looked at her properly, and there was something almost young in it. "Been a long time since I didn't know how a thing was going to land."

*  *  *

She thought about that, walking out with a fifth rose petal at her belt and an eighth card in her coat, and about the House dealer who had wanted nothing but for the wanting to stop, and about a woman rolling a die she could not bear to watch. Every region left one of these behind now — Ferridge on his empty green, a stallholder's debt of honour, a bear made gentle, a baroness who would never again be certain of anything — and she had begun, without quite meaning to, to keep something like a tally.

*Don't do the arithmetic tonight*, said the Querent, and there was less of the dry showman in it than usual. *Do it tomorrow. Or don't do it at all. There's rarely a good hour for that particular sum.*

The road west ran level and quiet, the halted train a pale folded seam along the southern edge of everything behind her, and far off on the horizon a forest stood up strange against the unmoving sky: a long dark treeline, every leaf on it hanging the wrong way, downward from the underside of every bough, as if the whole wood had been picked up once and set back down inside out.

The Axis glittered at the world's edge, small and silver and patient, and did not stop turning while she watched it, which she still, after eight cards, had not entirely got used to.

She whistled up the dog, who came at a trot with the self-satisfaction of an animal who considers a job well and thoroughly finished, and walked on west, toward the wood that hung upside down, and did not look back at the Wheel even once — though behind her, all the way to the horizon, she could still hear it turning.


---

# Chapter Ten — A Change of Perspective

She crossed from the Wheelhouse's grinning, unlucky streets onto a forest road that went from ordinary to wrong in the space of about nine trees, and she noticed the ninth one because a leaf, brown and curled and entirely unremarkable, drifted past her knee going the wrong direction. Not blown. Not carried. It simply rose, unhurried, and settled on the underside of a branch with a small private sound, and lay there.

She stopped and looked at it a while.

*Yes*, said the Querent, before she could ask. *That's the wood, that is. Not a trick of the light. Have your look. I'd be disappointed if you didn't.*

Further in, a whole glade hung the wrong way about — roots combing at a sky that shouldn't have been there, a stream running along a ceiling of moss without spilling a drop, a lost boot resting toe-up on nothing at all. She stood at the edge of it with the Bindle already up, because eleven regions had taught her hands that reflex before her head got a say.

*Staff up already*, said the Querent, dry and fond. *I did wonder if you'd do that. You've had a run of places that wanted hitting, I'll grant you — but do have a proper look round first, little Excuse. Nobody in this wood is going to make you use that thing.*

She let the Bindle down slowly, the way you let down a hand you've been holding over a flame — not sure yet the flame has gone.

Nothing came out of the trees to punish her for it. A squirrel went by along the underside of a bough at a brisk, unbothered trot, entirely unconcerned that its floor and ceiling had swapped places three hundred years back. Pip watched it go, faintly scandalised, and then sat, and kept watching.

She looped the staff back onto the Bindle. It felt like taking off a coat she hadn't known she was wearing.

*  *  *

A man came down a tree trunk toward her — not climbed down, came *down*, walking, boots finding the bark the way boots find a path, two good coils of rope over each shoulder and the long-settled ease of someone who had stopped noticing which way was up.

"Mind your step," he said, arriving at what was, for him, apparently the ground, "and I mean that in every direction at once, out here. Watch me, not your feet." He looked her over — fresh boots, a rose gone soft-pink at the belt, a small white dog deciding whether a stranger merited standing up for. "You'll want a guide. This wood eats travellers who trust their own eyes.

"Bracken Loft," he said, offering a hand. "I read the wood for folk who can't. Been at it years — longer than anyone living, near enough. Up's a suggestion in the Gallowwood. Down's a hobby."

"Which way is the man on the tree?" she asked.

"Straight in, and a good long way up. Or down. You'll see why that's a joke shortly." He started walking, and she and Pip fell in either side of him. "He's the reason the whole wood hangs like this. Three hundred years, happy as a cat in a sunbeam, and the trees just — went along with him. I'll take you as far as the hollows. After that it's you and him, and no rope I own is any use for *that*."

"Is he dangerous?"

"Him? Gods, no." Bracken Loft laughed, short and genuine. "Dangerous is a word for things that want something off you. He doesn't want a thing in this world. That's rather the trouble with him."

"Why does it hang this way?"

"Because *he* does, and the wood's polite." He said it with the fondness of a man complaining about someone he loved. "Enlightened, the pilgrims call it. I call it a man who got so comfortable the ground gave up arguing."

"I came to set him free," she said. Twelve words, and she meant every one of them.

Bracken Loft stopped walking, looked up the wood, and back at her.

"Aye," he said. "I expect you did." A careful pause. "Just — see him first, before you decide it's a mercy. Not everything up here's a prison."

He pulled a rope taut between two boughs, tested its lean, and nodded himself further into the wood.

"Come on then," he said, over his shoulder. "First thing this wood teaches you, it teaches by making you let go of a branch you'd very much rather hold. Everything after's the same lesson, louder."

*  *  *

The trail pinched out over a shallow dell, and on the far side it simply resumed — except there was no way across except *under*. A single low bough reached over the gap, and the only footing offered was its underside, and the only way onto it was to hang.

Bracken Loft did it without ceremony: reached up, took the branch, swung his feet loose, and hung there upside down as easily as another man leans on a fence, pointing with his chin at the handholds waiting along the bark beneath.

She reached up. Her hand closed on the branch, and for one whole heartbeat every year of instinct she did not know she had told her, quite firmly, that this was a terrible idea — that letting go of solid ground had never once gone anywhere good.

*You felt that, didn't you*, said the Querent, quiet in her ear, the dry gone soft at the edges. *That little argument your hands had with you before they opened. Hold onto it. You've done this once before — off a rather taller edge, first morning I knew you. Turns out it's the whole trick of this place, too.*

She thought of the cloud-floor and the long gold light of a first evening, and a small white dog going off the edge of the only world she had without one flicker of hesitation. She had not known, that first time, that letting go was a thing you could get *better* at.

She hung there a moment, arms taking the weight, and let herself notice — really notice — that this was the same shape as the Cliff, folded smaller and made safe: the same held breath, the same decision arriving in her hands before her head had finished making it.

The narrator will let her have that. It is not every day a person gets to feel a rhyme land in her own body.

At the branch's midpoint she released, dropped a few foolish safe feet, and landed with her knees soft the way falling had taught them, feeling entirely, ridiculously fine.

"There," said Bracken Loft, dropping easily beside her. "Not so bad, letting go, when the wood's the one that's upside down and not you. Keep that feeling handy. The hollows ahead don't ask so nicely."

*  *  *

The glade past the dell was inverted wholesale — grass hanging overhead like a green ceiling, a brook running along it without spilling a drop, and slung beneath the whole upturned floor a canopy bridge of planks and rope, plainly built to be walked *across*, which now meant walking it from below, boots to the boards and her head pointed at the true ground far, far above.

A carved post stood at the entrance, spiralled in a whorl she hadn't the eye to read yet, and the moment she crossed its line the sky went where the ground had been, gently, honestly, warned by the post but not softened by it.

She put her boots to the boards. Walking with her feet toward the sky felt, for the first four steps, exactly like drowning standing up. By the eighth step it felt like nothing at all, which frightened her rather more.

Pip padded along beside her on the underside of the bridge, tail going, entirely undisturbed, having required no instruction whatsoever in a discipline that had cost her four days on the Prestige's high wire.

A rope-checker hanging comfortably at her work called across without looking up: "Mind the west line, it's due a re-knot." A pilgrim hanging near the path, upside down and radiant, added: "Three hundred years he's hung there, serene as sunrise. There's a lesson in it. There has to be a lesson in it." She did not stop to argue. She was too busy discovering that wrong-way-up, held long enough, stops meaning very much at all.

*  *  *

The hollow past the glade was where the wood's confusion peaked — ground and not-ground swapping so fast the eye simply gave up choosing a side — and halfway down it a Coins-suit peddler was stuck fast to a root jutting, at that particular moment, from what counted as the ceiling. Pack still on his back. Knuckles white.

"Ah," said Bracken Loft, not unkindly. "There's always one. Gets turned around, freezes, decides the whole world's gone mad instead of just the one wood. Go on — talk them down. Or up. You'll work it out."

She climbed across to him.

"Don't you tell me to let go," he said. "I let go, I fall *up*, and I have been assured by three separate pilgrims that up, in this wood, is a very long way indeed."

"Let go," she said. "I'll catch you."

He stared at her. "You — will?" A beat, taking her in: a stranger with a dog and a stick. "...That's the most honest offer I've had all century. Right. Right. Here I—"

He let go, dropped the right way — of course he did, the ground had been right the whole while — and landed soft with the stunned, delighted face of a man who had braced for the worst fall of his life and got a two-foot hop for his trouble. He sat down hard and laughed once, and did not get up for a moment, and she let him have it.

"Right the whole time," he said, to nobody. "The ground was right the whole blessed time and I hung there like a bat." He looked up at her, sincere now. "Thank you. Genuinely. I'd have clung to that root till the world ended."

*There now*, said the Querent, soft, only for her. *That's the honest work, that is — not the letting go. The catching. Anyone can fall. Precious few will stand under it.*

*  *  *

The wood opened, at last, onto the World-Tree, and every idea she had been quietly building about up and down came apart at once and did not bother reassembling.

It was vast past sense — roots and crown so indistinguishable that the eye simply gave up hunting for a floor and let the whole tree be everywhere at once. On its greatest bough hung the Hanged Man: ankle crossed over knee, arms loose, coat pooling toward a sky that might as easily have been a floor, a man three centuries into a very good hang and radiating a stillness that made the wood's whole inverted grammar look, suddenly, like the smallest part of the strangeness here.

At the tree's foot — or its crown; she could not have told you which — a threadbare pilgrim sat camped among notebooks fat with almost-teachings, conducting an entirely one-sided conversation upward.

"—and so I put it to you again, master," he was saying, pen poised, "that the suspension of the self *is* the—" The Hanged Man made a small, contented sound, the noise a man makes settling deeper into a good chair. "—yes. *Yes.* Write that down. That was nearly the whole of it."

He did not look up as she passed. The narrator will not tell you whether Fenwick Sorrel ever found what he was listening for. Some searches are not the novel's to finish.

Bracken Loft stopped at the clearing's edge.

"Far as I go," he said, looking up at the hanging figure. "My rope's no good up there, and neither am I. Go careful with him. He's the happiest thing I ever met, and I've never once been able to decide if that's a comfort or the saddest thing in this whole crooked wood."

The Hanged Man turned his head — unhurried, upside down, entirely unsurprised — and found her with a look of plain, uncomplicated affection, as though he had been expecting exactly this visitor for a long and pleasant while.

"Oh, *there* you are," he said. "The one who belongs nowhere. I wondered when you'd get round to me. No hurry — there's never any hurry — but I did wonder."

"You know what I've come to do," she said.

"Mm. Turn me loose. Cut me down, more or less." He smiled, and it reached everything about him. "The others made a fight of it, I should think. Poor loves.

"I shan't fight you. I couldn't, and I wouldn't, and there'd be no point — you can't beat a man who's already exactly where he wants to be. But you *can* join me. Come up. Hang a while. Do the thing I've done for three hundred years, and then — then ask me if I'll come down."

"That's the whole trial? Hanging?"

"That's the whole trial. Hardest thing in the wood and the easiest, both at once. Letting go, and *staying* let go, all the way round and back to me." He tipped his head, delighted. "Nobody's ever done the full circuit. I'm rather excited to watch."

*  *  *

She left the Bindle slung and unused, and reached up for the great bough beside him.

The same motion as the low bough, the same argument in her hands, quieter now, half-remembered rather than fresh — and she hung there beside the Hanged Man, high above a ground indistinguishable from a sky, and felt her body slowly stop insisting there was anything to be afraid of.

"There," he said, easy, keeping pace beside her. "Feel your hands wanting to close? That's not fear, whatever they told you. That's just a body that never learned the difference between falling and flying. Neither did I, at your age. You unlearn it. You've all the time there is."

Then the circuit began, and this stretch of it remains, to my mind, the strangest and the gentlest of her whole journey.

She went hand over hand along boughs that hung the wrong way and stopped, within a few minutes, noticing that they did. She crossed a glade where the world honestly inverted around her, ground and sky trading places between two carved whorl-posts, her stomach only murmuring now rather than protesting outright. She walked canopy bridges from beneath a second time and a third, each one a little less like drowning and a little more like simply walking, because that, it turned out, was all it had ever been.

There were releases too — small deliberate ones, timed to swings, each a fresh little letting-go over a drop the wood kept promising was not the kind that hurt. She began, without quite deciding to, to let go early. On purpose. Before the swing had even finished asking her to.

The Hanged Man, hanging some way off and watching, called across the gulf with a whoop of a laugh.

"Ha! You *let go* early there. On purpose. Oh, that's lovely — most people fight the whole way and arrive furious. You're arriving *curious*. That's much the better way to arrive at anything."

Somewhere in the middle of the third or fourth glade, hand finding hold after hold without watching them, she stopped being able to tell you which way was which, and found this did not frighten her in the slightest. The world had simplified itself down to the one thing that mattered: the next hold, and nothing underneath it that could not be trusted.

She has told me, since, that it was the nearest thing to peace she found in the whole journey. I believe her. I was there, in her ear, the whole time, and I have wondered since whether that was the one stretch in which I, too, very nearly forgot to be afraid.

The circuit curved, by its own inverted logic, all the way round — and brought her back to the great bough where it had begun, hanging once more beside him, level, the whole crooked wood spread out below-and-above them, gold and still and entirely hers to have crossed.

*  *  *

"Well," said the Hanged Man, a slow, pleased breath in it, the sound of a man arriving somewhere he has waited a very long time to arrive. "You did the full circuit. Nobody's ever done the full circuit. So. Just the one question, then — the only one I've got, and I've had three hundred years to whittle it down to the true one.

"Comfortable?"

I have listened to her answer a great many questions across this whole long Reading, and heard her reach, almost every time, for the earnest one, the honest one, the one that cost her something to say. I want it on the record, plainly, that this is the single time in the whole journey she did not — the one deliberately foolish thing she ever told anybody, hanging upside-down to a man who had waited three centuries for exactly this moment. I would not trade it for any of the earnest ones.

"No," she said. "My arms fell asleep an hour ago."

The Hanged Man's whole face came apart with delight.

"*HA!*" It came out of him like something long corked and suddenly opened — a full, undignified, glorious laugh, upside down, three centuries in the making. "No! No, of course not, nobody is, nobody has *EVER* been, that's the secret nobody up here will say out loud!" He was laughing so hard he swung a little on the bough, and Pip, watching from below, gave one single wag of solidarity.

"You're the first honest soul to hang beside me in three hundred years," he said, wiping at his eyes with the back of a hand that had never once had reason to wipe them. "Bless you. Bless your dead arms. *That's* the letting go — not the comfort. The honesty.

"Either way, friend. *Either way.* You could have said anything and I'd have handed it over glad — that was always going to be the answer. I only wanted to hear you say a true thing while you hung there. And you did."

He began, unhurried, to laugh again — soft, this time, private — and the office cracked.

It was not a break. It was a loosening: the three-hundred-year serenity that had set around him like amber going soft at its edges all at once, gently, the way a held breath finally lets itself all the way out.

And under it, surfacing the way that breath does, came a name.

"...*Wendel*," he said, the way a man says the name of a town he was born in and has not spoken aloud since childhood — surprised, faintly disbelieving that it still fit his own mouth.

"Wendel." He laughed again, wet-eyed now and entirely without shame about it. "That's — yes. That's the name I let go of first, before all the rest. Funny thing to get back at the very end of letting go. Oh, I *missed* it. I didn't know I could miss anything. Turns out you can be perfectly happy and still have missed your own name the whole time."

Around them the World-Tree began, very slowly, to right itself — the smallest first stirrings of a turning that would take the whole wood the rest of the evening to finish. And Wendel let go of the bough one final time, not into a hang, but into a fall, gentle as a leaf coming down at its own unhurried pace, and was set down standing on the true ground beside her, both feet flat on something that had, at long last, stopped arguing about which way it was.

"Ground," he said, looking down at his own feet with frank wonder. "Would you look at that. I'd forgotten it was down there." A beat — the one honest, wistful note. "I'll miss the view, I think. That's allowed, isn't it. To be glad, and to miss it. I rather think that's the most honest thing there is."

He reached into the air beside him — no flourish, just a man opening his hand — and a card came loose, turned over, and dropped into her waiting palms.

"Trump the Twelfth," he said. "Overturn, if anyone ever bothers to write it down properly. It's the letting-go, made a thing you can carry. Feather-fall, they'll call the best of it. You'll never fear a drop again. That's my whole life's one lesson, in your pocket. Use it kinder than the world used me. Which—" he laughed, once "—it never did, in fact. That's the joke. Go well, little Excuse."

She slotted the card into the Past pocket, where Triumph had ridden since the toll-fort, and felt it settle with the small certain click every Trump gave her now — a truth she would carry, this time, about what it costs a person to let go of a thing they loved even when the letting go is the mercy.

*  *  *

The righting of the Gallowwood took the rest of the evening, and she stayed for the whole slow tide of it, because Wendel walked beside her a little way as it happened, patient and delighted, a man taking his first unhurried tour of a house he had only ever seen from the ceiling.

It was not sudden. It was a long, tidal reshuffle, glade by glade, three centuries of held-up gravity remembering its business and letting everything gently down. Leaves that had fallen upward for three hundred years reversed mid-drift and came home the ordinary way. The upside-down brook poured off its borrowed ceiling and found itself a proper bed among the roots. The canopy bridges swung slowly round until they hung the way bridges are meant to.

Underfoot now — unhappily, abruptly — a canopy-dweller's cottage built to the old gravity found itself upside down all at once. A woman stood in her own doorway, now above her head, staring at a kettle hanging from what had just become her ceiling, her face caught between grief and a bark of laughter that hadn't decided which one it meant to be.

"...Well," she said, to nobody. "My front step's the roof now. Forty years I built that the right way up. The *old* right way up."

She did not finish the thought, and the Fool did not ask her to; some things a person needs to walk through on their own account.

Further along, a pilgrim turned slowly on the spot, his whole religion resolved without asking his permission. "He's — he came *down*. After all that. He just walked off like anyone. I think I finally understand him. Now that he's gone." Near him a rope-checker laughed outright when her line pulled the ordinary way. "Whole trade's arse-over-tit. Reckon I'll cope. A knot's still a knot."

*Feel that?* said the Querent, in a voice pitched to carry beyond just her ear. *Went out of the wood and just... kept going. The frightened ones, the guilty ones, all across the Spread — a notch calmer tonight, and none of them will know why. He does spread, that one. Even done, he spreads.*

Then, quieter: *Twelve down. And the only one of the lot who was* glad *the whole time. Sit with that a moment, little Excuse.*

And then — the one thing I allow myself, once, in every card I turn with her:

*You're the hero of this story, and the kindest thing you did today was cut down a man who was perfectly happy. Do let that sit crooked in you. It's supposed to.*

She did not answer that one. Some things you are simply meant to carry crooked, and carrying them well is its own kind of answer.

*  *  *

The wood, righted, was only a wood by the time they reached the trail where she had first met him — handsome, ordinary, gold-lit, entirely legible to anyone with working eyes and no special skill at all.

Bracken Loft stood on it with his good ropes still coiled over both shoulders, testing one against a bough out of pure habit. It pulled straight down, the way rope pulls everywhere, for everyone, and had nothing left to say to a man who had spent twenty years learning its lies.

"Twenty years," he said, without heat, which was somehow worse. "Twenty years learning to read a wood, and the wood went and learned to read itself. Any child with a plumb-line could guide this forest now. I'm — expert in a place that stopped existing about an hour ago."

"The wood's freer now," she said. "That's worth it."

"Aye. It is. I know it is." A long breath. "Doesn't make a man less useless to be useless in a *better* world, mind. But — aye. It's worth it. I'll get there. Give me a season."

Something settled in him then — not, quite, happiness, but a decision, which would do, most days, just as well.

"Right. Well." He shouldered his useless ropes, a grin finding its way onto his face, wobbling, mostly real. "I'll keep guiding. Not the terrain — any fool can read this terrain now — the *history*. I'll walk folk through and tell them: here the brook ran on the ceiling, there a whole glade hung by its roots, and up there a happy man swung for three hundred years and never once thought to come down till a stranger with a dog asked him nicely. Somebody ought to remember which way it used to hang. Might as well be the one man daft enough to have loved it."

He started up the trail ahead of them, already telling the story to no one, tripping over the shape of his own new sentences — the Gallowwood's first historian of how it used to hang, out of work, out of purpose, and, against every reasonable odd, not entirely out of joy.

*There's your ache for the day, little Excuse*, said the Querent, quiet now, the wink long since spent. *He's right, of course. He usually is. The forest's freer, the man's freer, and one honest fellow's twenty years are worth exactly nothing now, and all three of those things are true at once and none of them cancels. Onward. There's more cards, and every one of them costs somebody.*

*  *  *

She walked the last stretch of the righted wood with Pip trotting easy at her heel and the new card settled warm against her ribs, and somewhere along it she stopped on a low bank above the ordinary trail and let herself do the thing Wendel's gift had made possible: she stepped off the edge, on purpose, into open air over nothing very much at all, and let herself drop.

Feather-fall took her the way a hand takes a dropped cup before it can shatter — slowing the fall to something a leaf might have chosen, until she came to rest as gently as a held breath let out. She stood a moment feeling her own heartbeat, which had not, this time, had anything at all to say about the matter.

*There you are*, said the Querent, something almost like pride in it. *A drop taken slow as a leaf. He'd have liked to see that. I daresay he still can, somewhere.*

She turned, in the last gold light of the wood, and looked north-west — out past the crooked shoulder of the Gallowwood, to where a mountain sat locked in a dusk that had never once, in three hundred years, deepened into night or lightened into day. On its far slope, small and steady and alone, one lantern-light moved — unhurried, going nowhere anyone had ever managed to follow it to.

"What's that one?"

*That*, said the Querent, and I will confess I let the fondness show more than I meant to, *is the Dim. And the very next thing on your road, if you've a mind to go and get properly lost for a while.*

She whistled up the dog and turned her boots toward the lantern, and did not look back at the Gallowwood again — though she would tell me, later, that she still thought sometimes about a man teaching a whole forest how to fall gently, and a guide learning to love a place for what it used to be.


---

# Chapter Eleven — The Light on the Mountain

The Gallowwood let her down properly at last — feet under her instead of over her, the
canopy bridges giving way to ordinary uphill ground the way a held breath finally lets go
— and she climbed out still walking a little sideways, the habit of two days spent
upside-down. A card worn close over her ribs had made falling into a thing that simply
happened to her now, rather than a thing she had to survive.

The treeline thinned, then stopped, and the last stunted pine gave up its argument with
the slope and let the mountain have it plainly.

Above, the sky did not so much begin as fail to begin: a held breath of a sky, cloudless
and starless both, blue-grey from one horizon to the other, giving her eye nothing at all
to land on. Not evening. Not the deep blue before morning. Simply dusk, going nowhere,
going on.

The cold met her a step past the last pine and did not let go again — not the sharp cold
of wind, for there was none here to carry it, but something patient, seeped into the
stone itself, a rime that had never once thawed and refrozen. Pip pressed in against her
leg. Not frightened — just cold, and glad of the company, his breath going up in two
small ghosts that hung and were still hanging when she looked back a dozen paces on.

Far off, on a shoulder of rock she could barely separate from the dusk, a single point of
warm light moved — amber, unhurried, walking a ridgeline at something very like a
person's pace, before it went behind a fold of stone and did not come out again.

*There it is*, said the Querent, and there was something in the voice she hadn't heard
before — not quite fondness, not quite dread, some third thing between the two. *The
famous light. Three hundred years it's walked those ridges, and three hundred years
nobody's laid a hand on it. Folk come up here meaning to. Folk go home again. The
mountain is very good at sending people home.*

She looked about for a road sign, a scuffed path worn thin — the ordinary furniture of
every region she'd crossed so far. There was none of it. No trail. No waypost. Only rock,
frost, and dusk.

"There's no path."

*No.* The Querent let that sit a moment, unadorned, which was its own kind of answer.
*This one doesn't hold your hand, little Excuse. This one asks you to read the ground.*

She settled the Bindle on her shoulder and started up.

*  *  *

She had gone perhaps a mile — hard to say, where the ground gave no landmark and the sky
gave none at all — when a second light showed itself. This one did not move.

It sat low in a fold of the slope: a small stone shack, one window lit gold, a thread of
chimney-smoke standing straight up, the exact shape smoke keeps where there is no wind to
argue it out of. Beside it, under a slate lean-to, stood a stone basin swept clear of
frost, and as she came near it the rose at her belt gave its small private shiver — five
petals now, stirring against a stillness they had no business stirring against.

The door opened before her knuckles reached it. A woman stood in the gap: small, upright
as a fence-post, a shawl pinned close with a bone pin, a ladle still in one hand, looking
the Fool over — boots, staff, frost whitening her eyebrows — with the thoroughness of
someone who has looked over every cold and lost thing to climb this path longer than
she's troubled to count.

"Come in out of it, then," she said. "Kettle's on. Kettle's always on — no sense a
mountain this cold and no kettle." She looked down. "Dog first, you after. He knows where
the fire is better than you do."

Pip went past her ankles at a trot, entirely satisfied, and the Fool followed him in out
of the dusk.

*  *  *

Inside was a single room built entirely around the business of being warm: cots along
one wall, more than one traveller could ever need; a long table worn pale by decades of
forearms; and on a shelf above the hearth, a row of mugs, no two alike, each left, she
understood without being told, by a traveller who had meant, at the time, to come back
for it.

"Fenner," the woman said, setting down the ladle and taking up the tin mug instead,
filling it without asking. "I keep the waypoint. Have done since — well." A small
motion, taking in the mugs, the cots, the whole cold weight of *since*. "Since keeping it
was worth doing. You warm your hands, and I'll tell you the one thing everybody comes up
here to ask."

The mug came into the Fool's palms warm all the way through.

"The light," the Fool said.

"The light." Widow Fenner sat across from her, hands folded, the shawl-pin catching the
firelight. "It walks the far ridges every dusk — which is every hour, up here, dusk being
all we get. Never the same slope twice. Never close enough to hail. Folk set out after it
bright as buttons, and the mountain walks them in circles till the cold talks sense back
into them, and they come here, and I feed them, and they go down home."

"Why keep the kettle on for strangers?"

Something crossed Widow Fenner's face and was gone before it could be called an
expression. "Because somebody has to be the warm window on a cold mountain. Been my
whole purpose, love. A body likes a purpose." Her chin came up half an inch. "Don't mind
me. Drink up."

The Fool drank. It went down into her like the first honest thing that had happened to
her in a mile.

"That's the trick nobody credits," Widow Fenner went on, watching the fire now. "The
light doesn't run. It just keeps *one ridge* on. Always one ridge. And one ridge is
exactly as far as a body can bear to be from a warm fire before they turn round." She
turned the empty ladle over in her lap. "Clever, that. Cruel, maybe. I've stopped
deciding which."

"Go on, then," she said, standing, brisk again, "since you've the look. Take the dog.
Read the ground. And when the cold starts making sense — and it will — you remember
there's a window down here still lit. Coming back isn't losing. Most folk never learn
that." She pressed a heel of warm bread into the Fool's hand at the door, the way you'd
press a coin into a child's fist, and made nothing of it whatsoever. "Off you go."

*  *  *

The ground tilted up past the shack into a long grey fan of loose scree, every stone
wanting to slide the moment weight came onto it, and here, for the first time, the
mountain had left tracks: cold fire-embers, long dead, and boot-prints between them
pressed into frost that had never once thawed to take them back.

"Find it, then," she told Pip; his nose went down and his tail went up and he cast
forward in the busy zigzag of a dog with proper work to do, pausing at each dead ember
only long enough to confirm it and move on.

She followed him up, reading the tilt of the prints, the drift of old ash thickest in a
boulder's lee. Twice the scree gave way under her and sent her sliding with a clatter
that went nowhere in the windless air — and both times something in her simply refused
to be afraid of it, the fall no worse than a bad step on level ground, the card over her
heart doing exactly what it had promised on the Gallowwood's bridges.

At the top of the fan the light showed itself close for the first time — a hundred yards
off across a shallow gully, held low by a shape the dusk would not quite resolve into a
person. For one entire held second, it was *right there*. She crested the lip at
something close to a run, and it was already on the next ridge over, going away at the
same unhurried walk it had kept the whole way up the mountain. Not fleeing. Simply
keeping its one ridge.

Something in her chest did a small ugly lurch — hope arriving and leaving inside the same
breath — and she stood with her fists closed on nothing.

*Ah. You felt that, did you*, said the Querent, gently, into the quiet after. *The little
lurch of it being close and then not. Everyone feels that one. It's the mountain's
opening move, and it has never once failed to work.* A pause, then, more useful: *Don't
chase the light itself. You can't. Chase the ground it walked. That, you can read.*

*  *  *

Past the gully the land opened into a valley so wide and flat its far walls stood only as
a darker line against a dusk barely darker still, the floor drowned to the knee in fog
that closed silent behind her boots. Overhead: nothing. No moon, no star, no mark to say
which way was which. She walked perhaps thirty steps toward where the light had last
shown, and understood by the thirty-first that she had already lost the line — her own
footprints swallowed the moment the mist closed over them.

*Nothing up there to help you tonight*, the Querent said. *Whoever's carrying that lamp
picked the one corner of the world where a light is the only thing worth looking at.
That's either despair or it's an invitation. I've a guess which.*

She crouched instead, hand flat to the frozen ground, and found — faint, but there — the
lean of old churned earth where other feet had gone before her, turning, doubling back,
here and there going purposefully on. She followed those as far as they held, then
called Pip in low and let him cast ahead, nose working the cold air for the one thing in
all this white nothing that had a smell at all: lamp oil, faint and wrong.

Twice the light showed through the mist — once dead ahead, drawing her true, and once
down a false spur her own feet very nearly took before Pip planted himself square across
it and would not be moved, an immovable small object between her and a drop she could
not see. "Good dog," she said, low, and meant it.

She crossed the valley by dirt and by dog, and never once by sky, and came up at last
against a slope of standing rock she'd not have found any other way.

*  *  *

The slope beyond was studded with cairns — dozens of them, stacked stone of every age.
Not grave markers, she understood: turning-points, each built by somebody who had
decided, on this exact step of this exact climb, that this was as far as they were
willing to go, and had raised a small monument to the deciding before turning back
toward Fenner's fire. Some had a scrap of cloth knotted to the topmost stone, fluttering
nowhere in the still air. One, low down, had a child's shoe set atop it, laces tied in a
bow that had not come undone in longer than she wanted to think about.

Among the cairns, wrapped in three coats against a cold that plainly hadn't the first
idea what it was up against, sat a traveller adding a stone, cheerful, to a cairn of
their own.

"Building mine up proper this time," they said, without turning round. "You after the
light? Don't. It's a ghost, that. Old lamplighter died on his round and never noticed,
still walks it, still lighting what's already lit. My gran said so. My gran was *rarely*
right, but she was right about that."

"How do you know it's a ghost?"

"Well, nobody's caught it, have they." The traveller settled the stone, patted it fondly.
"Living things get caught. Only a ghost keeps its distance three hundred years." A grin,
gap-toothed, entirely unbothered. "And a ghost you can stop chasing without feeling a
fool about it. Handy, a ghost. Very restful."

They looked at their own cairn, and the cheer thinned by exactly one shade, the way a
fire does when nobody's tending it.

"Built this the first time forty years back," they said, quieter. "Rebuild it every time
I come up and lose my nerve. Suppose the cairn's the thing I actually finish." A short,
surprised laugh, at their own expense. "That's *something*, though, isn't it. Catching a
thing you can finish."

The Fool looked at the shoe on its low cairn, and said the only honest thing she had.
"I hope you finish the climb one day."

"So do I, pet." They were already reaching for the next stone. "One more ridge. Always
tell myself one more ridge." A dry chuckle, not unhappy. "Mountain heard me the first
time, and it's been laughing ever since."

She left them stacking stones, and went on up.

*  *  *

Beyond the highest cairn anyone had ever built — the ground past it unmarked, unclaimed
by three hundred years of anybody — there was only slope, and the light, closer now than
it had let her see it since the scree.

She followed it across three linked ridges, and somewhere on the second something
changed. The light was not gaining distance any more. She walked; it walked; the gap
held a beat, then closed a hand's width, then a stride, and did not open again.

*Look at that*, said the Querent, real astonishment under the dryness now, three hundred
years of watching finally catching up with itself. *It's slowing. No — you're not
gaining because you're faster. You're gaining because you didn't stop. That's the whole
difference. That was always the whole difference. Nobody ever failed to catch that
light. They only ever failed to keep walking toward it.*

She crested the third ridge, breath tearing, legs gone to water, and the light was
waiting at the top of the rise beyond — not receding, not moving at all, simply
standing, as though whoever carried it had turned, at last, and seen, for the first time
in three centuries, someone still coming.

*Three hundred years*, the Querent said, quiet now, almost to itself, *and the one who
followed all the way turns out to be the card that can't be told where to stop.* A dry,
private breath of a laugh. *I'd say I planned that, little Excuse. But we both know
who's actually been doing the walking, and it isn't me.*

She went up the last rise at a walk, because she found, when it came to it, that she no
longer had the strength or the wish to run.

*  *  *

Over the crest, in a hollow tucked out of a wind that had never once reached it, a small
fire burned, steady and low. Beside it, set down on a flat stone — set *down*, for the
first time in three hundred years — sat the lantern, its light warm and unremarkable, an
ordinary light doing an ordinary job of holding back the dark.

And beside the lantern sat a man.

He was old the way the mountain was old — arrived at some far side of time where age had
simply become the shape he was. A traveller's cloak gone thin from long use; a face
gentle and remote, the face of someone who has thought so long he has forgotten there was
ever meant to be an end to it.

He did not startle. He did not rise. On the flat stone in front of him sat two cups of
tea, both steaming, both — she could see it even from here — just cool enough to have
been poured a little while ago, by someone who had known, at last, that company was
coming.

"There you are," he said, as she came into the firelight, his voice as unhurried as
everything else about him. "Sit — the second cup's gone cold about forty times over the
years, but I keep pouring it." He nodded at it, not quite smiling, not quite anything so
simple as sad. "A lantern's no use to a man alone. It's for being *followed* by. And in
three hundred years, do you know" — he said it without a grain of self-pity, the way
you'd remark on an odd fact of weather — "nobody once followed far enough to be worth the
pouring."

Pip walked past her legs and settled against the old man's knee, entirely at ease; the
old man rested a hand on his head, as if it were the most natural thing in the world.

She sat. The stone was cold and the fire was not, and between the two of them she found,
for the first time since the treeline, that she was properly warm.

"Not that the light was ever hidden," he said. "Anyone could have caught it. It only
ever asked one thing — that a body keep walking after it when the fire behind them said
turn round." A small, private shake of the head. "Turns out that's the rarest thing
there is. Rarer than courage. Courage is loud. This is just — not stopping."

"Why keep walking away, then," she asked, "if you wanted catching?"

He was quiet a long moment.

"Because the walking was the question," he said at last. "And I wanted to see who'd
answer it." He lifted the second cup and held it out to her across the fire, something
almost formal in the gesture, an old rite performed for the first time in longer than
either of them could reckon. "Drink. I've earned a conversation, and so, plainly, have
you." A dry warmth came into his face, there and gone. "Only — before the tea goes cold
a forty-first time — let me ask you the one thing I climbed all the way up here to ask,
and never had anyone to ask it *of*."

She took the cup. It was warm in her two cold hands, and smelled of woodsmoke and
something bitter-green she had no name for, and she held it and did not drink, because
this was not yet the moment for drinking.

He set his own cup down, folded his hands, and asked it plainly — the way a man asks a
thing he has been holding, unasked, for three hundred years.

"What have you been looking for," he said, "all this way?"

The fire cracked once and settled. Pip's tail moved against the old man's boot and went
still. The old man watched her the way you watch something you have waited a very long
time to see, careful not to hurry it, careful not to breathe too loud in case it
startled off.

She thought of the meadow she had woken in, and the tent full of people holding out
their arms to nobody, and a hundred small rooms of people who had been waiting, one way
or another, for somebody to come and let them finish — and found that the true thing,
when it came, was smaller than any of that, and truer for being smaller.

"Whatever's still waiting to be found," she said.

He looked at her a long moment — not surprised, exactly; more the look of a man hearing a
tune he half-remembered, played correctly at last by somebody else's hands.

"Ah," he said, softly. "Then you and I are the same animal." He picked his own cup back
up, turned it once in his fingers. "Only — mind you don't stop as long as I did. Three
hundred years is a great deal of *waiting to be found*, and it is not the same as being
found." He said it gently — as a thing, she thought, he wished somebody had said to him,
once, before the wanting wore itself into a walking he could no longer stop.

Then he drank his tea, and it was, at last, warm, and something in his face — some old
patient tension she had not clocked as tension until it left — let go all at once.

"There," he said. "I've been asked after, and answered, and had my tea hot." He set the
cup down, gently, finally, the way you set down a thing you will not be picking up
again. "That's the whole of what a lantern was ever for. I don't think I need to keep
walking any more."

*  *  *

He reached for the lantern.

She felt it before she understood what she was seeing: as his hand closed round the
handle, the light did not change, not one flicker of it, and yet the air around him
altered, the way the air alters before a weight drops — the lantern growing, in his grip,
impossibly heavy, a small warm flame taking on the whole dark mountain it had crossed and
crossed. He lifted it anyway. Both hands. His arms shook with it, and around him the
office came apart: the endless walking, the always-one-ridge-on, three centuries of being
a light and never once a man, cracking away with a sound like ice giving on a frozen
pond, fissures opening in something she had taken, until that moment, for simply his
face.

And underneath it, surfacing the way a held breath finally lets go, came a name.

"...*Ellery*."

He said it carefully, turning the shape of it over on his tongue, the way you'd test
frost with one boot to see whether it would bear your weight.

"Ellery. Yes." He looked up at her, and he was, all at once, only an old man — tired,
and glad, and entirely unperformed. "That was — that *is* mine. I'd gone so far into the
thinking I forgot there was a thinker with a name." A short laugh, rusty, real. "Well
caught, my friend. Not the light. *Me.* You caught me, which is the harder thing, and
the only one that ever counted."

He held the lantern out across the fire — both hands, arms still trembling with the
mountain-weight of it, though it burned in her own eyes as nothing more than an ordinary
lamp, warm and willing to be carried.

"Trump the Ninth," he said. "The Lantern. It's yours now — it was always going to be
whoever kept walking." He waited until her fingers had closed properly round the handle
before he let his own go. "Hold it high when the dark lies to you. It doesn't know how
to lie back." A small, mountain-old smile came and went at the corner of his mouth. "And
when you find the ridge you've been walking toward all this way — I hope there's
somebody sitting at it with the tea already poured."

In her hands, the lantern was only a lantern: warm, light, easy to carry. The weight, she
understood, had never once been in the lamp. It had been in the carrying of it alone.

*  *  *

The change came over the mountain the way a dawn would, if the Dim had ever once been
allowed one — not so much brightening as *unlocking*: the flat blue-grey lid of the sky
thinning, and pricking, one hard point of white at a time, then a dozen, then more than
either of them could count standing still and trying. Stars. Hundreds of them. Then,
thousands — laid out across a sky that had shown nothing at all, not a mark, for three
hundred years, arriving all at once the way an answer arrives when a question has
finally been allowed to finish being asked.

She stood with her head tipped back until her neck ached and did not care. There was no
day and night anywhere else in the wide Spread yet — but here, three centuries of held
breath let go all at once into a night of its very own: cold, clear, unlike anything the
rest of the world had been permitted to keep. She had not known a sky could hold so much
and still leave room for more.

Ellery stood beside her, no longer needing the lantern to see by, and looked up with her
in silence, which for him was a very great deal.

Down every trail, small warm lights began to move — the lost coming down out of the
hills at last, following an actual sky home the way they had never once been able to
follow a single wandering lamp. High on the far eastern face, one small window stayed
dark to everything but its own lamp, trained down a trail none of the returning lights
were using — kept for somebody who was not, tonight, among the ones coming home. She
could not fix that. Not tonight.

*  *  *

The shack, when she came back down to it, was emptying. Cots were being stripped by
their own occupants, travellers she recognised from the cairn field and the valley,
headed for a path that had, an hour before, not existed for any of them. By the time she
reached the door, the long table sat all but bare.

Widow Fenner stood among the mismatched mugs, drying one that no longer had anybody left
to come back and claim it.

"Well," she said, not turning round straight away. "There they go. All of them, near
enough. Down the mountain like water that's finally found its slope." She set the mug
back on its shelf, in the space it had always occupied. "Three hundred years I've been
the warm window on a cold mountain, love." A breath, level, without self-pity. "Turns
out the mountain's not so cold any more. Turns out it doesn't much need a window."

She said it without bitterness, and that was somehow the worst part of it — no one to be
angry at, nothing left to set right.

"I'm sorry," the Fool said.

"Don't be, love." Widow Fenner's chin came up, sharp. "Don't you *dare* be. You gave a
mountain of people their way home. If the price of that's one old woman with too many
clean mugs — that's the cheapest thing I ever heard tell of. Go on. I'll not have it."

The Fool said nothing, and watched her fill the kettle and set it back over the fire —
though the shack was emptying, and there was, by any sensible reckoning, nobody left up
here to warm.

"Old habit," she said, catching the Fool watching, chin lifting, half a dare and half
something softer. "In case someone's still finding their way. You never do know." The
kettle ticked, settling onto the iron. "And a body likes a purpose. Even a made-up one."
A small, unguarded smile. "*Especially* a made-up one."

The kettle began, very quietly, to sing. Outside, the new stars held steady over a
mountain that had finally, after three hundred years, arrived somewhere.

*  *  *

She left before the singing finished, because the road did not wait politely for a
person to feel finished with a place before it asked to be walked.

At the treeline she opened the near pocket of her coat — the one that had carried, since
a carnival ring three hundred years less lonely than this mountain, a card with a
