Twice she reached for the Hermit's lantern in the near pocket, because a raised light had solved two regions for her, and twice she got it up and blazing and it did nothing whatever but throw her own enormous shadow across the sand, there being nothing hidden anywhere on that shore to reveal. The second time she caught the look on his face — pure sympathy, as at a child trying a door that was never locked — and let the light go out, and did not reach for it again.

So it was hands and feet and the length of a stick against a farmer with a farmer's tool, and there was nothing else in the world.

And her breath began to come in the right places. Every window she got, she made. She dodged a sweep late and shallow, on purpose, and the world went slow and syrupy in her chest the way it had first gone on a plateau above the clouds, and she stepped through the arc as through a doorway and got two clean strikes into his flank before it closed. She baited the pull twice and slipped it. He grunted. He gave ground. He came back.

And somewhere in the fourth exchange, wringing wet, with sand up both sleeves and her heart going like a mill-race, she noticed the thing that changed the whole afternoon for her.

He was enjoying it.

It was not cruelty and it was not pride. It was in the set of his shoulders, and the almost-smile that never entirely left him, and the small satisfied noise he made when she read the pull correctly for the fourth time and made him work for the fifth. Here was a man doing, well and with his whole attention, the single thing his office had ever been for — doing it against somebody who could actually receive it, for the first and last time, at the end of an unbearably long working life. He fought her the way a great baker bakes a last loaf.

She stopped being frightened of him somewhere in there. She did not stop being frightened.

*  *  *

He stepped back out of measure, planted the scythe butt-down in the wet sand and rested one hand on the top of the snath. He was not staggering, and he was certainly not out of breath, because he never had been. He had simply chosen to stop. The candlelight steadied. The water went glass-still.

"Now. While we've breath between us. Because you've earned the *whole* of it, and I'll not send you back into that second half half-blind."

She stood in the middle of the ring with her chest heaving, and knew what was coming the way a person knows a letter's contents from the handwriting on the outside.

"You know what I am," Mortimer said. "You've worked out, I think, most of what the rest are. Every card you've turned loose so far, you did it by letting something *end* — a show, a harvest, a verdict, a long march. That's the shape of the whole journey. That's not a trick being played on you. That's just what it is."

She had done that sum. She had done it in a fog-grey courtroom four regions back, got as far as the last line of it, and stopped, deliberately, and gone to sleep instead.

"The last card in the Great Reading is The World. When it turns, the Reading is done. And when the Reading is done, friend, the world is gathered up and dealt again — the Shuffle." He held her eyes across the candles. "Finishing the journey doesn't *save* the world. Finishing the journey *ends* it. That's what completing the Reading means. I'll not pretend otherwise. Nobody has ever loved a thing enough to pretend otherwise to it, and I love you too much to start."

He bent and picked the scythe up off the sand, gently, and held it across both hands, level — offered, rather than raised.

"You are the ending, little Excuse. I am only the door."

The candles burned without moving. Far off, small and silver and perfectly still, the Axis sat at the middle of everything, and the tiny figure at its heart went on turning.

And then, in her ear, the voice that had gone quiet on the road came back.

*He's right.*

There was no music in it. None of the dry lift, none of the little swerve, none of the sideways brightness it put on things when it did not wish to be asked a question. It came out flat and plain and unhidden, the way a voice comes out when its owner has stopped arranging it.

*Everything he just said is true, and I've known it was true since the cliff, and I let you find it out slow because I couldn't bear to be the one to say it. But he can say it, because he's Death, and Death doesn't flinch.*

A pause. She stood very still.

*So — there. No more sliding round it. The journey ends the world. It always did.* Quietly. *You've a right to know that standing up, and now you do.*

She was standing up. She had not, until that moment, understood that this had been arranged for her.

She looked at the candles, and at the water holding every one of them twice, and at a small white dog sitting at the light's edge with his ears up, entirely unchanged, waiting for whatever came next the way he had waited on the lip of a cliff. Then she looked across the ring at the man who had told her, and not at the world, and not at anything else.

"Then I'll walk it with my eyes open."

Mortimer's whole face eased — three centuries coming off it at once, the way a held weight comes off a shoulder.

"That's all I wanted for you." His voice had gone rough. "Not that you'd go on — that you'd go on *seeing*." He settled his grip on the scythe, and the almost-smile came back, and it was proud. "There's the difference between a mercy and a murder, and you just chose the right side of it."

He came up to guard.

"Then let's finish it. Both of us. Nothing hidden now, on either side. Come on, friend. Come and do it *right.*"

*  *  *

The second half was the same duel and an entirely different fight. He was faster: the sweeps harder and closer together, the pull tightened until the half-step warning was barely a shift of weight, the reap chained now into follow-ups that asked for everything the first half had taught her and a little more it had not. He gave her nothing. He forgave her nothing. He had never once been trying to lose.

And it was — she would think about this for a long time afterwards — *easier*, in the only way that mattered. Not simpler. Lighter. Nothing stood anywhere on that shore now that was not true, and a great deal of what had been tiring her turned out to be the carrying of a thing she had refused to look at. She was not tricking her way to an ending any more. She was choosing one, blow by blow, having been told to the last penny what it cost.

She spent three more petals. She took the reap on the Bindle and felt her arms go numb to the shoulder. She got the pull wrong once more, late, and paid for it in the shallows.

And then the opening came, and it was hers, and it was not given. He had chained a sweep into the reap, and the sand under his forward boot was wetter than he had judged, and for a quarter of a heartbeat the blade hung at the top of its arc with all his weight committed under it. The world slowed. She saw the grain of the snath and a bead of water coming off the edge and hanging there with nothing to do. She stepped inside the arc and put the Bindle in under his ribs with everything she had left, and the whole shore rang with it.

Mortimer did not fall like a man struck down. He sank like a man setting down a weight he had carried past all reason: knees to the sand, then the scythe laid flat beside him with a care that was almost tenderness, then a breath going out of him, long and slow and complete — and at the very end of it, something that had been locked in his chest for three hundred years going out with it.

*  *  *

The office cracked.

It did not shatter. It came apart the way frost comes off a window in the first honest warmth — not a break, a thawing: the dark of the robes going ordinary, the authority draining out of the scythe until it was a farm tool lying on a beach, the whole great weight of being *Death* rather than a man lifting off him and away in the space of two breaths.

He knelt there in the candlelight, smaller and plainer and unbearably relieved, and turned his own hands over in front of his face and looked at them as though somebody had just given them to him.

Everyone in that marsh had called him Mortimer for three hundred years. He had answered to it, gently, a thousand thousand times, on a thousand thousand thresholds. He had never once been able to say it of himself.

Now he could.

"...Mortimer."

It came out barely aloud. He tested it, the way a man tests a word in a language he was born to and never permitted to speak.

"*I'm* Mortimer."

He said it again, steadier, and his eyes came up, wide and quietly astonished at the sound of it in his own mouth.

"Do you know, in three hundred years — that's the first time I've ever got to say it. They all called me it. Every soul I ever sat with. And I never once got to say it *back.* Mortimer. My name is Mortimer." The breath he took then was almost a laugh and almost a sob and landed as neither. "Oh, that's — that's a good sound. That's a *good* sound."

He reached into the front of the robe, over the heart, and drew out the card: Trump XIII, Passage, plain as a gravestone and warm from three hundred years of being carried against a man's chest. He did not flourish it. He took her hand in both of his and folded the card into it and closed her fingers over it and held them there — both his hands round her one, on his knees in the sand, entirely sincere.

"Don't waste it."

That was all. He let go, and got to his feet — steady, whole, unhurried in a way that was suddenly a choice and not an office — and stood looking out at the lantern lanes he had kept for three centuries with the face of a man about to go home at last.

She stood with the card in her fist. Then she opened her coat and took the Assize's Verdict out of the third pocket, the deep one, the *not yet* one, and put Passage in its place, and it went in as though the pocket had been cut for it. She did not feel powerful. She felt like somebody carrying a full cup down a flight of stairs.

*  *  *

The change came the way the office had left: not with spectacle, but with a hush going out of the air. She felt it on the causeway before she saw it — a pressure lifting off the whole flat country, the way a room changes when a held breath is let go somewhere in it. Nothing collapsed. Nothing cried out. Across the marsh, all at once and very quietly, the ones who had been ready began to be able to go.

In the great raftered house down the long causeway, the woman at the far window stopped watching.

That was all it was. Her hands stayed in her lap. Her chair stayed three-quarters turned from the room. Her watching simply, gently ended, and the two who loved her came and closed her eyes, and one of them put a hand flat on her hair for a while and did not say anything. It was the first true ending the Stillmarsh had held since before the Stall, and every person under that roof knew it, and it was right.

Nearer the door, Gammer Thist was waving both arms.

"There you are, my duck — quick, come here and *witness it*, the old goat's finally going first, I win, I *win* —" she was pink to the ears "— three hundred years I've held that wager and I've got him at last!"

Old Barrow lay back on his pillows with his cards squared on the blanket and an expression of enormous dignity and not one shred of hurry.

"Won fair," he said. "Loaf of bread, when either baker's reborn to bake it." He turned his head to the Fool, dry as dust. "Tell her she can gloat all she likes. I'll not be about to hear it, and that, madam, is the sweetest part of losing."

Gammer Thist laughed until she had to hold her own ribs, and Old Barrow's shoulders went, and half the room went with them. It was the last laugh either of them got. Both of them plainly knew it, and they spent every penny of it on each other anyway.

*  *  *

The marsh emptied the way a lamp lowers: slowly, without drama, one light at a time.

She counted the days in meals, and in the burning-down of candles, which was itself the newest thing in the world. Nobody in the Stillmarsh had ever seen a candle burn down. On the second day people were coming out onto the causeways to look at pools of wax, and pointing, and calling one another over; and Corse Millbank stood beneath his own flame with his arms folded and a great deal to say about the merchant who had sold it to him three hundred years ago on the understanding that it was quality goods.

The lanes thinned. The vigil-houses went quiet room by room, and then bed by bed, and then whole rooms were only rooms. And into the emptied hush came two things the marsh had not held since before the Stall.

The first was funerals. They were the world's first, and nothing whatever like the dread the word carried: modest, communal, closer to a barn-raising than a horror. Neighbours carried a neighbour out along the causeways by lantern-light, six at a time, changing shoulders at the corners. There were a few plain words at the water's edge, said by whoever knew the person best and could get through it. A cup went round. A name was said aloud, properly, once, and then let go, and everybody went back and ate an enormous quantity of soup.

"We buried Sedge Fenner this morning," a man told her, leaning on a spade with his sleeves rolled and mud to the elbow. "First grave dug in the Stillmarsh in three hundred years. Hard work, digging. Good work. Honest work. We'd forgotten."

The second was the cold. It came in off the water on the third day, a real cold with an end somewhere in it. The sky did not change; the sky had not changed since before anyone there was born, and would not for a long while yet. But the willows along the north lanes went from green to a hard rusty gold in the space of two days, and then, one at a time and then in handfuls, they let their leaves go.

She stood on a causeway and watched the first of them come down onto the black water. It landed. It floated. And then, unhurried, in no particular hurry about anything, the water carried it away.

"Cold's come in," said an old woman beside her, hugging herself and beaming. "Proper cold. Autumn, they're calling it — them as remember the word. My old bones ache, and I could weep for the joy of it."

*  *  *

On the reed road at the marsh's eastern edge, three Blanks came up out of the standing water. It was not a hard fight, and she put the last of them down with the Bindle across the back of the knees and stepped away, already turning, because she knew exactly what happened next: the body slumps like a puppet set down kindly, and the card peels off the tabard and goes up, tumbling end over end on a wind that is not there, away over the nearest ridge, to find somebody new to stand up and be.

The card did not go up. It lay on the tabard where it had always been, and the colour went out of it — the reds and the black draining away into the grey of old newsprint — and the blank face under it settled into the reeds, and the whole arrangement lay there being nothing at all.

She stood over it a long time with her heart going in a way the fight had not caused. Then she crouched and turned the grey card over with one finger, and found nothing on the back of it either, and put it down, and went on.

*  *  *

Some days on, the lamp at Bettony's door was out.

The bed was made. That was the first thing: a neatly made bed, blanket squared at the corners, in a house where a bed had been occupied without a break for longer than the woman beside it had been alive. The window was open to the turning cold. Bettony Marsh sat in the chair with her hands in her lap, and her face was doing something that had no name and would not be given one.

"He went in his sleep. Three nights back." Her voice was perfectly steady, which was the worst of it. "Just — went. Gentle as you like, the way I always swore to him he would, if he'd only hold on till you came." She looked at the empty bed. "And he held on. He held on a hundred years past sense, because I asked him to. And then you came, and he didn't have to any more, and he *didn't.*"

She looked up. There was no accusation anywhere in her face. There was only arithmetic that would not come out.

"I keep waiting to feel one thing about it. Relief, or grief, the way you're meant to pick one. And I can't. It's both, all the time, at once, and they won't sit still long enough to be either." Her chin came up. "He's *out.* Three hundred years and he's finally out, and I'm so glad I can hardly breathe. And he's *gone.* And I'm so —"

And there it cracked, for the first time, only slightly, the way a plate cracks and holds.

"— I wanted him free more than I've ever wanted anything, and now he is, and the wanting's got nowhere to go."

She turned to the bed. On the pillow, tucked where nobody would find it until the sheets were changed, lay a folded scrap of paper, written in a shaky deliberate hand on some patient night against exactly this morning. It had plainly been unfolded and refolded a hundred times in three days. She opened it and read it out anyway, because she could not stop.

"*Took you all long enough.*"

And it did precisely what the old man had meant it to do. It broke her open in the good way and not the bad one: the laugh came up through the tears and carried half of them off with it — a wet, undignified, entirely involuntary bark of a laugh, hands over her mouth, shoulders going — the driest man in the marsh getting the last word in from three nights dead and spending it, deliberately, on making his granddaughter laugh at his own funeral, because he loved her and knew to the inch exactly how she loved.

She held both feelings at once, and neither gave way, and nothing tidied them.

"...Trust him." She wiped her face with the heel of her hand, wet-eyed and almost smiling. "Trust him to have the last word. Three hundred years too tired to sit up, and he saves his breath for *that.*" She folded the paper away, small, into her sleeve. "Go on. I'll be all right. Not today. But I'll be all right — which is more than any of us could say last week. Thank you. For the door. For letting him find it."

*  *  *

The ferry was where it had always been, poling the same black lanes past the same low lanterns, and it was not the same crossing, and both of them knew it. Old Sallow brought the boat in and leaned on the pole and looked at her a while before he said anything.

"Well. You did it. Marsh is emptying out around me by the day. Folk I've carried across nowhere in particular for three centuries, and now the far bank's a real bank and they're actually *landing* on it." He shook his head slowly. "Strange, watching them go. Good strange. The best strange there is."

He looked out over the water — at the lanterns doubling in it, at the leaves the willows had finally been allowed to drop, riding away in twos and threes toward somewhere.

"I've been nearly done dying for three hundred years, friend. And do you know — now that it's on the table, now that a body can actually *finish* — I find I'm rather looking forward to it. Not rushing at it, mind. I've a ferry to keep a while yet; somebody's to carry the last of them over, and I'll not leave that to young Tarn on his own. But when my turn comes round at the back of the line, where it belongs — I'll take it. Gladly. I've earned a good long nothing."

She stood on the jetty with a small white dog beside her and a rose grown whole again at her belt and thirteen cards in her coat, and did not trust herself to say anything at all.

Old Sallow bowed to Pip, without deciding to, the way he always had. Then he pushed off, unhurried, the pole going down without a splash, and called back across the still water the plainest and most patient thing the Stillmarsh had to say.

"No rush, mind. I've waited this long."

The ferry slid out across the doubled dusk, and the candles burned low and level in their hundreds, and the first leaves of the first autumn in three hundred years came down onto water that was, at last, allowed to carry them somewhere.


---

# Interlude — The Lantern Ledger

*Being pages from the book kept by Tarn Loach, ferryman's mate, at the landing of the
Stillmarsh — copied here exactly as he wrote them, blots, crossings-out, and the odd
argument with himself left in.*

*  *  *

**A note, first, on how this book is to be kept** (written some years back, and copied
fair into every new volume since, because a standard, once set, ought to be honoured):
*One name per line. Waiting-time in years, estimated where not known exactly, and marked
"est." so nobody mistakes a guess for a fact. One small true thing about the person,
where I can learn it, so they are not only a name to whoever reads this after me — if
anybody ever does. No opinions of my own. This is a record, not a diary, and I will
thank myself to remember the difference, which I frequently do not.*

**GOODWIFE HALLOW'S GREAT-GRANDMOTHER** — waiting 300 years (est.). Asks after the
weather every visit, though the weather has not changed in living memory. Waits anyway.
Small true thing: she was, by all accounts, a terror at cards, and has not lost the look
of a woman who is still winning.

**THE SAILOR'S WIFE, sixth window along** — waiting 300 years (est.). Watches the
crossing and says nothing. I have tried three times to learn her name and been told,
gently, that it isn't mine to have yet. Small true thing: her candle has never once
guttered, not in three hundred years, and the man who tends it will not hear a word
against tallow merchants in her hearing.

*(A run of some four hundred further entries follows in the same hand, omitted here for
space. They are none of them less important than the two above. I want that written down
somewhere, even if only in my own book, even if only I ever read it.)*

*  *  *

**Note to self, undated:** *A new column has gone in today, at the right-hand margin,
where there has never before been the smallest need of one. I have ruled the line twice
to be sure it is straight. I do not know yet what will go under it, only that something
will, which is a stranger feeling than I have words ready for.*

**Heading, right-hand margin, new column, ruled twice:** CROSSED.

**THE SAILOR'S WIFE, sixth window along.** Crossed today. First true crossing the
landing has ever recorded — mine or my two predecessors' books, and I have checked both,
carefully, standing up, because it did not seem a thing to check sitting down. She went
watching the water, same as always, and then she simply was not watching it any more,
and the ones who loved her closed her window and did not need me for anything except to
write it here, which I have done, and my hand shook doing it, and I am not ashamed to
say so in my own book.

**Note to self:** *Ledger-keeping standard amended. Under CROSSED, record only the day
counted from today, called the First Day, there being no better word for it and no
sense waiting for a finer one. I am aware "day" is a habit of speech more than a fact of
the sky in this marsh. I am keeping the habit. A man has to count from somewhere.*

**OLD BARROW.** Waited 300 years (est.). Crossed, First Day, some hours after the
window. Won a wager laid the night before the Stall, the stakes a loaf of bread neither
baker now living is able to bake — Gammer Thist has instructed me, at some length and
volume, that this makes her the winner and him the loser, on grounds I did not follow
and was not permitted to question. Small true thing: he went out saying it was the
sweetest part of losing, that he wouldn't be about to hear her gloat, and grinning like
the winning hand himself. I have recorded the grin. I stand by the grin.

**GAMMER THIST.** Still waiting, and in tremendous spirits about it, which I did not
expect to be the honest note here and am recording anyway, standards being standards.
Small true thing: she has asked me twice today whether I intend to put "I WON" in the
ledger in letters a hand high. I have explained that this is not how the ledger works.
She has explained that she does not care how the ledger works. We have reached no
agreement. I record the disagreement, in the interest of completeness, and because it
was the first thing all week that made me laugh out loud at the landing, which felt
almost indecent, and then did not.

**SEDGE FENNER.** Crossed. First grave dug in the marsh in three hundred years — I did
not attend, Sallow needed the ferry and someone has to keep it, but I wrote the name in
by lantern-light the same evening, in the new column, with the day counted beside it,
and it seemed to me that mattered nearly as much as standing at the graveside would
have, though I would not say that to a mourner's face.

**Ambient note, several days on:** *The willows have dropped leaf. I am told this is
called autumn, by those old enough to remember the word, and that it happens every year,
somewhere, to people who have never once had to be told what it was. I have added
"AUTUMN (first)" to the top of this page as a heading, the way other books mark a new
chapter. It felt like the kind of thing a ledger ought to notice.*

**GAFFER CORLIN.** Crossed, some days after the window, gentle in his sleep, three
nights before his household knew it. Bettony Marsh sent word down to the landing by a
neighbour, for the book, in her own words exactly, which I set down here unaltered
because they did not want improving: *"Tell the ferry he's not late for once in his
life, and tell it I said that, because he would have wanted the joke on record before
the sadness was."* I have recorded both. I could not, in honesty, have separated them if
asked.

*  *  *

Sallow's own words, this evening, poling in with the lanterns doubled on the water
behind him, recorded as near verbatim as I am able, my memory for his dry remarks being
tolerably good after some years of practising it:

*"Marsh is emptying out around me by the day, Tarn, and do you know — I find I'm rather
looking forward to my own turn, when it comes round fair at the back of the queue, where
it belongs. Not rushing at it. Somebody's to carry the last of them over first, and I'll
not leave that to you on your own. But I'll take it gladly, when it's mine. I've earned
a good long nothing."*

I have written this down twice, once as he said it and once underneath, smaller, so that
if the ink of the first ever fades there will still be the second. I do not entirely
trust my own hand to hold something that important only the once.

*  *  *

**Last entry this book, for now.**

**THE ONE WHO OPENED THE DOOR.** No name given, none asked for, none, I think, offered
lightly to anyone. Came through the landing quiet, did what needed doing at the heart of
the marsh, and then stayed on through the whole emptying and went only when there was
nobody left who still needed the door held — taking such thanks as were pressed on her,
and I saw two of them given, with the faint alarm of a woman who suspects gratitude of
being a large dog that might jump up. The small white dog with her was bowed to at every
door she passed, as is proper, though not one person doing the bowing could have told
you why, myself very much included. Small true thing, the truest I have: she is the
reason every entry above this one has a right-hand column at all.

**Note to self, and I mean to keep this one, standards or no standards:** *The book is
very near finishing what it was for. There will be a last name in it eventually, and
then no more waiting-names to add, only crossed ones, and then, I expect, not even
those, once there is nobody left at the landing who has not already gone over. A ledger
that has run out of names to keep is a ledger that has done its whole job.*

*I find I am going to keep writing in it anyway. Somebody ought to remember they were
here. That was always the whole of the reason, and it has not stopped being true just
because the waiting has.*


---

# Chapter Fifteen — The Perfect Measure

The road out of the Stillmarsh went east along the water, and for the first mile or so
the water went with it — real water now, with a current in it, murmuring over root and
stone the way it must have murmured before anyone thought to stop it. The willows along
the bank had begun letting go of their leaves. She had never seen a leaf let go of
anything before. She stood and watched one turn over twice in the air and land, gold side
up, on the moving surface, and be carried off, and could not for the life of her explain
why it made her chest hurt in the specific, generous way it did.

*That's the first autumn this world's had in three hundred years,* said the Querent, at
her shoulder, quieter than usual. *Get a good look. There'll be more of it, but there's
only one first.*

She had not gone very far before she found the funeral.

It was nothing like what the word had made her brace for. A dozen people stood at the
water's edge outside a low turf-roofed house, in no particular order, in their ordinary
clothes, and a plain box of new pale wood sat on two trestles among them, and a woman was
saying a name — just the name, said plainly, the way you'd hail someone across a green —
and then not saying anything else, and letting the silence stand in for the rest of it. A
cup went hand to hand along the little crowd, and each person took a swallow and passed it
on, and nobody wept extravagantly, and nobody was silent about their crying either. It was,
more than anything, like watching a barn go up: a great many hands doing an old task
together because the task had, at long last, come due.

She stood well back with Pip pressed to her boot and did not go closer, because it was not
hers to go closer to, and after a while the box went into the ground and the dozen people
began, unhurriedly, to talk about the dead man's terrible taste in hats.

*They're not sad the way you'd think a thing that new would be sad,* the Querent said.
*Give it time. Grief needs practice same as anything, and they've three hundred years of
none to make up. They'll get better at it. Or worse. I've genuinely never been able to
tell which is the mercy.*

She walked on, and did not ask who the man had been, because she understood that it was
not a story owed to a passing stranger, only witnessed by one. Eyes open, she thought.
That was the whole of what she had promised Mortimer, on the black water, with the
candles unmoving all around them. She meant to keep it walking, too.

The road bent south along a wider channel, and somewhere in the space of a hundred yards
the leaves stopped falling.

She only noticed because she was watching for the next one. The willows on this new bank
stood in the same wind-combed shapes as the ones behind her — but they were shapes, not
motions, held the way a carving holds a wave. The current under the bridge ahead of her
ran on exactly as before, bright and busy, and yet something about it had gone the
particular kind of still that had nothing to do with stillness: a held thing wearing the
coat of a moving one. Pip's ears went up. He had felt it too, a beat before she did.

She had walked out of one held breath and directly into another. The Confluence had not
finished dying yet. She was here to finish it.

*  *  *

The bridge stopped two-thirds of the way across the channel, in a clean lip of dressed
stone with a bucket of mortar beside it gone hard as the rock it was meant to have joined.
Scaffolding stood against the drop, weathered to silver, its lashings gone to stone
themselves. A plank walkway, added later by somebody with no faith whatever in the
original plan, carried the rest of the crossing on a nervous diagonal.

Pip went out onto the warm dressed stone, sniffed the dead mortar bucket with the grave
attention of a coroner, and sat down at the edge of the planking and refused, with total
dignity, to go one step further until she went first.

"Coward," she told him, not unkindly, and went first.

*Three hundred years of nearly,* the Querent murmured, once they were both across. *You'll
find they're rather proud of it here. Watch for the pride. It's a better clue than the
sadness.*

Past the bridge the city opened out along narrow stone wharves — mixing-houses thrown open
to the water, steam and cold mist trading places over a hundred small braziers and
ice-cellars, none of it moving faster than a held thought. On a public bench beside the
water sat an old man tending a small pot on its own stand, unhurried, entirely
unbothered, with the specific contentment of someone who has all the time there has ever
been and knows precisely what he means to do with none of it.

He looked up as she came near — pleased, not startled, the way you're pleased by a slow
and welcome thing you've been expecting.

"You've the look of the one they're whispering about," he said, before she'd got a word
out. "The one who *finishes* things." He nodded at the bench beside him, unbothered, as
if he'd made his peace with the sentence the moment he'd finished saying it. "Sit anyway.
There's time. There's always been time — that's the trouble with it."

She sat. Pip inspected the underside of the bench and, finding it satisfactory, lay down
across both their feet.

"This pot's been steeping three hundred years," the old man said, "and I'll tell you
plain — it has never once been better than it is right now. Another minute, mind. Always
another minute. Steep it a breath too long and you've *ruined* it, haven't you. Poured it
out and it's only tea."

"So you never drink it?"

"Drink it? And end it?" He looked at her as though she'd suggested something faintly
indecent. "No, no. You keep it *nearly*. That's the whole art of the delta, that is — the
nearly. The bridges, the blends, the great pour itself. All of it, forever on the very
edge of done and never once tipped over. Perfect. Perfectly held."

He said *perfect* the way a man says it about a grief he has decided, with considerable
effort, to be proud of instead.

*There it is,* said the Querent, low, once he'd settled back to his pot. *Nobody down here
thinks it's a tragedy. That's the tell, little Excuse — when the stuck ones are *pleased*
about it. Go on down. She's at the heart of it, pouring, and everything between here and
her is going to try to teach you why she can't stop.*

*  *  *

Delphine Marchetti's mixing-house stood open-fronted on a low wharf, its shelves ranked
with copper and glass, and the moment the Fool came within sight of it she understood the
noise she'd been hearing for the last fifty yards was one woman losing an argument with
two teacups.

One cup stood scalding, steam rolling off it in ropes. One stood frosted white, cold
vapour spilling over the lip and pooling on the counter. Between them, sleeves shoved up,
both hands full and clearly losing whichever of them she wasn't holding, stood a young
apprentice glaring at the pair of them like a woman refereeing a fight she had somehow
also been drafted into.

"They say you're finishing things, region to region." Delphine didn't look up; the cold
cup had her whole attention. "Well. Finish *this* one for me first, would you — a small
finish, before the big one. Practice." A short, nervous laugh went with it, the kind that
knows exactly what it's asking.

"Right." Delphine wiped one wrist across her forehead, achieving nothing. "Here's the
whole of it. Hot ruins cold, cold ruins hot, and everyone in this city will tell you the
trick is to hold them apart forever and admire the *tension*." She said it flatly, like a
woman quoting scripture she'd stopped believing years ago. "The tension is not drinkable.
I have tried to drink the tension. Do not."

"So what's the actual trick?"

"You don't hold them apart. You pour them *into each other* — a touch, at the right
beat, so each one takes the edge off the other, and the middle you're after opens up in
the gap. Temper them. Conduct them." She looked up at last, and there was something
almost pleading under the exasperation. "Stop refereeing and start *steering*."

"Show me how to steer it."

Delphine's whole face changed, the way a face changes when a request it had stopped
hoping for finally lands. "Watch my hands. Hot into cold on the cold's rising beat —
there — and cold back before it scalds. Feel it?"

She did not, the first time. She scalded her sleeve and made a noise she would be
embarrassed about for two days, and Pip gave her a look of profound professional
disappointment. The second attempt caught the beat a half-count late and the frost cup
fogged over and spat. The third time she stopped watching the cups and watched the rhythm
between them instead — the small held breath each pour took before it turned — and put
the hot into the cold exactly as it rose, and felt the strange satisfaction of two things
that wanted to ruin each other agreeing, instead, to carry one another a little way.

On the third clean round the two cups met in the middle and held there, steaming and
frosting in a slow, ordinary balance, and a third cup began filling itself in the gap
between them — pale, plain, entirely unremarkable tea.

Delphine let out a breath she had plainly been holding for weeks.

"*There.* Look at it. Perfectly ordinary tea." She pressed the back of one wrist to her
eyes, half laughing. "I could cry, honestly. Nobody's made an ordinary anything in this
delta since the Stall. Keep that in your hands — the steering, I mean. You'll know it
again when you feel it."

"Whether you'd have learned it or not," the Querent said, once they were back on the
wharf, "that's the shape of the whole delta in one cup. Steering, not scrambling. Remember
it or don't — the one at the bottom is going to ask you either way."

"I'll remember," she said, and found, to her own faint surprise, that she meant it as more
than manners.

*  *  *

To reach the heart of the delta the road ran out through the open channels, where the two
rivers ran close enough to throw their tempers into the open air. It did not flood with
water here. It flooded with weather that had nowhere else to be: bands of scalding mist
rolling off the hot river in slow, unvarying pulses, bands of frost-spray sheeting off the
cold one on the offbeat, and between them, for a breath at a time, a clear neutral lane
that opened and closed like something breathing in its sleep.

Pip went across in short confident dashes, waiting out each pulse on the reed-islands with
his tail going, entirely unbothered, as though he had made this exact crossing more times
than anybody had thought to count.

She found the rhythm on the second island and kept it after that — scald, gap, frost, gap
— stepping into each clear lane the instant it opened rather than lunging for it, the way
Delphine's cups had taught her hands to think.

*Feel that?* the Querent said, somewhere in the mist. *She's not trying to catch you.
She's just pouring, and the pouring has edges. Learn the edges and the middle's always
open. Fight the edges and the middle closes on you.*

She crossed dry, if not entirely warm, and came up the far bank onto the last approach to
the plaza — the great half-bridge itself, largest of the Confluence's unfinished spans,
reaching out toward the twin cups and stopping, as everything here stopped, one clean lip
short of arriving.

*  *  *

At the very end of the laid stone, on his knees with a trowel, a bridge-mason was setting
a single new stone into mortar that would never set enough to finish and never crumble
enough to fail.

He rose as she came on, wiping his hands on a rag that had given up being a rag some
decades back, and planted himself — gently, immovably — between her and the plaza beyond.

"I know what you are now," he said. "Word's come up the delta ahead of you. I know
exactly what *finished* is going to mean, in the end — for the bridge, for the city, for
everything." He looked at her steadily. "I'm going to ask you anyway."

She waited. He seemed to need the waiting more than he needed anything she could say.

"They'll tell you the bridge is a tragedy. Half a bridge, three hundred years, never
joins the far bank. Terrible, they say, and go on drinking their nearly-tea." He shook his
head. "It was never the tragedy. It was the *work*. I have had three hundred years of
good work, and a stone to lay every single morning, and hands that knew what to do with
the day."

"You don't want it finished."

"I know it's coming." His voice did not rise; it only went quieter, and more exact. "I
know what you are, and I know a finished thing is a done thing, and a done thing's the
start of the long goodbye — I've done the sums. I'm not asking you to spare the world.
I'm asking you to let me not-know, one more morning. That's all."

He looked down at the stone under his knees as though it might answer for him.

"A finished bridge is a bridge you walk across and never think of again. I'd sooner have
the laying of it than the crossing." He said it quietly. "But you've the look of someone
the delta's been waiting for. So I'll not stand in your road. I only wanted somebody to
know it wasn't nothing, what I had. Will you remember that it wasn't nothing?"

"I'll remember," she said, and it cost her a fraction more, this second time, to say it
plain.

"Then that's more than I hoped for. Off you go." He almost smiled. "And — thank you, for
the hearing of it."

He stepped aside himself, and sat back down at his stone, and did not watch her go. Behind
her, the sound of a trowel resumed — patient, even, a man getting one more morning of the
only thing he knew how to do.

"Keep hold of that one," the Querent said, gentler than she'd heard it in a long while.
"You'll meet a great many who beg to be let finish. He's the other kind. There's always
the other kind, and the world doesn't ask them before it turns."

*  *  *

The plaza was not so much a place as the inside of a pour. Two colossal cups tilted toward
one another overhead, cathedral-tall, and between them, astride the meeting of the hot
river and the cold, stood the Mixer — serene, enormous, unhurried, a jug of scald in one
hand and a jug of frost in the other, pouring one into the other and back in a figure that
had not broken its rhythm in three hundred years. The floor of the plaza was the
meeting-water itself, held in bands: a stripe of steaming scald, a stripe of clear, a
stripe of rimed frost, cycling slow and beautiful in time with her pour.

She did not stop pouring as the Fool came into the light. She inclined her head, and her
voice, when it came, was calm as level water — the calm of a hand that could not afford,
not for one instant, to shake.

"Ah. The unbalanced element." Scald into frost; frost into scald. "I know what your
presence means. I have measured it precisely — you finish things, and a finished thing is
a poured-out thing, and a poured-out thing is *over*. I have spent three hundred years
making certain nothing here is ever over."

"Doesn't your arm get tired?"

"Tired." The pour wavered — a hair, no more — and recovered. "I cannot afford to be
tired. That is rather the point." A stillness came into her voice that was worse than any
raising of it could have been. "There is no tired. There is only the measure, and the
measure is perfect, and it will go on being perfect. You are the one thing in it that does
not balance. So. Let us see if you can be blended in — or poured away."

*  *  *

The flood came first. The plaza swept with alternating bands of scald and frost in time
with her jugs, and the old animal urge to run, to scramble for cover, put the Fool square
in the path of whichever pulse was next; she learned that inside the first minute, the
hard way, with her whole left side singing from the frost.

She stopped running. She started counting the beat the way Delphine's cups had taught her
to count it, and the clear lanes — which had never once been trying to catch her, only
pouring, the way water pours — held long enough to move, and breathe, and close the
distance.

"Do you see?" the Mixer said, unhurried, over the pour. "You cannot out-run a measure. You
can only mistime it. Be still. Be *balanced*." A beat, watching her hold the rhythm.
"...No? A pity."

Then came the part the whole delta had been teaching her, without once saying so: not to
dodge the pours, but to turn them into each other. She caught a scald as it swept and
redirected it into the path of the coming frost, and felt, for the second time that day,
two things that wanted to ruin each other agree instead to cancel — and in the gap where
they tempered, plain and clear and undefended, stood the Mixer.

"No — that is not —" The first pour turned against her, and for the first time in three
hundred years her voice was not quite calm. "You don't *pour them together*, they're meant
to be *held apart*, that is the entire — " A jug tipped a fraction wrong. "— the entire
measure —"

Pressed again, and again, conducted rather than fought, her pour finally missed its beat.
Only once. A thread of scald that should have met its frost overshot and fell, harmless,
into the clear — the first uneven moment the Confluence had held in three hundred years.
Both great cups, for a single held heartbeat, stopped.

The Mixer looked down at where the drop had fallen wrong, jugs hanging forgotten in her
hands, and the Fool crossed the stilled water between them and did not raise the Bindle at
all. She closed the last of the distance and laid a hand over the Mixer's own, steadying
it, and stopped the pour with nothing but that.

*  *  *

The office cracked. It did not shatter like glass or crumble like stone; it came apart
cleanly, once, straight down the middle, with the small final sound of a dropped cup
meeting a stone floor.

"It fell." The Mixer stared at the water where the drop had gone. "A drop of it *fell*.
Three hundred years and I never once — " Her voice, for the first time, was unsteady. "Oh.
*Oh.* I remember being tired. I had forgotten I was allowed."

Something surfaced in her the way a held pour finally lets go — not an injury. A name,
coming up quiet and whole.

"...Averil."

She said it once, tasting it, and the office fell away from around the word the way the
two halves of a cup come apart in the hands.

"Averil. Yes. That's — that was my name, before it was a measure. Before I was the thing
that holds." She looked at her own two hands, marvelling. "I've been pouring so long I
forgot there was a somebody doing the pouring."

The last of the scald and the last of the frost ran out of the two jugs together into the
clear, and tempered, and were only water, and the jugs were empty. She went on holding
them, one in each hand, the way a woman holds her own hands after a lifetime of not being
able to look up from her work.

"Empty. I don't believe they've been empty since — " she couldn't find the year — "since
ever. Since the very start of it." She laughed, wet and astonished. "They're so *light*."

Still holding both empty jugs, she tipped the card out of the air the way she had once
tipped a pour — no free hand for a flourish, none needed — and offered it across, resting
on the lip of one jug.

"You finish things. I know. I measured you the moment you crossed the plank — and I find,
now the measure's broken, that I don't mind being one of them." A small, unsteady laugh.
"Perhaps the mercy was always in the finishing. I only ever saw the pouring-out."

She turned the card over once, as though checking her own work.

"Trump the Fourteenth. Blend, if anyone writes it down. Two things that ruin each other,
made to carry each other instead — that was always the whole of my craft. Take it. I've
held the balance long enough. Somebody ought to get to *use* it, instead of just *being*
it."

The Fool took it, and felt it settle into the coat with the rest of them, changing nothing
that was already where it belonged — a small private weight, exact.

*  *  *

With the pour stopped, the two great cups finished tipping at last, the final measure of
three hundred years running out between them, down into the delta, toward a sea it had
been three centuries late reaching. The water that had run banded scald and frost ran
clear now, ordinary, unbothered by any argument at all. It was just a river, arriving.

"There it goes," the Querent said. "Not a flood — the opposite. The plumbing finally
finishing." A pause, watching the current go by, honest all the way down. "Three hundred
years the Stall held that water on the very lip of the sea, and now it just... arrives."

Over the hours and days that followed — and she stayed near, and watched, because some
things deserved watching all the way through — the delta pulled back. Mudflats that had
been channel-bottom firmed into grassland, green coming up through the silt with the speed
of a held breath finally let go. Rooftops surfaced glittering and dripping out of the
draining water, a whole street of a city that had gone under before the Stall and had
simply been waiting, the way everything here had been waiting, to be allowed to arrive at
something.

The old man at the benches had a new pot going when she passed him again, and he was
talking to it in a completely different tone.

"The pour's *finished*," he told her, before she'd asked. "Do you know what that means? It
means I can start a new pot. A whole new pot, and steep it, and *drink* it." He looked at
his own hands, faintly stunned. "I don't know that I remember how you end a cup of tea.
I'm going to find out."

She found Elgin Thatch on his own bridge, days on, and it was not a half-bridge any
longer. The far bank had risen to meet the laid stone while the water fell, and the span
simply reached the other side now, as if it had always meant to, as if three hundred
years had been only a slow afternoon. Travellers crossed it without a second thought. He
stood at its centre, off his knees, trowel still in his belt, not working, looking at the
far bank his bridge had finally touched, not knowing what to do with his hands.

"It joined," he said, the way a man says a word in a language he's forgetting. "The far
bank came up and it just... joined. No stone from me. The water did it. Three hundred years
of my mornings and in the end the *water* finished it, in an afternoon, while I wasn't
looking."

"It's a good bridge, Elgin."

"It's a *finished* bridge." He took a long breath. "That's the thing of it. Nobody'll ever
watch it being built again. Nobody'll ever lay the morning stone." He wasn't angry; he was
bereft, and gentle about it. "I'm not sorry you did what you did, understand. The world
had to turn. I just — I mourn the *finishing* of it. Not the work. The end of the work. Is
that a foolish thing to grieve?"

"What will you build now?"

"Something that can be *finished*, I suppose. Terrifying thought." He put his hand on the
trowel at his belt and did not take it out. "What I'd want is a bridge that stays half-done,
to practise on." Something came most of the way up to a laugh and stopped short of it.
"There aren't any left."

"It wasn't nothing."

He looked at her a long moment.

"No," he said. "It wasn't nothing. You remembered. That's — aye. That's the whole of what
I asked for, isn't it. Thank you."

He didn't undo anything. He didn't ask her to. He sat down on the finished span, in the
middle of it, and looked at the far bank for a good long while, and she left him to it,
because that too was not hers to interrupt.

At Delphine's bench there was a queue — a real one, people waiting with cups held out, and
Delphine moving down the line with a cork in one hand and a look of someone drunk on
ordinary competence.

"I finished four orders before noon," she said, catching sight of the Fool. "*Finished*
them. Poured out, corked, done, next. I nearly wept over the first cork." Then, quieter, a
real laugh only a little frightened under it: "You finished the biggest thing in the delta
so I could finish a cup of tea. Doesn't seem a fair trade for you, whatever comes at the
end of your road. But I'll take my finished cups and be grateful. Someone should be."

*  *  *

She came at last to the old wharf overlook, where the whole drained delta lay out below
her — mudbanks gone to grass, the risen rooftops of the old city catching what light there
was, the two great empty cups tilted quiet above it all, and Averil somewhere down among
the new-dry streets with two empty jugs and, for the first time in three hundred
years, nothing left to hold.

The light was going long and low over the water, by habit if not by any sun that had
troubled itself to move — the Confluence kept its hours the way it always had, by candle
and kettle and appetite, and would a while yet — but there was a real chill coming up off
the newly bare ground now, the first she'd felt down here, and she pulled her collar up
against it and did not mind it at all.

"There," said the Querent. "Now it's finished." A beat, watching the old rooftops glitter
under the lowering light. "Doesn't that feel *dreadful*."

She waited. She had learned, by now, to wait through that particular silence.

"And don't mistake me making a joke of it. You know by now what finishing is, region to
region, the whole slow shape of it. She got her ending early, and gently, and I called it
dreadful, and I meant every letter." A pause longer than any that had come before it.
"Every last one of them gets one, little Excuse. Including the big one. Including you."

She sat with that a while, the way she'd sat with the tree on the plateau, the way she'd
sat across a ring of candles from the kindest man in the world. She did not ask what it
meant, exactly, because she thought she already knew, and eyes open had never once meant
she had to like what she saw. It only meant she wasn't going to look away from it.

Fourteen cards now, warm in the coat over her heart. Six petals on the rose at her belt.
Behind her, the delta settled into its first true evening, and somewhere down in it a man
sat on a finished bridge, and a woman corked her first honest bottle, and an old man
steeped a pot he actually intended to drink.

Ahead, west, past the last of the drained mudflats, the land began to rise — a long dry
shoulder of hill climbing toward something she couldn't make out yet in the low light,
except that it went down rather than up at the last of it: a dark mouth cut into the
ground, a gate, waiting the way everything here had learned to wait, except that this one,
she understood without being told, had never once been stuck. It had simply always been
underground, and always would be, whichever way the Reading turned.

Far off, past hill and gate both, the Axis sat small and silver at the centre of
everything it could see, and did not glitter so much this evening as hold very, very
still, watching, the way it always did, whatever came next.

She whistled up the dog. The first cold evening air of the world's first autumn moved
against her face — real air, going somewhere, the way air was meant to — and she turned
west, toward the gate down into the earth, and walked on into it.


---

# Chapter Sixteen — Terms and Conditions

The road west out of the Confluence had learned, in about a fortnight, how to be autumn.

The sun had not moved to permit it — the sun over the Spread sat exactly where it had sat
for three hundred years — but the hedges had gone rust and copper along their tops, and
every so often a handful of fallen leaves would come up off the verge in a gust and go past
her ear, and Pip would spin round after them with the outrage of a professional whose yard
has been interfered with.

Then the country began to lean west, and somewhere past a line of thorn the wind stopped.

Not died down. *Stopped* — the way a sound stops when a door shuts on it. One stride the
grass was moving and the next it stood exactly as the last breeze had left it, holding the
shape like a portrait of itself. She had crossed this seam fifteen times now. It got worse
every time, not better. That is what happens when you learn what a thing means.

*Fifteenth,* said the Querent, in her ear. *And still the same little slap in the face,
isn't it. Good. I should worry about you if it ever stopped being one.*

Ahead, the western hills went up in long bare shoulders under an evening that was going
nowhere: the light out here had been six o'clock since before anybody's grandmother, low
and gold-brown across the gorse, every shadow lying the same way and never once creeping.
It was the loveliest hour in the world, and it had been that hour for three centuries.

And in the fold of two of those hills was a stair.

*  *  *

It went down and kept going: cut stone, four abreast, the handrail worn to a shine by an
enormous number of hands. Out of the throat of it came a light that was not daylight,
rising the way heat rises — warm, low, the colour of butter and old brandy — and with it a
smell of coin and beeswax and something sweet underneath that she could not name and was
not sure she wanted to.

Across the head of the stair, absurdly, hung a velvet rope: crimson, gold-tasselled, frayed
where three centuries of sleeves had gone past it — the sort a theatre keeps at the door so
that the queue may feel chosen. It could have been stepped over by a determined toddler.
Nobody, she suspected, ever had.

Growing at the very lip of the stairwell, where the last of the gold light fell across the
top step and stopped, was a wild white rose — the last living growing thing between her and
the dark.

She crouched to it. She had six petals at her belt by then, and she had learned that a
grafting is not a thing you *take*, exactly. She was slow about the knife. She apologised
out loud, which she did every time and never once mentioned to me afterwards, and then she
bound the cutting in at her own Rose's stem and held her thumb over it until it stopped
being two things. The seventh petal came up out of the white about as fast as a person
swallows.

*There,* said the Querent, quietly. *That's the last one that'll grow for you for a while.
Nothing grows down there, little Excuse — not for want of soil. They simply found they
didn't miss it, and stopped paying for the window.* A pause. *Mind the last of the sky on
your way past. They don't keep any of it below.*

Away east across the whole width of the world the Axis sat small and silver and perfectly
still, with something turning at the middle of it. Then she stepped past the velvet rope
and went down, and Pip went at heel — game as ever, but keeping one ear turned back on her
the whole way, the way a good dog minds a companion in a stranger's house.

*  *  *

The stair let out onto a balcony over the whole of it, and everyone who arrives at that
balcony stops.

It is a pit: a well of a city dropping away in tier after tier, balconies and
counting-houses and private boxes going round and down and round and down into a warm gold
dark with no floor you can find, every rail and lintel worked in gold leaf gone soft at the
corners the way gold does when it is loved and never replaced.

There is no sky. That is not a poetic remark: it is the one region in the whole Spread with
a lid on it. What it has instead is gaslight — thousands of jets in brass brackets going up
the walls in constellations, every flame standing perfectly, mathematically still. No
draught. No flutter. Three hundred years of open flame and not one of them leaning.

She noticed that before she noticed the people, who were everywhere, and busy, and
*cheerful*. From their wrists and ankles and belt-loops, catching the light in warm little
strings all the way down, hung chains — fine as jewellery, the kind of gold links a person
might be given at a wedding.

*The Undervault,* said the Querent. *Every chain you can see, somebody asked for. Signed
for. Haggled the price of, a few of them, and came away feeling they'd got the better end.
There's no lie anywhere in this place, little Excuse. Not one, top to bottom. That's what
makes it the worst room in the whole Spread.*

*  *  *

The topmost tier was a receiving-floor with a queue on it, and the queue was in a good
mood. A woman with a ledger at her belt stepped out of it to let the Fool by, and mistook
her — warmly, immediately — for somebody come down to sign.

"New down here? Oh, you'll do well." She had a slim gold chain from her wrist that ran off
and fastened to nothing in particular. "Everyone does well down here. That's rather the
point of the place."

Pip sat down and looked at the chain. Pip had opinions.

"Aren't you trapped?"

"*Trapped!*" She laughed, and it was a kind laugh, not a defensive one, which is the whole
difficulty of the place. "Dear, the door's right there." Her hand went over her own wrist
as she said it, a half-second too fast, and stayed there. "I've simply nowhere I'd rather
be. That's not a trap. That's a *home*."

"Do you ever miss the sky?"

The woman opened her mouth to answer, and did not. It went on a good deal longer than a
pause has any business going on.

"...You know," she said at last, "nobody's asked me that in years." She frowned, the way
you frown at a sum that will not come out. "No. No, I don't think I do." And then, almost
to herself: "Isn't that a strange thing to be sure of."

Then she nodded the Fool onward and went back to her ledger, at peace inside four seconds.

*She isn't lying,* said the Querent, without a grain of dryness in it. *Not one word. Hold
on to how that feels. You'll want to remember what an honest answer sounds like before you
get to the desk.*

*  *  *

The descent ran past a row of private boxes, each lit like a little stage, each holding
somebody living inside a bargain already struck.

In the first, a thin grey man sat at a card table that dealt itself. As she drew level the
table turned him a hand, and he looked at it with the flat calm of a man for whom nothing
has been uncertain in a very long time, and won.

"I traded my luck for a sure thing," said Merrow Slate, without looking up. "Now I win
every hand and I've forgotten what a *good* hand felt like." He gathered the cards. "Don't
pity me. I'd sign it again tomorrow."

In the second, a Cups-suit woman sat with her hands folded and her eyes shut and her mouth
moving, conducting a silence as though it were an aria she had written.

"I traded my voice for quiet." No sound came out of her; the words simply arrived, the way
a smell arrives. "You've no idea how loud a life gets, love. I've never slept so well."
A small pleased breath. "Never sung again either. Small price."

In the third, a Wands-suit woman rocked an empty cradle with one foot, at perfect ease.

"I traded a grief for the memory of it." She was smiling, and it was a real smile, and that
was the whole of the horror. "Now I remember loving her and I don't remember losing her."
The cradle went on going. "It's the kindest thing anyone ever sold me."

Pip pressed against the Fool's boot and stayed there.

*Every one of those is a true story with the price paid in full,* said the Querent, low.
*Not lies — terms. And the awful part isn't that they were cheated, little Excuse. The
awful part is that they weren't. Death gave endings away for nothing at all; this one
charges, and prints the bill, and hands you a copy for your records.*

*  *  *

The last room before the desk was a counting-office no bigger than a scullery, with one
standing contract pinned open under a reading-lamp and a door on the far side that did not
open.

She read it. Three paragraphs of ordinary print about the storage of grain, and it made no
sense at all, and it took her a while to see why: beneath the flourish at the bottom, in a
smaller hand, ran four more lines set the wrong way round. Mirror-script. Not hidden —
nothing here was ever hidden; only small, and backward, and down where a person's eye
stops.

The Veil had taught her the habit, months back, in a house of pillars where she kept a
woman's secret and never told a soul including me: the habit of letting a page be read *at*
you, sideways, patiently, until it gives up. She took the lamp off its hook and turned the
flame and the glass and her own head about until the backward lines came round the right
way.

They said what the big print said. Exactly, without a grain of difference — only
completely, and with the cost in it.

*There,* said the Querent. *That's the whole of him in one room. It isn't hidden. It's just*
small, *and backward, and nobody down here has ever once held it up to a light. You did.
Keep the habit. You'll want it in about thirty seconds.*

The door opened.

*  *  *

The lowest tier was the grandest: black-and-gold marble under the whole ringed city, every
balcony above it full of contented people leaning on gilded rails to watch. And at the
centre of it, where you would have expected a throne, there was a desk — mahogany, green
leather, an inkwell, a fair stack of blank contracts squared true at the corners, and one
pen laid across them at a considerate angle for a right-handed guest.

The man behind it rose the exact correct amount: trim, unhurried, dressed like somebody who
has never once in his life been cold, with a face built by whatever builds faces for the
specific purpose of putting people at their ease. It worked on her, and she knew what he
was.

At the foot of the dais Pip stopped dead. His hackles came up along his whole back and he
growled — once, low, from somewhere under the floor of him — a sound she had never heard
him make at anything in the entire world. Not at the Blanks. Not at the Bataleur. Not at
the lion. She glanced down; he settled, watchful, deeply unhappy, and stayed where he was.

"There you are." Old Nick Lowry gestured at the chair on her side. "Do sit — no? Stand,
then. A standing guest drives a harder bargain, and I've no objection to a hard bargain. I
so rarely get a *new* one." He came round the desk far enough to be sociable and not one
inch further. "You'll have seen the residents on your way down. Happy, every one. Hold that
in mind, because you're about to assume I did something dreadful to them, and I'd rather
have the assumption gone before we talk." He said it pleasantly. He said everything
pleasantly. "I never did a single thing to anybody in this city that wasn't written down
first, read aloud, and signed atop. I don't cheat. I don't lie. I've never had the need.
The truth closes more deals than a lie ever managed — that's the one secret of the trade,
and I'll give it you for free."

"And if I've come to fight you?"

"Oh, we'll get there. We always get there." He sounded genuinely warm about it. "But you
came down a very long stair, and it would be *rude* of me not to offer you terms first.
Three of them. All honest. All yours to take or leave." He turned the top three contracts
face-up and squared them with two fingers. "Read every word. I'll not stop you. I never
stop anyone. Stopping people is the only thing in this business that would actually be
dishonest."

And here — once, and I shall not do it again — I leaned close and said the thing I say when
I cannot help myself:

*Mind how you sign, little Excuse. You and I both know there's a hand on the far side of
you doing the actual choosing, and it does so love a good bargain.* A dry beat. *He knows
it too. Why else do you suppose the pen's laid out that way round.*

*  *  *

The first contract read, in plain print, in words no smaller than any of the others:

*THE COIN CLAUSE. The signatory's fortune shall run richer for the coming engagement —
Fortune generated increased by one half, for its duration. In consideration whereof: an
honest cut of the coin now carried on the signatory's person shall pass to the house,
counted and taken at signing, and shall not be returned by any outcome, win or lose. The
sum is real. The loss is permanent.*

"Is the coin ever refunded?"

"Never." He looked delighted to be asked. "Not if you win, not if you lose, not if you
weep. It says so, there, in the ordinary-sized words. I do like to keep the important terms
*large*."

She thought about it properly, because she knew what Fortune bought her in a bad minute:
the lantern up, the world going slow, the extra breath between a mistake and its
consequence. A purse is only a purse.

"No," she said.

"Sensible." He drew it back without a flicker. "Keep your coin. It fights better in your
pocket than it ever did in mine, most likely."

The second he slid forward without lingering on it, which was its own sort of tell.

*THE FLESH CLAUSE. For the coming engagement the signatory's White Rose shall blossom whole
and carry one petal beyond its natural span — a fuller measure of mending than the Rose was
ever grown to hold. In consideration whereof: one petal of the Rose's utmost capacity shall
be surrendered to the house at signing. The Rose bears one petal fewer thereafter. This is
not a loan.*

Her hand went to her belt before she could stop it. Seven petals, an hour old, the newest
of them cut out of the last light on the last stair.

"Why do you want a petal?"

He opened his mouth to answer, and then — the only time all evening it happened — he did
not.

"...Do you know," said Old Nick Lowry, "I've stopped asking myself that." He turned the
inkwell a quarter turn. "I collect what people will part with. The Rose parts easier than
most. That ought to tell you something, and it never does."

"Is my life worth one petal?"

"*That,*" he said, "is the only question in this room worth asking, and it's the one clause
I'll not answer for you. You'll price your own petals, or you'll leave them be. Either's an
honest choice."

She left them be. He took the contract back and said, "Keep the petal. It was never much to
me and it's everything to you. Odd, how often that's the way of a price" — and she has told
me since that of everything said at that desk, it was the sentence she thought about
longest.

Then he slid the third forward, more gently than the other two, and something moved behind
the ease in his face. Not guilt; he hasn't the equipment for guilt. Something adjacent — a
craftsman's respect for a cut that lands.

At the foot of the dais, Pip growled again.

*THE COMPANIONSHIP CLAUSE. For the coming engagement a second shall stand at the
signatory's side — a spectral echo bearing the likeness of the signatory's Present-slot
Trump, casting as it casts, for the whole of the fight. In consideration whereof: the white
dog waits beyond the gate, closed for the engagement's duration. The dog is not harmed, not
held, not touched. He simply waits outside, and cannot be called, and can be heard.*

"I'll be plain, since plain is all I do," said Old Nick. "That one hurts. Not the dog — the
dog's fine, the dog will be *bored*, which for a creature like that is nearly an insult. It
hurts *you*. That's the term. I don't touch him. I don't need to. I only shut a gate, and
you fight the biggest fight of your life listening to him wait for you on the other side of
it." He let it sit exactly as long as it needed. "Everything printed. Nothing hidden. The
cruelty's entirely in the arithmetic."

"He'll wait the whole time?"

"Every second. Right at the gate. He won't understand it and he won't stop." Old Nick
spread his hands. "Dogs don't sign, you see. It's the one contract I've never once got a
paw to." A small, entirely genuine smile. "Believe me, I've offered."

And she imagined it — precisely, once, the way you only ever imagine a thing you are about
to refuse. A shut gate of gilded iron, brandy-coloured light on the far side, and the
noise. Not barking; he would bark for a minute and then stop, because barking is for
telling somebody something and there would be nobody on that side to tell. What came after
was the part she saw clearly: the click of one small dog turning on the flags and turning
back, the scrape of a paw at the bottom of a gate that does not move, and a whine gone thin
and workmanlike and patient, over and over, from a creature who had never once doubted she
was coming back — while she stood forty feet off with a chain round her ankle and heard
every note of it, and could not make his name mean *wait, I'm here, it's nearly done*
however loudly she said it.

She had gone off a cliff for that dog on the first day she was alive, more or less, and had
never in fifteen regions put a door between them.

"No," she said.

It came out flat and fast and not remotely earnest, and it was the coldest word she spoke
in the whole journey.

"Keep the dog," said Old Nick, after a moment. "Sentimental." He squared the three untouched
contracts and set them aside, and there was something in the set of his mouth that had not
been there before — not disappointment. Appetite. "I mean that as the compliment it isn't
quite."

Then he stood and rolled one shoulder, and the desk and the pen and the whole green-leather
ease of him did not vanish so much as *set aside*, like a coat hung up, revealing underneath
a thing that had run a city on charm alone for three hundred years and had not had to fight
for a day of it in nearly as long.

"Terms concluded. Now the other half of the evening. Do try to make me *work* for it — it's
been an age since anyone did, and a body forgets it's a pleasure."

*  *  *

It was the hardest fight of her life to that point, and she had refused every advantage she
was offered, and she never once looked as though she regretted it, which is not the same as
not regretting it.

He fought with the chains — the same fine gold links the whole city wore, except that in
his hands they came off the marble like whips and went out across the pit faster than a
person can decide anything. He put one round her ankle in the first ten seconds and pulled
her over the rail of the second tier, and she came down among a crowd of delighted,
applauding residents and did not break, because Overturn had been in the pocket over her
heart since the Gallowwood, and a drop is only a drop to somebody a hanged man taught to
fall like a leaf.

She got up. He was already there.

And here is what won it: she had spent an hour that evening learning to hold small backward
things up to a light, and she did it again in the middle of the fight. The gaslight down
there showed nothing, so she spent Fortune she had not bought and raised the Hermit's
Lantern instead — a thin honest white light in a room made of flattering gold — and in it
she could see the chains for what they were: not magic, not fate, only links, with a slack
in them, and a moment at the top of every throw when they were nothing at all.

After that it was half an hour of the worst work she had ever done, and twice she burned
petals off the Rose two at a time while the whole pit watched from its balconies and bore
her no ill will whatsoever.

"You took *nothing*," he said, late on, when they were both wrecked and the chains were
coming slower. "Do you know how long it's been since somebody took nothing? You walked into
the one shop in the world that gives fair value and paid full price in the only coin I
don't stock." He was laughing — the first ungoverned sound he had made all night. "*Pride.*
I could kiss you. I could also end you. Both, I think. In that order."

She hit him with the Bindle across the forearm on the outside of a throw, which is the one
place a chain cannot help a man, and then she hit him again, and that was the end of it.

*  *  *

Something went out of him that had nothing to do with the blow. He stopped, and looked down
at the chain in his own hand as though it were a thing handed to him by mistake, at a
party, by somebody he did not know.

"That's — hm. That's not a term I recall drafting. Losing." He turned the words over the
way you turn over a coin from a country you have never heard of. "I'd have priced it
differently if I'd known the shape of it."

He sat down on the marble. Slowly. Without a desk to sit behind for the first time in three
hundred years.

"Do you know, I don't believe I ever wrote myself a clause for this. Careless. I'm never
careless." He looked up at the whole warm gilded well of it going up into its lid. "What
have I been *doing* down here."

And the office cracked.

Not the desk — the *office*: the ease, the smoothness, the whole beautiful apparatus of the
contract-keeper coming apart along a seam, with a sound not like a mask breaking but like a
seal going on a document that has waited a very long time to be allowed to close.

Underneath it, surfacing the way a held breath finally lets go, was a name he had heard
shouted across that pit ten thousand times and never once said of himself.

"They all call me it," he said, barely. "Every day. *Old Nick. Old Nick Lowry.* Across the
floor, up the stair — I hear it more than any name's ever been heard by anybody." He
stopped. "And I never — it was the office's name. The desk's name. Never *mine* to say."

He tested it, the way a man picks up a piece of cutlery he has not held in so long that he
has forgotten which hand it belongs in.

"...Nick." A pause. "*I'm* Nick."

And then he laughed — small, astonished, without one grain of awful anywhere in it.

"Oh. Oh, that's — I'd forgotten there was a *me* under the terms of me. Three hundred years
keeping everybody else's word. Nobody ever offered to keep mine."

He got up off that marble a plainer man than the one who had sat down on it — smaller,
older about the eyes, the charm gone quiet and the awfulness gone entirely, only the funny
left, which had always been the part that was actually his. He reached into the air and
drew out a card, without any flourish at all.

"Trump the Fifteenth. Bargain, they'll call it, and they'll be right — it's the honest one,
it always was." He turned it once in his fingers. "And I know what you're doing, by the by.
You told me nothing; I read it off you at the desk inside the first minute. You're the one
spending down the whole world's chains, region by region, and there's a last one, and you
know exactly what it costs. I find I don't mind. It's the first deal in three centuries
where I could see the whole price up front, and it's *fair*. That's rarer than you'd think.
That's nearly holy."

He put it into her palms with both hands.

"Take it. Both hands — there. A thing given freely. I don't believe I've done one of those
since before the desk. Turns out it's the only sort worth the ink."

*  *  *

It went up through the pit like a change of pressure. On every tier, from every wrist and
ankle and belt-loop, the fine gold chains simply *opened*. Nothing snapped. It was a quiet
universal unlatching, as though every contract in the Undervault had reached its final
clause at the same instant and been permitted, at last, to end.

And then the air came down: down the gate stair from the western hills, cold and damp and
smelling of gorse and turned earth and rain three counties off — and every one of those
thousands of gas jets leaned. All at once. The whole pit guttered and swayed and steadied,
and the shadows of a city moved for the first time in three hundred years, and somewhere
below a woman screamed and then laughed at herself for screaming.

People looked down at their open chains. Some wept. Some sat down where they stood. And
then, in ones and twos and then in a slow dazed upward tide, they began to climb.

Pip watched them go with his head tilted and his tail going uncertainly, plainly weighing
whether this was a thing that ought to be herded.

"Look at them all *leave*," said Nick, at her shoulder, in a voice of pure bewilderment. "I
always told myself they stayed because the terms were good. And the terms *were* good,
that's the — the terms were always good." He watched them go. "So why are they all in such
a hurry up that stair. I'll be chewing on that one a while."

*  *  *

She climbed out with them; and partway up, against the whole current of it, one man was
standing still.
